Further empirical validation and practical tools are necessary to fully realize the impact of this work. Nonetheless, the potential influence of this paper justifies a score of 7.5. The paper definitely opens up the possibility to relate general concepts in dynamical systems to neural networks and generalization, but further work is needed to apply this result.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ModelingAgent: Bridging LLMs and Mathematical Modeling for Real-World Challenges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15068v1)
- **Authors**: Cheng Qian, Hongyi Du, Hongru Wang, Xiusi Chen, Yuji Zhang, Avirup Sil, Chengxiang Zhai, Kathleen McKeown, Heng Ji
- **Abstract**: Recent progress in large language models (LLMs) has enabled substantial advances in solving mathematical problems. However, existing benchmarks often fail to reflect the complexity of real-world problems, which demand open-ended, interdisciplinary reasoning and integration of computational tools. To address this gap, we introduce ModelingBench, a novel benchmark featuring real-world-inspired, open-ended problems from math modeling competitions across diverse domains, ranging from urban traffic optimization to ecosystem resource planning. These tasks require translating natural language into formal mathematical formulations, applying appropriate tools, and producing structured, defensible reports. ModelingBench also supports multiple valid solutions, capturing the ambiguity and creativity of practical modeling. We also present ModelingAgent, a multi-agent framework that coordinates tool use, supports structured workflows, and enables iterative self-refinement to generate well-grounded, creative solutions. To evaluate outputs, we further propose ModelingJudge, an expert-in-the-loop system leveraging LLMs as domain-specialized judges assessing solutions from multiple expert perspectives. Empirical results show that ModelingAgent substantially outperforms strong baselines and often produces solutions indistinguishable from those of human experts. Together, our work provides a comprehensive framework for evaluating and advancing real-world problem-solving in open-ended, interdisciplinary modeling challenges.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ModelingAgent: Bridging LLMs and Mathematical Modeling for Real-World Challenges":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ModelingBench, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of real-world mathematical modeling problems.  These problems, sourced from math modeling competitions, require open-ended, interdisciplinary reasoning, integration of computational tools, and structured report generation. The authors also present ModelingAgent, a multi-agent framework composed of specialized roles (Idea Proposer, Data Searcher, Modeling Implementor, Report Writer) coordinated by a Critic Module. They further propose ModelingJudge, an expert-in-the-loop system leveraging LLMs as domain-specialized judges for output evaluation. Empirical results demonstrate that ModelingAgent outperforms strong baselines, often producing solutions comparable to human experts. The code and resources are publicly released.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

This paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a significant gap in the LLM evaluation landscape: the ability to solve complex, real-world problems through mathematical modeling. Current LLM benchmarks often focus on abstract mathematical tasks, failing to capture the nuances and complexities inherent in real-world applications. ModelingBench directly tackles this limitation, providing a more realistic and demanding assessment environment.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Benchmark:** ModelingBench is a well-motivated and thoughtfully designed benchmark. Sourcing problems from established math modeling competitions ensures relevance and grounding in real-world scenarios. The inclusion of unrestricted tool access mirrors the flexibility available to human participants, enabling a more accurate assessment of LLMs' capabilities.
*   **Multi-Agent Framework:** ModelingAgent represents a creative approach to problem-solving, mimicking human team dynamics in a collaborative setting. The specialized agent roles and the Critic Module facilitate a structured workflow and iterative self-improvement.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The development of ModelingJudge, an LLM-based evaluation system, demonstrates a commitment to thorough and nuanced assessment. Simulating expert grading practices with diverse perspectives enhances the objectivity of the evaluation.
*   **Strong Results:** The empirical results convincingly demonstrate that ModelingAgent outperforms strong baselines. The fact that its implementations can sometimes fool human judges is a testament to its ability to generate human-like solutions.
*   **Public Resources:**  Releasing the code and benchmark data facilitates future research and development in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Innovativeness Gap:** While ModelingAgent shows significant improvement in groundedness, the authors acknowledge that innovativeness remains a challenge. This suggests that LLMs still struggle to generate truly original and creative solutions.  This is a crucial area for future research.
*   **Tool Dependency:** The strong performance of ModelingAgent is highly dependent on the effectiveness of the available tools. This may limit the generalizability of the results to environments with different toolsets.
*   **Subjectivity in Evaluation:**  Even with ModelingJudge, the evaluation of open-ended modeling tasks remains inherently subjective. The reliance on LLMs as judges could introduce biases. While human evaluation mitigates this to some extent, a more robust evaluation framework is needed.
*   **LRM Limitation:** The finding that LRMs do not significantly outperform LLMs highlights the universal difficulty of the task, but also raises questions about the limitations of current reasoning architectures when faced with complex real-world scenarios. What specific reasoning limitations hinder effective performance?

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it:

*   **Shifts the focus of LLM evaluation:** Moves beyond abstract mathematical tasks towards more practical, real-world applications.
*   **Provides a valuable resource for researchers:** ModelingBench offers a challenging and relevant benchmark for assessing LLMs' problem-solving abilities.
*   **Introduces a promising framework for LLM-based problem-solving:** ModelingAgent demonstrates the potential of multi-agent systems for tackling complex tasks.
*   **Highlights areas for future research:** Identifies limitations in LLMs' creativity, generalization, and reasoning capabilities.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a **Score: 8**.  The paper presents a novel benchmark, a creative problem-solving framework, and a comprehensive evaluation system.  The empirical results are compelling and the public release of resources is commendable. The identified weaknesses, particularly the innovativeness gap and tool dependency, highlight important directions for future research, but they do not detract significantly from the paper's overall contribution. It is a significant step forward in evaluating and advancing LLMs' real-world problem-solving intelligence.  While it's not a perfect 10, it provides a strong foundation for further research and has the potential to significantly influence the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can Large Language Models Understand Internet Buzzwords Through User-Generated Content
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15071v1)
- **Authors**: Chen Huang, Junkai Luo, Xinzuo Wang, Wenqiang Lei, Jiancheng Lv
- **Abstract**: The massive user-generated content (UGC) available in Chinese social media is giving rise to the possibility of studying internet buzzwords. In this paper, we study if large language models (LLMs) can generate accurate definitions for these buzzwords based on UGC as examples. Our work serves a threefold contribution. First, we introduce CHEER, the first dataset of Chinese internet buzzwords, each annotated with a definition and relevant UGC. Second, we propose a novel method, called RESS, to effectively steer the comprehending process of LLMs to produce more accurate buzzword definitions, mirroring the skills of human language learning. Third, with CHEER, we benchmark the strengths and weaknesses of various off-the-shelf definition generation methods and our RESS. Our benchmark demonstrates the effectiveness of RESS while revealing crucial shared challenges: over-reliance on prior exposure, underdeveloped inferential abilities, and difficulty identifying high-quality UGC to facilitate comprehension. We believe our work lays the groundwork for future advancements in LLM-based definition generation. Our dataset and code are available at https://github.com/SCUNLP/Buzzword.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty/significance score:

**Summary**

The paper introduces CHEER, the first Chinese internet buzzword dataset, complete with definitions and usage examples.  It addresses the challenge of large language models (LLMs) accurately defining internet buzzwords, which are dynamic and often absent from traditional dictionaries. The paper proposes a novel method called RESS (RESS), designed to improve LLMs' ability to generate these definitions by mimicking human language acquisition, breaking down comprehension into several key aspects. The authors benchmarked RESS against existing methods, finding that RESS improves performance but also highlighting limitations such as over-reliance on prior knowledge and underdeveloped inferential abilities.  The paper makes its dataset and code publicly available.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The CHEER dataset fills a critical gap by providing a structured resource for Chinese internet buzzwords, a domain previously lacking such curated data. The RESS method offers a novel approach to guiding LLM comprehension through a structured, human-inspired approach, addressing a recognized weakness in LLM's handling of dynamic, evolving language.
    *   **Methodology:** The paper presents a well-defined task, a clear methodology (RESS), and a comprehensive evaluation framework including automatic metrics and human evaluation. The contamination-free evaluation is particularly valuable in demonstrating the challenges LLMs face with truly novel terms.
    *   **Analysis:** The in-depth analysis of LLM performance reveals crucial insights into the limitations of current LLM approaches, such as over-reliance on prior exposure, limited inferential capabilities, and the challenges of identifying high-quality UGC. The exploration of various aspects of buzzword comprehension used in RESS is interesting and opens avenues for future research. The ablation studies provide further understanding of the contributions of each element of the RESS approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While RESS improves performance, the overall scores for semantic accuracy and completeness remain relatively low, suggesting that significant challenges persist.
    *   **Complexity:** The RESS method, while conceptually interesting, introduces additional complexity.  The choice and implementation of aspects is subjective and may require careful tuning.
    *   **Limited Scoping:** The paper focuses solely on Chinese buzzwords, which limits the generalizability of the findings to other languages. Also, the limitations sections points that they focused solely on textual information in this paper, but there are other modalities (image, audio) that may contribute to better performance.
    *  **Dataset size** While the dataset is a good starting point it is not exceptionally large, it is on the order of 1000 items and it is not immediately obvious how easy it would be to expand or create versions for other languages.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    *   The CHEER dataset provides a valuable resource for researchers working on LLMs, NLP, and computational linguistics, particularly in the context of Chinese language processing.
    *   The RESS method offers a promising direction for improving LLMs' ability to understand and define dynamic language, with potential applications in areas such as dictionary creation, chatbot development, and social media monitoring.
    *   The in-depth analysis provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of current LLM approaches, guiding future research directions towards more effective and robust language comprehension models.

*   **Novelty and Impact:**

    *  The proposed aspect-based technique has roots in past works of cognitive science that have used similar techniques in childhood development. However, the adaption and utilization for buzword definition provides an interesting and novel approach.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field with a novel dataset and an insightful analysis of LLM limitations. The RESS method is a promising approach, but it falls short of achieving high performance on its own. The results underscore the complexities of capturing the dynamic nature of online language. Given these considerations, a score of 7 reflects a good contribution with clear novelty and potential for future impact, but with some limitations in the scope of improvement.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DISCO Balances the Scales: Adaptive Domain- and Difficulty-Aware Reinforcement Learning on Imbalanced Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15074v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhang Zhou, Jing Zhu, Shengyi Qian, Zhuokai Zhao, Xiyao Wang, Xiaoyu Liu, Ming Li, Paiheng Xu, Wei Ai, Furong Huang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly aligned with human preferences through Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF). Among RLHF methods, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has gained attention for its simplicity and strong performance, notably eliminating the need for a learned value function. However, GRPO implicitly assumes a balanced domain distribution and uniform semantic alignment across groups - assumptions that rarely hold in real-world datasets. When applied to multi-domain, imbalanced data, GRPO disproportionately optimizes for dominant domains, neglecting underrepresented ones and resulting in poor generalization and fairness. We propose Domain-Informed Self-Consistency Policy Optimization (DISCO), a principled extension to GRPO that addresses inter-group imbalance with two key innovations. Domain-aware reward scaling counteracts frequency bias by reweighting optimization based on domain prevalence. Difficulty-aware reward scaling leverages prompt-level self-consistency to identify and prioritize uncertain prompts that offer greater learning value. Together, these strategies promote more equitable and effective policy learning across domains. Extensive experiments across multiple LLMs and skewed training distributions show that DISCO improves generalization, outperforms existing GRPO variants by 5% on Qwen3 models, and sets new state-of-the-art results on multi-domain alignment benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of domain imbalance in Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF), specifically when using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). GRPO, while efficient, is susceptible to bias towards dominant domains in multi-domain datasets, leading to poor generalization on underrepresented domains. The authors propose Domain-Informed Self-Consistency Policy Optimization (DISCO), an extension to GRPO that uses domain-aware and difficulty-aware reward scaling to counteract this imbalance. Domain-aware scaling reweights prompt groups based on their frequency, while difficulty-aware scaling prioritizes uncertain prompts based on a self-consistency measure.  Experiments across various LLMs and datasets demonstrate that DISCO improves generalization and outperforms existing GRPO variants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptively scaling rewards based on domain frequency and prompt difficulty within the GRPO framework has reasonable novelty, but not groundbreaking. While previous work has explored data imbalance in NLP and reward shaping in RL, the specific combination of these techniques within the context of GRPO for LLM alignment seems relatively original. The use of prompt-level self-consistency as a proxy for difficulty is a practical and useful insight. However, the core idea of re-weighting rewards depending on rarity is conceptually not new, the innovation is applying this to the RLHF setting in a multi-domain scenario.

*   **Significance:**  The paper tackles a practically relevant problem in RLHF: the skewed distribution of data and its impact on model fairness and generalization. If DISCO consistently delivers better performance, especially on underrepresented domains, it has the potential to improve the usability and robustness of RLHF-aligned LLMs. The empirical results, demonstrating improvements over existing GRPO variants, support this potential. The paper highlights a critical issue that can affect the real-world deployment of RLHF models and provides a practical solution that can be readily integrated into existing workflows.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of domain imbalance in GRPO and its consequences.
    *   **Well-Motivated Solution:** The proposed DISCO method is logically motivated by the identified problem. The two-pronged approach of domain and difficulty awareness is sensible.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are relatively comprehensive, covering multiple LLMs and datasets. Ablation studies demonstrate the importance of both domain-aware and difficulty-aware scaling.
    *   **Good Results:** The results show consistent improvements with DISCO over baselines, particularly for Qwen3 models.
    *   **Writing and Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements are positive, they are not necessarily transformative. The gains of around 5% on Qwen3 models, while significant, might not be large enough to drastically change current practices.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alternative Difficulty Metrics:** The paper uses self-consistency as the sole metric for prompt difficulty. While this is a reasonable choice, exploring other metrics (e.g., perplexity, variance in log probabilities) could potentially lead to even better results.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** The paper is primarily empirical, and lacks any theoretical analysis to explain the convergence properties or optimality guarantees of DISCO.
    *   **Rule-based Rewards:** The reliance on a rule-based reward signal is a weakness as this greatly simplifies the reward landscape. Real-world applications of RLHF usually involve complex learned rewards from human preferences, so there is a lack of connection to reward noise.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's potential influence is moderate. The findings are likely to be of interest to researchers and practitioners working on RLHF and LLM alignment. DISCO provides a practical solution to a real-world problem, and its relatively simple implementation could encourage its adoption. The work is also a good stepping stone towards a more generalized reward scheme when doing RLHF.
* **Further Considerations**: There could be some ethical ramifications stemming from the domain-aware reweighting of prompts that prioritize some domains over others. It may cause some fairness issues.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the reasonably novel approach, significant results, and good methodology, this paper deserves a solid score, but the incremental nature of the gains and the absence of theoretical guarantees hold it back from being exceptional. The application of self-consistency in RLHF is a key contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Traveling Across Languages: Benchmarking Cross-Lingual Consistency in Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15075v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Wang, Pinzhi Huang, Jihan Yang, Saining Xie, Daisuke Kawahara
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) has significantly enhanced their real-world applications. However, achieving consistent performance across languages, especially when integrating cultural knowledge, remains a significant challenge. To better assess this issue, we introduce two new benchmarks: KnowRecall and VisRecall, which evaluate cross-lingual consistency in MLLMs. KnowRecall is a visual question answering benchmark designed to measure factual knowledge consistency in 15 languages, focusing on cultural and historical questions about global landmarks. VisRecall assesses visual memory consistency by asking models to describe landmark appearances in 9 languages without access to images. Experimental results reveal that state-of-the-art MLLMs, including proprietary ones, still struggle to achieve cross-lingual consistency. This underscores the need for more robust approaches that produce truly multilingual and culturally aware models.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, a critical evaluation of novelty and significance, and a score with justification for the provided paper, "Traveling Across Languages: Benchmarking Cross-Lingual Consistency in Multimodal LLMs."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces two new benchmarks, KnowRecall and VisRecall, to evaluate the cross-lingual consistency of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). KnowRecall is a visual question answering (VQA) benchmark assessing factual knowledge consistency across 15 languages, focusing on cultural and historical information about global landmarks. VisRecall evaluates the consistency of visual memory by prompting models to describe landmarks in 9 languages without direct visual input. The paper presents experimental results showing that current state-of-the-art MLLMs, including proprietary ones, struggle with cross-lingual consistency, particularly when dealing with low-resource languages or languages dissimilar to English. The authors also find that inference-time reasoning and structured CoT prompts can improve consistency, and that multimodal training doesn't guarantee better performance on visual recall tasks, when compared to text-only LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution is the introduction of the KnowRecall and VisRecall benchmarks. Evaluating cross-lingual consistency in MLLMs, especially with a focus on cultural and historical knowledge, is a relatively unexplored area. While cross-lingual consistency has been examined in text-only LLMs, extending this analysis to multimodal models is a relevant and timely contribution. The design of VisRecall, which asks models to describe landmarks without visual input, is particularly novel and highlights potential shortcomings in how MLLMs leverage visual memory across languages. The novel approach of evaluating based on performance in English (EN), local language (LOC) and all languages (GLO) is a useful diagnostic tool.
*   **Significance:** Cross-lingual consistency is a crucial aspect of making MLLMs truly globally applicable. Inconsistencies across languages could lead to biased or unreliable outputs, hindering the deployment of these models in real-world scenarios. By highlighting these weaknesses, the paper motivates further research in creating more robust and culturally aware MLLMs. The benchmarks themselves can serve as valuable resources for future research and development in this area, facilitating systematic comparison of different models and training techniques. The analysis of inference-time reasoning and the comparison between MLLMs and text-only models offer insights into potential directions for improvement.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The benchmarks are well-motivated by the importance of cross-lingual consistency.
    *   The paper provides a clear explanation of the benchmark creation process and evaluation metrics.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, covering a range of open-weight and proprietary MLLMs.
    *   The analysis of the results is insightful, highlighting specific challenges and potential solutions.
    * The GLO setting provides an interesting and practically relevant perspective on cross-lingual performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation in VisRecall relies on CLIPScore, which while reference-free, may not perfectly capture the quality of visual descriptions, especially when generated in different languages. The authors themselves acknowledge this limitation.
    *   The number of languages used in VisRecall (9) is smaller compared to KnowRecall (15), potentially limiting the scope of the benchmark.
    *   The translation process for KnowRecall and VisRecall using Gemini-1.5-Pro could introduce translation errors, though the authors perform a study of translation accuracy.
    *   It is difficult to evaluate the claims about what constitutes truly multilingual models. More analysis of the model's architectural differences and training data could have helped bolster the claim that multimodal models aren't truly multilingual.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by raising awareness about the importance of cross-lingual consistency in MLLMs and by providing valuable benchmarks for future research. The results may also encourage researchers to explore alternative training techniques and architectures that better address the challenges of multilingual multimodal learning.
*   **Overall:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by introducing useful benchmarks and providing a detailed analysis of the cross-lingual consistency of MLLMs. While there are some limitations in the evaluation methods and scope, the paper is well-motivated, clearly presented, and offers valuable insights into the challenges and opportunities in this emerging area.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a good, but not groundbreaking, contribution. The benchmarks address a relevant problem. They offer important insights and a new analysis setting in the landscape of multimodal language models. While the methodology is sound and the experiments are thorough, they are based on current technologies and thus follow in a line of recent papers which may slightly limit the overall impact. Further iterations with even more advanced prompts, models, etc would be even more helpful to the community. While the topic is timely, it follows logically from the current trends in language models. The limitations, such as the reliance on CLIPScore and the translation process, prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Data Augmentation and Resolution Enhancement using GANs and Diffusion Models for Tree Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15077v1)
- **Authors**: Alessandro dos Santos Ferreira, Ana Paula Marques Ramos, José Marcato Junior, Wesley Nunes Gonçalves
- **Abstract**: Urban forests play a key role in enhancing environmental quality and supporting biodiversity in cities. Mapping and monitoring these green spaces are crucial for urban planning and conservation, yet accurately detecting trees is challenging due to complex landscapes and the variability in image resolution caused by different satellite sensors or UAV flight altitudes. While deep learning architectures have shown promise in addressing these challenges, their effectiveness remains strongly dependent on the availability of large and manually labeled datasets, which are often expensive and difficult to obtain in sufficient quantity. In this work, we propose a novel pipeline that integrates domain adaptation with GANs and Diffusion models to enhance the quality of low-resolution aerial images. Our proposed pipeline enhances low-resolution imagery while preserving semantic content, enabling effective tree segmentation without requiring large volumes of manually annotated data. Leveraging models such as pix2pix, Real-ESRGAN, Latent Diffusion, and Stable Diffusion, we generate realistic and structurally consistent synthetic samples that expand the training dataset and unify scale across domains. This approach not only improves the robustness of segmentation models across different acquisition conditions but also provides a scalable and replicable solution for remote sensing scenarios with scarce annotation resources. Experimental results demonstrated an improvement of over 50% in IoU for low-resolution images, highlighting the effectiveness of our method compared to traditional pipelines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel data augmentation pipeline to improve tree segmentation in aerial images, particularly when dealing with low-resolution data and scarce annotation resources.  It combines upsampling techniques using GANs (pix2pix, Real-ESRGAN) and Diffusion models (Latent Diffusion, Stable Diffusion) with domain adaptation strategies to enhance image quality and unify scales across different datasets with varying ground sample distances (GSD).  The proposed approach aims to generate realistic, annotated synthetic data to augment training datasets and improve the robustness of segmentation models (specifically SegFormer) without requiring extensive manual labeling. The results show significant IoU improvements (over 50% in some cases for low-resolution images) compared to traditional supervised training pipelines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of several established techniques (GANs, Diffusion models, Domain Adaptation) into a unified pipeline specifically tailored for tree segmentation in remote sensing with the additional constraint of data scarcity. The idea of using super-resolution and image translation *specifically* to address GSD discrepancies and annotation limitations is a reasonably novel contribution. While the individual components are not new, the orchestration of these components is a key aspect.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very practical problem in remote sensing: the difficulty of obtaining large, accurately labeled datasets for training deep learning models, especially in regions where high-resolution imagery is expensive or unavailable. The proposed method offers a potential solution by generating synthetic data to improve the performance of segmentation models. The reported improvement in IoU is substantial, particularly for low-resolution images, which could have a significant impact on real-world applications where high-resolution data is not feasible.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of tree segmentation in remote sensing, particularly the limitations imposed by data scarcity and varying image resolutions.
    *   **Well-structured approach:** The proposed pipeline is well-defined and explained, with clear descriptions of each component and how they contribute to the overall goal.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation, comparing the performance of the proposed method with baseline approaches and using multiple evaluation metrics. It tests several combinations of GANs and Diffusion models.
    *   **Practical implications:** The method is potentially scalable and replicable, offering a cost-effective solution for remote sensing applications where annotation resources are limited.
    *   Addressing the resolution discrepancy problem is significant.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental improvements:** The paper largely combines existing techniques. The novelty is primarily in the application and integration rather than a groundbreaking new algorithm or theoretical insight.
    *   **Limited comparison:** The paper primarily compares against baseline supervised learning with the original datasets.  A more direct comparison against other data augmentation or domain adaptation techniques commonly used in remote sensing would strengthen the paper. This could include established remote sensing specific data augmentation techniques.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the method shows promise for tree segmentation, the generalizability to other remote sensing applications and different types of imagery (e.g., multispectral, hyperspectral) is not fully explored. The performance might vary significantly depending on the specific characteristics of the target application.  Is the prompting specific to the tree segmentation task.
    *   **Dependency on existing models:** The performance of the pipeline relies heavily on the quality of the pre-trained GANs and Diffusion models.
    *   **Discussion of Stable Diffusion Prompt selection is weak:** There is not much exploration into prompt engineering and how prompts impact results.
*   **Potential Influence:** If the methods prove robust and generalizable, the paper could influence research directions by encouraging the use of GANs and Diffusion models as a crucial step to address data limitations and heterogeneity in remote sensing. It could be a valuable contribution to research in low-resource environments.
*   **Clarity:** Well written and easy to read

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the above points, I assign a score of 6. The paper presents a reasonable solution to a practical problem by integrating existing techniques in a novel way. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method, especially for low-resolution images. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the lack of comparison against other existing techniques, and the dependency on the performance of the pre-trained GANs and Diffusion models slightly limit the overall impact and significance. Additional work is needed to demonstrate the generalizability of the method to other remote sensing applications and to explore further improvements to the image translation and super-resolution components.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### SUS backprop: linear backpropagation algorithm for long inputs in transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15080v1)
- **Authors**: Sergey Pankov, Georges Harik
- **Abstract**: It is straightforward to design an unbiased gradient estimator that stochastically cuts the backpropagation flow through any part of a computational graph. By cutting the parts that have little effect on the computation, one can potentially save a significant amount of back-propagation computation in exchange for a minimal increase in the stochastic gradient variance, in some situations. Such a situation occurs in the attention mechanism of the transformer architecture. For long sequences, attention becomes the limiting factor, as its compute requirements increase quadratically with sequence length $n$. At the same time, most attention weights become very small, as most attention heads tend to connect a given token with only a small fraction of other tokens in the sequence. These weights become promising targets for cutting backpropagation. We propose a simple probabilistic rule controlled by a single parameter $c$ that cuts backpropagation through most attention weights, leaving at most $c$ interactions per token per attention head. This brings a factor of $c/n$ reduction in the compute required for the attention backpropagation, turning it from quadratic $O(n^2)$ to linear complexity $O(nc)$. We have empirically verified that, for a typical transformer model, cutting $99\%$ of the attention gradient flow (i.e. choosing $c \sim 20-30$) results in relative gradient variance increase of only about $1\%$ for $n \sim 2000$, and it decreases with $n$. This approach is amenable to efficient sparse matrix implementation, thus being promising for making the cost of a backward pass negligible relative to the cost of a forward pass when training a transformer model on long sequences.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "SUS backprop" (Sparse Unbiased Stochastic backpropagation), a novel algorithm designed to reduce the computational cost of backpropagation in Transformers, particularly for long sequences. SUS backprop stochastically cuts the backpropagation flow through the attention mechanism by sparsifying attention weights, leaving at most *c* interactions per token per attention head. This transforms the quadratic complexity O(n²) of attention backpropagation to linear complexity O(nc), where *n* is the sequence length. The paper demonstrates empirically that this sparsification can be achieved with minimal increase in gradient variance, suggesting significant compute savings during training, especially for long sequences. The authors perform experiments on various transformer models and analyze the attention spread, comparing the empirical results with predictions from a simplified toy model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of stochastically sparsifying backpropagation in the attention mechanism is quite novel. Prior works have explored sparsification techniques, but the specific application to attention weights with unbiased gradient estimation is less common. The use of a single parameter *c* to control the level of sparsity makes the algorithm simple and easy to implement. Prior work on sparsity in attention like Reformer, Longformer, and Big Bird, focus on sparsifying the *forward* pass. SUS backprop sparsifies the *backward* pass while keeping the forward pass untouched and thus it is complementary to the other methods.

*   **Significance:** Reducing the computational burden of Transformer training is a crucial problem in the field of deep learning. If SUS backprop can deliver significant speedups without substantially affecting model performance, it would be very impactful. The paper's empirical results showing low gradient variance increase with substantial sparsification are encouraging.
However, the results shown are only on a single forward pass. More convincing would be an actual full training run of SUS backprop.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly outlines the algorithm and its theoretical underpinnings.
    *   The empirical evaluation provides initial evidence supporting the algorithm's effectiveness.
    *   The analysis of attention spread across different models provides valuable insights.
    *   The toy model provides a theoretical framework for understanding the sparsity-variance tradeoff.
    *   The claim of unbiased gradient estimation is strong, since training with unbiased estimators generally leads to better convergence properties.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are somewhat limited. The gradient variance is measured on a pre-trained model.
    *   There isn't a full training example with SUS backprop. Showing the speedup on a training run would be more convincing. This is not addressed.
    *   The evaluation focuses on the *OPT* models, and while Mistral experiments are included, there is much less analysis for the latter.
    *   The paper acknowledges the need for a more efficient implementation, but doesn't provide concrete solutions beyond custom kernels.
    *   The paper doesn't address hyperparameter sensitivity of the proposed method.
    *   The significance section claims that this method could allow the backward pass to be negligible compared to the forward pass. However the compute cost of the backward pass in other parts of the network (besides attention) is not addressed.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the implementation issues can be addressed and the algorithm consistently demonstrates significant speedups in real-world training scenarios, it could be widely adopted. It has the potential to make training very large Transformer models more feasible.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a novel and potentially significant algorithm for reducing the computational cost of Transformer training. The theoretical justification and initial empirical results are promising. However, the lack of complete end-to-end training experiments is a significant limitation. Therefore, the score reflects the potential of the work while acknowledging its current limitations.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Leveraging Large Language Models for Command Injection Vulnerability Analysis in Python: An Empirical Study on Popular Open-Source Projects
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15088v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxuan Wang, Jingshu Chen, Qingyang Wang
- **Abstract**: Command injection vulnerabilities are a significant security threat in dynamic languages like Python, particularly in widely used open-source projects where security issues can have extensive impact. With the proven effectiveness of Large Language Models(LLMs) in code-related tasks, such as testing, researchers have explored their potential for vulnerabilities analysis. This study evaluates the potential of large language models (LLMs), such as GPT-4, as an alternative approach for automated testing for vulnerability detection. In particular, LLMs have demonstrated advanced contextual understanding and adaptability, making them promising candidates for identifying nuanced security vulnerabilities within code. To evaluate this potential, we applied LLM-based analysis to six high-profile GitHub projects-Django, Flask, TensorFlow, Scikit-learn, PyTorch, and Langchain-each with over 50,000 stars and extensive adoption across software development and academic research. Our analysis assesses both the strengths and limitations of LLMs in detecting command injection vulnerabilities, evaluating factors such as detection accuracy, efficiency, and practical integration into development workflows. In addition, we provide a comparative analysis of different LLM tools to identify those most suitable for security applications. Our findings offer guidance for developers and security researchers on leveraging LLMs as innovative and automated approaches to enhance software security.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty and significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Leveraging Large Language Models for Command Injection Vulnerability Analysis in Python: An Empirical Study on Popular Open-Source Projects" investigates the effectiveness of using Large Language Models (LLMs) such as GPT-4, GPT-40, Claude 3.5 Sonnet and DeepSeek-R1, for automated command injection vulnerability detection in Python code. The authors apply LLM-based analysis to six popular open-source Python projects (Django, Flask, TensorFlow, Scikit-learn, PyTorch, and Langchain) and compare the results with those obtained using a traditional static analysis tool, Bandit. The study assesses the strengths and limitations of LLMs in this context, evaluating detection accuracy, efficiency, and practical integration into development workflows. The paper also analyzes types of vulnerabilities that LLMs might miss and provides a comparative analysis of different LLM tools to identify the most suitable ones for security applications, and their associated costs (time and finance). The work releases a dataset of Python files to facilitate further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

*   **Relevant Problem:** Command injection vulnerabilities are a significant and persistent security threat in dynamic languages like Python. The paper tackles a practical and important problem.
*   **Methodological Rigor:** The study involves a reasonably large dataset of real-world open-source projects, providing a practical foundation for the analysis. The comparison with Bandit offers a baseline for evaluating the improvements achieved by LLMs. The performance analysis, including metrics like accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score, offers a sound assessment of the LLM performance.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper explores different LLMs, assessing their accuracy, consistency, and efficiency. Additionally, it assesses the costs associated with using these models and analyzes the types of vulnerabilities that the LLMs might miss.
*   **Dataset Availability:** Making the dataset publicly available allows other researchers to build upon the work and validate the findings.
*   **Clarity and Structure:** The paper is well-structured and clearly presents the methodology, results, and findings.
*   **Prompt Engineering Considerations:**  The authors mention using "mimic-in-the-background" prompting method which highlights a recognition of prompt engineering as an important aspect of LLM evaluations.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope of Vulnerabilities:** While command injection is a critical vulnerability, the study focuses solely on it, neglecting other potential security weaknesses. A broader investigation of different vulnerability types could provide a more comprehensive understanding of LLMs' capabilities.
*   **Lack of Generalizability:** All the projects studied were popular open-source projects. While the analysis might generalize to similar project types, the performance of LLMs may vary significantly for projects in different domains or with different code styles.
*   **Dependence on Prompt Engineering:** The study's results heavily depend on the prompts used to interact with the LLMs. The paper describes the prompt engineering strategy, but a more detailed analysis of the impact of different prompt designs on the results could be valuable.
*   **Limited Analysis of Bandit:** While the study compares LLMs with Bandit, the evaluation is somewhat limited. A deeper dive into why Bandit fails in certain cases, possibly linked to its static analysis approach, would strengthen the comparative analysis.
*   **Modifications for Test Execution:** The need to manually modify test scripts generated by the LLM to ensure compatibility is a potential drawback. It highlights limitations in the LLM's ability to generate fully executable and environment-aware tests. It would be worthwhile to understand how the required modifications vary across the LLMs.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper makes a valuable contribution by exploring the potential of LLMs for command injection vulnerability detection. While the application of LLMs to code analysis and security testing is not entirely new, this paper provides a solid empirical evaluation in the context of Python open-source projects.

The significance lies in:

*   **Demonstrating LLMs' Potential:** The study showcases that LLMs can be a useful tool for security assessment and could identify vulnerabilities missed by traditional tools.
*   **Identifying Limitations:** The analysis of the types of vulnerabilities that LLMs fail to detect provides valuable insights for improving LLM-based security testing methods.
*   **Providing a Benchmark:** The released dataset serves as a valuable resource for future research in this area.
*   **Comparing Different LLMs**: The study includes multiple LLMs instead of limiting it to one, adding to its rigor.

However, the paper's novelty is somewhat limited by existing research in the broader area of LLMs for code analysis. The application to a specific vulnerability type (command injection) and language (Python) is a valuable contribution, but it doesn't represent a groundbreaking departure from previous work.

**Score:** 7

*Justification:*

The paper presents a solid empirical study demonstrating the potential and limitations of using LLMs for command injection vulnerability detection in Python. It has clear strengths in its methodology, comparative analysis, and the release of a benchmark dataset. The inclusion of multiple LLMs and recognizing prompt engineering is commendable. However, its novelty is somewhat limited, and a deeper investigation into aspects like prompt engineering, the limitations of Bandit, a wider class of vulnerabilities, and further generalizability would significantly increase its impact. Despite these drawbacks, the paper is a valuable contribution to the field and provides a good foundation for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### DeFTX: Denoised Sparse Fine-Tuning for Zero-Shot Cross-Lingual Transfer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15090v1)
- **Authors**: Sona Elza Simon, Preethi Jyothi
- **Abstract**: Effective cross-lingual transfer remains a critical challenge in scaling the benefits of large language models from high-resource to low-resource languages. Towards this goal, prior studies have explored many approaches to combine task knowledge from task-specific data in a (high-resource) source language and language knowledge from unlabeled text in a (low-resource) target language. One notable approach proposed composable sparse fine-tuning (SFT) for cross-lingual transfer that learns task-specific and language-specific sparse masks to select a subset of the pretrained model's parameters that are further fine-tuned. These sparse fine-tuned vectors (SFTs) are subsequently composed with the pretrained model to facilitate zero-shot cross-lingual transfer to a task in a target language, using only task-specific data from a source language. These sparse masks for SFTs were identified using a simple magnitude-based pruning. In our work, we introduce DeFT-X, a novel composable SFT approach that denoises the weight matrices of a pretrained model before magnitude pruning using singular value decomposition, thus yielding more robust SFTs. We evaluate DeFT-X on a diverse set of extremely low-resource languages for sentiment classification (NusaX) and natural language inference (AmericasNLI) and demonstrate that it performs at par or outperforms SFT and other prominent cross-lingual transfer baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DEFT-X, a novel approach to zero-shot cross-lingual transfer learning. DEFT-X builds upon the Lottery Ticket Sparse Fine-Tuning (LT-SFT) framework by incorporating a denoising step using Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) *before* identifying sparse subnetworks for fine-tuning. The core idea is to prune higher-order components (associated with lower singular values) in the pretrained model's weight matrices, which are assumed to contain noise, thereby leading to more robust and effective sparse fine-tuned vectors for cross-lingual transfer. The resulting sparse vectors are then composed for zero-shot transfer. The authors evaluate DEFT-X on sentiment analysis (NusaX) and natural language inference (AmericasNLI) tasks for low-resource Indonesian/indigenous languages, demonstrating performance improvements over LT-SFT and MAD-X baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *integration* of SVD-based denoising within the LT-SFT framework. The use of SVD for low-rank approximation and denoising in neural networks is not entirely new (as the authors acknowledge), but its application to *improve sparse fine-tuning for cross-lingual transfer* is a worthwhile contribution. The results suggest the denoising step is effective.
* **Significance:**  Effective cross-lingual transfer, especially in zero-shot settings and for extremely low-resource languages, is a crucial challenge in NLP.  DEFT-X addresses this challenge by improving the quality of sparse fine-tuned vectors, which directly impacts model size, training efficiency, and transfer performance. The gains over existing baselines, while not dramatically large, are consistent and highlight the benefits of their approach, especially when you consider the limitations imposed by the extreme low-resource nature of the target languages in the experiments.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of effective cross-lingual transfer for low-resource languages and motivates the need for improved sparse fine-tuning techniques.
    * **Well-Defined Approach:** The DEFT-X algorithm is clearly explained, with a good balance of mathematical rigor and intuitive explanation.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experimental setup is comprehensive and uses established benchmarks (NusaX, AmericasNLI). The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of individual components of DEFT-X (denoising, sparsity, and re-training), strengthening the findings.
    * **Comparison to Baselines:**  The comparisons to strong baselines (LT-SFT, MAD-X) provide context and demonstrate the advantages of DEFT-X.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** The gains over LT-SFT are present but not earth-shattering.  This raises the question of the *practical* significance in scenarios where computational resources aren't heavily constrained (a situation less frequently seen these days).  A more thorough analysis of the computational overhead of SVD compared to the training improvements would strengthen the analysis.
    * **Parameter Sensitivity:** The method requires tuning the rank parameter (`r`) for SVD, which is model- and task-specific. While they explore uniform rank selection, the manual tuning aspect makes it less plug-and-play. The optimal rank values are dependent on the dataset, architecture, and task and, hence, reduces the method’s ease of use and general applicability.
    * **Limited Scope of Experiments:** The evaluation is restricted to encoder-only transformer models and classification tasks. Extending the evaluation to decoder models and other NLP tasks would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The paper lacks a strong theoretical justification for *why* denoising in this manner *should* lead to better sparse subnetworks for transfer. While the results provide empirical support, a more in-depth theoretical analysis could further elevate the work.
    * **XLM-RLarge Results:** The decrease in performance when moving from XLM-RBase to XLM-RLarge, while acknowledged, needs a more in-depth investigation and discussion. If the method doesn't scale to larger pre-trained models without modification, that's a significant limitation.

* **Impact and Influence:** DEFT-X provides a useful technique for improving sparse fine-tuning for cross-lingual transfer, which can potentially contribute to more efficient and effective adaptation of large language models for low-resource languages. It offers a useful perspective for future work on PEFT and cross-lingual transfer. However, the incremental nature of the gains and the parameter tuning requirements may limit its immediate widespread adoption. The work may also spark interest in adapting task arithmetic for cross-lingual tasks.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel *combination* of existing techniques (SVD and sparse fine-tuning) for a relevant problem (cross-lingual transfer). The empirical results support the effectiveness of the approach, demonstrating consistent improvements over strong baselines. However, the gains are incremental, the parameter tuning aspect is a limitation, the scope of evaluation is somewhat narrow, and the theoretical justification is weak. Therefore, while the paper makes a useful contribution, its novelty and significance are not at a level to warrant a higher score. The fact that denoising large models is a frequent topic and is used heavily in image generation also diminishes the novelty of the technique.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ThinkRec: Thinking-based recommendation via LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15091v1)
- **Authors**: Qihang Yu, Kairui Fu, Shengyu Zhang, Zheqi Lv, Fan Wu, Fei Wu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled more semantic-aware recommendations through natural language generation. Existing LLM for recommendation (LLM4Rec) methods mostly operate in a System 1-like manner, relying on superficial features to match similar items based on click history, rather than reasoning through deeper behavioral logic. This often leads to superficial and erroneous recommendations. Motivated by this, we propose ThinkRec, a thinking-based framework that shifts LLM4Rec from System 1 to System 2 (rational system). Technically, ThinkRec introduces a thinking activation mechanism that augments item metadata with keyword summarization and injects synthetic reasoning traces, guiding the model to form interpretable reasoning chains that consist of analyzing interaction histories, identifying user preferences, and making decisions based on target items. On top of this, we propose an instance-wise expert fusion mechanism to reduce the reasoning difficulty. By dynamically assigning weights to expert models based on users' latent features, ThinkRec adapts its reasoning path to individual users, thereby enhancing precision and personalization. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that ThinkRec significantly improves the accuracy and interpretability of recommendations. Our implementations are available in anonymous Github: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ThinkRec_LLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ThinkRec, a framework designed to improve recommendation systems by leveraging large language models (LLMs) in a more deliberate, "System 2" (rational) manner rather than relying on superficial pattern matching ("System 1"). ThinkRec achieves this through two key mechanisms: 1) a "thinking activation" process that augments item metadata with keyword summaries and injects synthetic reasoning traces during training to guide the model towards forming interpretable reasoning chains; and 2) an instance-wise expert fusion mechanism that adapts the reasoning path to individual users by dynamically weighting expert models based on latent user features. The authors evaluate ThinkRec on several real-world datasets and demonstrate significant improvements in both recommendation accuracy and the interpretability of the generated explanations compared to existing LLM-based and traditional recommendation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central idea of shifting LLM-based recommendation from a "System 1" to a "System 2" paradigm is conceptually novel. While previous works have explored reasoning and instruction tuning in LLM-based recommendation, ThinkRec's explicit focus on activating and guiding interpretable reasoning chains, combined with personalized expert fusion, is a distinct contribution. Augmenting items with keywords is not particularly innovative in itself, but its integration within the broader thinking activation framework enhances its impact. Creating synthetic reasoning traces is a notable technique that could be adopted in related fields.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of existing LLM-based recommendation systems: their tendency to rely on superficial similarity matching rather than deeper behavioral understanding. By improving both accuracy and interpretability, ThinkRec represents a step towards building more trustworthy and effective recommendation systems. The gains demonstrated over strong baselines on benchmark datasets further highlight its potential impact. The approach of utilizing LLMs in a way that leverages their reasoning capabilities has broad implications for using LLMs in data science tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivates the proposed solution.
    *   The ThinkRec framework is well-defined and technically sound.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, with comparisons to several strong baselines on diverse datasets.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of ThinkRec.
    *   The case study effectively illustrates the advantages of ThinkRec in terms of interpretability and reasoning quality.
    *   Reasoning Data Synthesis approach ensures the trustworthiness and alignment of recommendations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a separate LLM for generating synthetic reasoning traces raises concerns about potential biases or inaccuracies in the generated data. While this method is convenient, it relies heavily on the accuracy and trustworthiness of another model, rather than an objective ground truth.
    *   The expert fusion mechanism, while effective, relies on user clustering based on latent features from traditional models. The choice of clustering algorithm and the sensitivity to the clustering parameters are not thoroughly explored.
    *   While improvements in interpretability (measured by METEOR and BLEURT) are shown, a more direct evaluation of human understanding and trust in the recommendations would further strengthen the claims. Real user engagement studies would also add significant value.
    *   The complexity of the framework may limit its adoption and widespread application. There is a high complexity of using and implementing the framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** ThinkRec has the potential to influence future research in LLM-based recommendation by highlighting the importance of explicit reasoning and personalized modeling. The thinking activation and expert fusion mechanisms could be adopted and adapted in other recommendation systems and related tasks. The work also encourages further exploration of methods for generating high-quality reasoning data and evaluating the interpretability of LLM-based systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to improving LLM-based recommendation by explicitly focusing on activating and guiding reasoning. The demonstrated improvements in both accuracy and interpretability are significant. While the reliance on synthetic reasoning data and the complexity of the framework represent limitations, the potential influence of ThinkRec on future research in this area is substantial. The paper offers valuable insights and practical techniques that can be adopted and extended by other researchers and practitioners.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Steering Generative Models with Experimental Data for Protein Fitness Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15093v1)
- **Authors**: Jason Yang, Wenda Chu, Daniel Khalil, Raul Astudillo, Bruce J. Wittmann, Frances H. Arnold, Yisong Yue
- **Abstract**: Protein fitness optimization involves finding a protein sequence that maximizes desired quantitative properties in a combinatorially large design space of possible sequences. Recent developments in steering protein generative models (e.g diffusion models, language models) offer a promising approach. However, by and large, past studies have optimized surrogate rewards and/or utilized large amounts of labeled data for steering, making it unclear how well existing methods perform and compare to each other in real-world optimization campaigns where fitness is measured by low-throughput wet-lab assays. In this study, we explore fitness optimization using small amounts (hundreds) of labeled sequence-fitness pairs and comprehensively evaluate strategies such as classifier guidance and posterior sampling for guiding generation from different discrete diffusion models of protein sequences. We also demonstrate how guidance can be integrated into adaptive sequence selection akin to Thompson sampling in Bayesian optimization, showing that plug-and-play guidance strategies offer advantages compared to alternatives such as reinforcement learning with protein language models.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Steering Generative Models with Experimental Data for Protein Fitness Optimization."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of optimizing protein fitness, a key task in protein engineering, by steering generative models (specifically, discrete diffusion models) using limited experimental data. The authors systematically compare various steering strategies, including classifier guidance and posterior sampling, on two protein fitness datasets (TrpB and CreiLOV) using small amounts of labeled sequence-fitness pairs. They also explore integrating guidance with adaptive sequence selection, akin to Thompson sampling in Bayesian optimization. The results suggest that plug-and-play guidance strategies (particularly with discrete diffusion models) are more effective than alternatives like reinforcement learning with protein language models, especially in data-scarce regimes. The paper emphasizes the steerability and low computational cost of plug-and-play guidance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several important contributions, though some aspects build upon prior work.
    *   A systematic evaluation of different steering strategies (classifier guidance, posterior sampling, and DPO) across diverse generative models (continuous and discrete diffusion models, language models) is valuable. While individual steering methods and generative models have been explored before, the comprehensive comparison under limited experimental data is novel.
    *   The integration of uncertainty-aware exploration (Thompson sampling) into SGPO for diffusion models is a noteworthy extension, though the underlying idea of using uncertainty for exploration is well-established in Bayesian optimization. Applying Thompson Sampling to a plug-and-play steering framework seems to be a key element of originality.
    *   Demonstrating the effectiveness of decoupled annealing posterior sampling (DAPS) in this context is also a specific contribution.
    *   The focus on real-world, wet-lab data is a strong point, as much of the existing work relies on surrogate rewards.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper's findings are significant for protein engineering because they provide practical guidance on how to effectively leverage generative models with limited experimental data. This is a common scenario in wet-lab experiments, where data acquisition is expensive and time-consuming.
    *   The comparison between plug-and-play guidance (with diffusion models) and finetuning-based approaches (with language models) is particularly relevant. The result that plug-and-play methods outperform finetuning in data-scarce regimes has important implications for the development of more efficient protein optimization workflows.
    *   The inclusion of adaptive optimization strategies, such as Thompson sampling, addresses a critical need for balancing exploration and exploitation in protein design.
    *   The public availability of user-friendly code will facilitate adoption of the proposed methods by other researchers and practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation: The paper rigorously compares several methods across two distinct protein datasets.
    *   Focus on realistic scenarios: The emphasis on data-scarce regimes and real-world experimental data makes the findings highly relevant to practitioners.
    *   Clear and well-written: The paper is well-structured and presents the results in a clear and understandable manner.
    *   The use of multiple diffusion models adds some robustness to the findings.
    *   The experiments involve different kinds of molecules, increasing confidence in the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a supervised oracle as a proxy for protein fitness is a limitation. While the authors acknowledge this, it is important to recognize that the oracle may not perfectly capture the true protein fitness landscape. This could lead to biased results. The oracle approach is well-justified for a controlled environment, but there is no replacement for actual experimentation.
    *   The performance of DPO is shown to be quite sensitive to the choice of hyperparameter, limiting the generality of the results.
    *   Some of the methods may be highly specific to TrpB, weakening the generalizability of the claims.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper will likely influence the development of more efficient protein optimization workflows.
    *   The results provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different generative modeling and steering strategies in data-scarce regimes.
    *   The open-source code will facilitate the widespread adoption of the proposed methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of protein engineering by providing a systematic comparison of steering strategies for generative models under realistic data constraints. The integration of adaptive optimization techniques further enhances the practical utility of the proposed methods. While the reliance on supervised oracles is a limitation, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses. It seems to make a useful contribution to the subfield by providing a recipe for training and fine-tuning protein models in a constrained context.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: q-bio.BM
- **Score**: 7/10

### SciCUEval: A Comprehensive Dataset for Evaluating Scientific Context Understanding in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15094v1)
- **Authors**: Jing Yu, Yuqi Tang, Kehua Feng, Mingyang Rao, Lei Liang, Zhiqiang Zhang, Mengshu Sun, Wen Zhang, Qiang Zhang, Keyan Ding, Huajun Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown impressive capabilities in contextual understanding and reasoning. However, evaluating their performance across diverse scientific domains remains underexplored, as existing benchmarks primarily focus on general domains and fail to capture the intricate complexity of scientific data. To bridge this gap, we construct SciCUEval, a comprehensive benchmark dataset tailored to assess the scientific context understanding capability of LLMs. It comprises ten domain-specific sub-datasets spanning biology, chemistry, physics, biomedicine, and materials science, integrating diverse data modalities including structured tables, knowledge graphs, and unstructured texts. SciCUEval systematically evaluates four core competencies: Relevant information identification, Information-absence detection, Multi-source information integration, and Context-aware inference, through a variety of question formats. We conduct extensive evaluations of state-of-the-art LLMs on SciCUEval, providing a fine-grained analysis of their strengths and limitations in scientific context understanding, and offering valuable insights for the future development of scientific-domain LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "SciCUEval: A Comprehensive Dataset for Evaluating Scientific Context Understanding in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SciCUEval, a novel benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the scientific context understanding capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). SciCUEval distinguishes itself from existing benchmarks by focusing on diverse scientific domains (biology, chemistry, physics, biomedicine, and materials science) and integrating multiple data modalities, including structured tables, knowledge graphs, and unstructured text. The benchmark assesses four core competencies: relevant information identification, information-absence detection, multi-source information integration, and context-aware inference.  The authors conduct extensive evaluations of various state-of-the-art LLMs on SciCUEval, providing a detailed analysis of their strengths and limitations in scientific context understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a crucial gap in the evaluation of LLMs, as existing benchmarks often fail to adequately assess their performance in complex, scientific contexts. The strengths of the paper include:

*   **Novelty:**  SciCUEval's comprehensive nature, encompassing multiple scientific disciplines and data modalities, makes it a significant contribution to the field. The explicit focus on context understanding, rather than just question answering, is a notable advance.
*   **Significance:** The benchmark is likely to be highly valuable to researchers developing and refining LLMs for scientific applications. The fine-grained analysis of LLM performance across different competencies provides actionable insights for improvement. The findings, especially regarding the need to address overconfidence and enhance structured data comprehension, are significant.
*   **Thoroughness:** The experimental evaluation is extensive, covering a range of LLMs and providing detailed performance breakdowns. The dataset creation process, including the use of LLMs for question generation and expert validation for quality control, is rigorous.
*   **Reproducibility:** The paper provides ample information regarding the source data, dataset generation, and evaluation methodology, enhancing reproducibility.

However, the paper also has some limitations:

*   **Focus on Textual Data:** While the benchmark includes tables and KGs, a stronger integration of non-textual modalities like images and 3D molecular structures, which are prevalent in scientific domains, would make it even more comprehensive.
*   **Potential for Dataset Bias:** The automated question generation process, even with expert validation, could introduce biases reflecting the LLM's inherent limitations. Further analysis to identify and mitigate such biases might be needed.
*   **Generalizability Beyond The Specific Disciplines:** While the dataset spans 5 major disciplines, the vastness of scientific knowledge means that generalizability to all rapidly evolving scientific fields can still be debated.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

SciCUEval is a substantial and novel contribution to the field. It provides a comprehensive and valuable benchmark for evaluating LLMs' scientific context understanding abilities. The focus on diverse data modalities and core competencies makes it particularly relevant for advancing LLMs in scientific applications. While there's room for improvement regarding the inclusion of non-textual data and mitigation of potential biases, the overall rigor and significance of the work warrant a high score. Its design offers significant insights into the strengths and limitations of current LLMs and paves the way for more sophisticated scientific domain LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Cost-aware LLM-based Online Dataset Annotation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15101v1)
- **Authors**: Eray Can Elumar, Cem Tekin, Osman Yagan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled automated dataset labeling with minimal human supervision. While majority voting across multiple LLMs can improve label reliability by mitigating individual model biases, it incurs high computational costs due to repeated querying. In this work, we propose a novel online framework, Cost-aware Majority Voting (CaMVo), for efficient and accurate LLM-based dataset annotation. CaMVo adaptively selects a subset of LLMs for each data instance based on contextual embeddings, balancing confidence and cost without requiring pre-training or ground-truth labels. Leveraging a LinUCB-based selection mechanism and a Bayesian estimator over confidence scores, CaMVo estimates a lower bound on labeling accuracy for each LLM and aggregates responses through weighted majority voting. Our empirical evaluation on the MMLU and IMDB Movie Review datasets demonstrates that CaMVo achieves comparable or superior accuracy to full majority voting while significantly reducing labeling costs. This establishes CaMVo as a practical and robust solution for cost-efficient annotation in dynamic labeling environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cost-aware Majority Voting (CaMVo), a novel online framework for efficiently annotating datasets using large language models (LLMs).  CaMVo adaptively selects a subset of LLMs for each data instance based on contextual embeddings, balancing confidence and cost without requiring pre-training or ground-truth labels.  It employs a LinUCB-based selection mechanism and a Bayesian estimator to determine a lower bound on each LLM's accuracy.  This allows it to aggregate responses via weighted majority voting while reducing the computational cost compared to querying all available LLMs. Experiments on MMLU and IMDB datasets demonstrate that CaMVo achieves comparable or better accuracy than full majority voting while significantly reducing labeling costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty (Moderate):** The novelty lies in the online adaptation of both vote weights and the queried subset of LLMs without relying on pre-training data or ground truth labels. Existing LLM routing and confidence estimation techniques often require some form of offline training. The combination of LinUCB with a Bayesian Beta-mixture confidence estimator is a practical contribution. Prior work mostly optimize only the weights. This paper adaptively selects both weights and number of votes.

*   **Significance (Moderate-High):** Reducing the annotation cost of LLM-based data labeling is crucial for wider adoption, especially as datasets become larger. While ensembling has become a common strategy, its high cost is prohibitive. This paper makes a significant step toward addressing the cost bottleneck. The empirical results demonstrate substantial cost savings, making LLM-based annotation pipelines more practical.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Online Learning:** The online nature of the algorithm is a significant advantage as it doesn't require initial training data and can adapt to changing conditions.
    *   **Cost-Efficiency:** Empirical results clearly demonstrate a substantial reduction in labeling costs compared to full majority voting, without sacrificing accuracy.
    *   **Theoretical Soundness:**  The integration of LinUCB and Bayesian estimation provides a solid theoretical foundation.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and explains the algorithm clearly, including pseudo-code and proofs.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** Evaluation on two diverse datasets (MMLU and IMDB) demonstrates the generalizability of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Independence Assumption:**  The algorithm relies on the assumption that the outputs of LLMs are independent. This assumption is often violated in practice, especially when using similar LLMs. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Limited LLM Diversity:** The ensembles used in the experiments may not be fully representative of the entire LLM landscape. Further testing with a wider range of LLMs could strengthen the claims.
