    *   **Dependence of embedding model**. The performance of the method depends on the quality of embeddings used.
    *  **Lack of comparison with some baselines.** The selection can be implemented with some other algorithms which are widely used for multi-armed bandits.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   CaMVo has the potential to significantly reduce the cost of LLM-based dataset annotation, making it accessible to a broader range of users and applications.
    *   The algorithm's online learning capability makes it well-suited for dynamic labeling environments.
    *   It inspires further research in the field of cost-efficient LLM utilization.

*   **Justification for the Score:**
The paper offers a valuable and practical solution to a significant problem (cost-efficient LLM-based annotation). The approach is theoretically grounded, empirically validated, and presented clearly. The primary limitation is the reliance on the independence assumption and the lack of comparison with some simple baseline selection methods. This somewhat limits the scope of potential impact. Nevertheless, it's a well-executed piece of work that advances the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mechanistic evaluation of Transformers and state space models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15105v1)
- **Authors**: Aryaman Arora, Neil Rathi, Nikil Roashan Selvam, Róbert Csórdas, Dan Jurafsky, Christopher Potts
- **Abstract**: State space models (SSMs) for language modelling promise an efficient and performant alternative to quadratic-attention Transformers, yet show variable performance on recalling basic information from the context. While performance on synthetic tasks like Associative Recall (AR) can point to this deficiency, behavioural metrics provide little information as to why--on a mechanistic level--certain architectures fail and others succeed. To address this, we conduct experiments on AR and find that only Transformers and Based SSM models fully succeed at AR, with Mamba a close third, whereas the other SSMs (H3, Hyena) fail. We then use causal interventions to explain why. We find that Transformers and Based learn to store key-value associations in-context using induction heads. By contrast, the SSMs compute these associations only at the last state, with only Mamba succeeding because of its short convolution component. To extend and deepen these findings, we introduce Associative Treecall (ATR), a synthetic task similar to AR based on PCFG induction. ATR introduces language-like hierarchical structure into the AR setting. We find that all architectures learn the same mechanism as they did for AR, and the same three models succeed at the task. These results reveal that architectures with similar accuracy may still have substantive differences, motivating the adoption of mechanistic evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the mechanistic differences between Transformers and State Space Models (SSMs) on synthetic in-context retrieval tasks, specifically Associative Recall (AR) and a novel extension called Associative Treecall (ATR). The authors use causal interventions to analyze how these architectures solve these tasks. They find that Transformers and a variant of SSMs called Based learn to store key-value associations using induction heads, while other SSMs rely on direct retrieval at the last state. They also demonstrate that Mamba leverages short convolutions to succeed in these tasks. The ATR task introduces hierarchical structure, and the authors find that similar mechanisms are used across architectures for both AR and ATR. The paper argues that mechanistic evaluations reveal fundamental differences that behavioral metrics alone cannot capture, providing a new tool for architecture analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of mechanistic interpretability techniques to compare Transformers and SSMs on synthetic retrieval tasks. While AR is a well-established task, the introduction of ATR as a hierarchical variant, and the systematic use of causal interventions across multiple architectures to understand their internal mechanisms, is novel. Comparing different architectures through mechanistic interpretability is a significant step forward.
*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they challenge the notion that achieving similar performance on benchmarks implies similar internal mechanisms. The paper shows that Transformers, Based, and other SSMs solve in-context retrieval problems in fundamentally different ways. Further, identifying the role of short convolutions in Mamba's performance provides actionable insights for architecture design. This work motivates using mechanistic evaluations to understand the strengths and weaknesses of different architectures more deeply, going beyond mere performance comparisons. The introduction of ATR creates a potential testing ground for understanding hierarchical structure learning in language models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined methodology, combining behavioral evaluations with causal interventions.
    *   **Clear Explanations:** The explanations of the different architectures and the mechanisms they employ are relatively clear, supported by experimental evidence.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides actionable insights by identifying the role of short convolutions in Mamba, which can guide future architectural improvements.
    *   **Novel Task:** The introduction of the ATR task and comparing the mechanisms of different architectures to perform it is a novel and valuable contribution.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Tasks:** The reliance on synthetic tasks raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to real-world language modeling scenarios. While the authors argue that ATR is more realistic than AR, it is still a simplification of natural language.
    *   **Limited Scope of Architectures:** Although the paper tests several popular architectures, it doesn't cover the entire spectrum of existing efficient alternatives to Transformers. Further exploration might reveal other interesting mechanisms.
    *   **Generalization of Mamba's Solutions:** The paper concludes that Mamba also achieves high generalizability performance on ATR, although, on a high level, it is due to high memorization, so one should consider this point when evaluating Mamba.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by advocating for the adoption of mechanistic evaluations as a standard practice for architecture analysis. It can also inspire further research on understanding the internal mechanisms of different language models, as well as on developing more realistic synthetic tasks.
*   **Score:** 7/10

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates solid novelty by extending mechanistic interpretability techniques to compare Transformers and SSMs. The actionable insights it provides regarding Mamba and short convolutions, the introduction of the ATR task, and the general push for richer model analysis contribute to the paper's significance. The limitations surrounding the reliance on synthetic tasks and the limited scope of architectures prevent it from being a higher score. However, overall, this is a contribution that encourages the move toward mechanistic understanding in architecture comparison, which leads to a reasonable impact in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### StepSearch: Igniting LLMs Search Ability via Step-Wise Proximal Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15107v1)
- **Authors**: Ziliang Wang, Xuhui Zheng, Kang An, Cijun Ouyang, Jialu Cai, Yuhang Wang, Yichao Wu
- **Abstract**: Efficient multi-hop reasoning requires Large Language Models (LLMs) based agents to acquire high-value external knowledge iteratively. Previous work has explored reinforcement learning (RL) to train LLMs to perform search-based document retrieval, achieving notable improvements in QA performance, but underperform on complex, multi-hop QA resulting from the sparse rewards from global signal only. To address this gap in existing research, we introduce StepSearch, a framework for search LLMs that trained with step-wise proximal policy optimization method. It consists of richer and more detailed intermediate search rewards and token-level process supervision based on information gain and redundancy penalties to better guide each search step. We constructed a fine-grained question-answering dataset containing sub-question-level search trajectories based on open source datasets through a set of data pipeline method. On standard multi-hop QA benchmarks, it significantly outperforms global-reward baselines, achieving 11.2% and 4.2% absolute improvements for 3B and 7B models over various search with RL baselines using only 19k training data, demonstrating the effectiveness of fine-grained, stepwise supervision in optimizing deep search LLMs. Our implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/zxh20001117/StepSearch.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "StepSearch: Igniting LLMs Search Ability via Step-Wise Proximal Policy Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces StepSearch, a novel reinforcement learning framework designed to enhance the search capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) for multi-hop question answering.  The core idea is to move beyond coarse, global reward signals in RL and instead provide fine-grained, step-wise supervision during the search process. This is achieved by:

1.  **Rich Intermediate Rewards:**  Designing rewards that incentivize both information gain and penalize redundancy at each search step.
2.  **Token-Level Supervision:**  Applying information gain and redundancy penalties at the token level within each search query.
3.  **Data Augmentation Pipeline:**  Creating a new dataset of question-answering examples with sub-question-aligned search keyword trajectories based on the MuSiQue dataset. This generates a dataset tailored for step-wise RL.

The StepSearch framework is evaluated on several multi-hop QA benchmarks. The results demonstrate significant improvements over baseline RL methods that rely solely on global reward signals. The paper emphasizes the effectiveness of fine-grained, stepwise supervision in training LLMs to perform more effective iterative search.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a strong claim of novelty in its fine-grained supervision approach. While using RL for training LLMs with search capabilities isn't new, the implementation of step-wise reward functions with token-level information gain and redundancy penalties marks a departure from existing approaches. Creating a dataset that explicitly captures intermediate search steps is also a contribution. It also shows how to train even smaller models to outperform larger models.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper lies in its potential to improve the performance of LLM-based agents in complex reasoning tasks. Multi-hop QA is a challenging problem, and the results presented demonstrate that StepSearch can effectively address the limitations of relying solely on global reward signals. This has direct implications for applications such as information retrieval, knowledge discovery, and decision-making systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing RL-based approaches for training search LLMs.
*   **Well-Defined Framework:** StepSearch is presented as a cohesive and well-engineered framework with clear components.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates StepSearch on several benchmarks and compares it against diverse baselines.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results consistently show significant improvements over baseline methods.
*   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides valuable insights into the contribution of different components of the StepSearch framework.
*   **Case Studies:** The case studies provide qualitative examples of how the StepSearch framework improves the model's search process.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Dependence:** The data augmentation pipeline is specific to the MuSiQue dataset. The framework's adaptability to other datasets and languages should be investigated further.
*   **Scalability:** The paper focuses on relatively small LLMs (3B and 7B). It is unclear how well StepSearch would scale to larger models and more complex tasks. There's a mention of limitations regarding scalability in the paper, but it's crucial to emphasize this point in a critical analysis.
*   **Environmental Assumptions:** The paper's reliance on publicly available resources (like wikipedia) makes it useful, but also limits it due to lack of real world data.

**Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

*   **Step-Wise Supervision:**  The idea of providing fine-grained, step-wise supervision could be adopted in other RL applications for LLMs.
*   **Information Gain and Redundancy Penalties:**  The use of information gain and redundancy penalties could be explored in other search-based tasks.
*   **Data Augmentation for RL:** The data augmentation pipeline could be adapted for other LLM training tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8** to this paper.

*   **Positives:** The paper is a solid contribution to the field, clearly addressing a well-defined problem and providing a novel solution with strong empirical support. The fine-grained supervision approach is a significant departure from existing RL methods.
*   **Negatives:** The limitations related to data dependency and scalability prevent the paper from receiving a higher score. While the improvements are significant, the impact could be limited by these factors. The potential for issues in the real world are also limiting.

### A Risk Taxonomy for Evaluating AI-Powered Psychotherapy Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15108v1)
- **Authors**: Ian Steenstra, Timothy W. Bickmore
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Intelligent Virtual Agents acting as psychotherapists presents significant opportunities for expanding mental healthcare access. However, their deployment has also been linked to serious adverse outcomes, including user harm and suicide, facilitated by a lack of standardized evaluation methodologies capable of capturing the nuanced risks of therapeutic interaction. Current evaluation techniques lack the sensitivity to detect subtle changes in patient cognition and behavior during therapy sessions that may lead to subsequent decompensation. We introduce a novel risk taxonomy specifically designed for the systematic evaluation of conversational AI psychotherapists. Developed through an iterative process including review of the psychotherapy risk literature, qualitative interviews with clinical and legal experts, and alignment with established clinical criteria (e.g., DSM-5) and existing assessment tools (e.g., NEQ, UE-ATR), the taxonomy aims to provide a structured approach to identifying and assessing user/patient harms. We provide a high-level overview of this taxonomy, detailing its grounding, and discuss potential use cases. We discuss two use cases in detail: monitoring cognitive model-based risk factors during a counseling conversation to detect unsafe deviations, in both human-AI counseling sessions and in automated benchmarking of AI psychotherapists with simulated patients. The proposed taxonomy offers a foundational step towards establishing safer and more responsible innovation in the domain of AI-driven mental health support.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel risk taxonomy specifically designed to evaluate AI-powered psychotherapy agents. It addresses the lack of standardized evaluation methodologies for these systems and aims to provide a structured approach to identifying and assessing user/patient harms. The taxonomy was developed through a literature review, expert interviews, and alignment with clinical criteria and assessment tools. It categorizes risks into "Immediate Risk" and "Potential Risk" and details potential use cases such as monitoring real user interactions, evaluating with simulated patients, and benchmarking different AI therapists. The paper argues that this taxonomy is a crucial step towards safer and more responsible innovation in AI-driven mental health support.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its focused application of risk assessment principles to the emerging field of AI psychotherapy. While risk taxonomies are not new concepts in healthcare or AI safety, the specific adaptation and tailoring of such a framework to the nuanced and sensitive domain of AI-driven therapy is a significant contribution. It moves beyond generic AI safety benchmarks (e.g., toxicity detection) by considering the specific relational and psychological risks inherent in therapeutic interactions.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper is substantial. It directly addresses a critical gap in the responsible development and deployment of AI mental health tools. The potential for harm (including suicide, as cited) is a real and present danger, and the absence of standardized evaluation methods has been a major impediment to ensuring safety. This work provides a framework for systematic assessment, allowing developers, regulators, and healthcare providers to better understand and mitigate risks. The structured approach can also facilitate comparative analysis of different AI systems and inform ethical guidelines.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-grounded methodology:** The taxonomy's development is based on a rigorous and multi-faceted approach, incorporating literature review, expert interviews, and alignment with existing clinical criteria (DSM-5) and assessment tools (NEQ, UE-ATR).
    *   **Clear structure and categorization:** The categorization into "Immediate" and "Potential" risk allows for differentiated responses, focusing on both crisis management and proactive monitoring of vulnerabilities.
    *   **Practical use cases:** The paper outlines concrete use cases that demonstrate how the taxonomy can be implemented in real-world scenarios (e.g., real-time monitoring, simulated patient testing, benchmarking).
    *   **Adaptability:** The framework is designed to be adaptable to specific populations or problems.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of validation:** The taxonomy's effectiveness has not yet been empirically validated. While the development process is robust, further research is needed to demonstrate its ability to accurately identify and predict risks in actual AI psychotherapy interactions. This requires large-scale studies and comparison against human expert judgements.
    *   **Subjectivity of risk factors:** Many of the "Potential Risk" indicators rely on subjective assessments (e.g., "Hopelessness Level," "Collaboration/Engagement Level"). Establishing objective and reliable measures for these constructs will be crucial for practical implementation. While comparing change to baselines helps, validated thresholds for those changes and clearer guidelines will be necessary.
    *   **Oversimplification of nuance:** While the taxonomy strives for a structured approach, it necessarily simplifies complex human emotions and interactions. Capturing the full nuance and context-dependent nature of psychotherapy within a structured framework remains a challenge. The experts interviewed themselves acknowledged the loss of human nuance as a downside of AI therapy compared to humans.
    *   **Limited consideration of long-term effects:** The taxonomy focuses primarily on immediate and potential risks within or directly following interactions. Long-term effects of AI psychotherapy, such as dependency, altered perceptions of therapy, or impacts on real-world relationships, are less explicitly addressed.

*   **Potential influence:** The taxonomy has the potential to become a widely adopted framework in the AI mental health field. It can inform the development of safer AI systems, guide regulatory policies, and empower clinicians to make informed decisions about using these technologies. It also provides a valuable foundation for future research on risk assessment in AI psychotherapy.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**
The paper's originality, significance, and thorough methodology merit a relatively high score. It is an important and timely contribution that addresses a critical need in a rapidly evolving field. However, the lack of empirical validation and the inherent limitations of structured risk assessment in capturing the complexities of psychotherapy necessitate a more tempered score. The paper provides a strong *foundation* but requires further research and refinement to fully realize its potential impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### RoT: Enhancing Table Reasoning with Iterative Row-Wise Traversals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15110v1)
- **Authors**: Xuanliang Zhang, Dingzirui Wang, Keyan Xu, Qingfu Zhu, Wanxiang Che
- **Abstract**: The table reasoning task, crucial for efficient data acquisition, aims to answer questions based on the given table. Recently, reasoning large language models (RLLMs) with Long Chain-of-Thought (Long CoT) significantly enhance reasoning capabilities, leading to brilliant performance on table reasoning. However, Long CoT suffers from high cost for training and exhibits low reliability due to table content hallucinations. Therefore, we propose Row-of-Thought (RoT), which performs iteratively row-wise table traversal, allowing for reasoning extension and reflection-based refinement at each traversal. Scaling reasoning length by row-wise traversal and leveraging reflection capabilities of LLMs, RoT is training-free. The sequential traversal encourages greater attention to the table, thus reducing hallucinations. Experiments show that RoT, using non-reasoning models, outperforms RLLMs by an average of 4.3%, and achieves state-of-the-art results on WikiTableQuestions and TableBench with comparable models, proving its effectiveness. Also, RoT outperforms Long CoT with fewer reasoning tokens, indicating higher efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Row-of-Thought (ROT), a novel approach to enhance table reasoning capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs).  ROT addresses the limitations of Long Chain-of-Thought (Long CoT), specifically its high training costs and low reliability due to table content hallucinations.  ROT achieves this by performing iterative row-wise table traversal, allowing for reasoning extension and reflection-based refinement at each step. This sequential traversal, leveraging the reflection capabilities of LLMs, promotes greater attention to the table content, thus reducing hallucinations. The paper demonstrates that ROT, even with non-reasoning models, outperforms Long CoT and achieves state-of-the-art results on several benchmark datasets, proving its effectiveness and efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of iterative row-wise traversal is relatively novel within the specific context of table reasoning. While iterative processing and reflection are known techniques in LLMs, their application to guide attention and reduce hallucination during table processing provides a distinct contribution. Prior works have explored chain-of-thought reasoning and tool use, but ROT distinguishes itself by its focus on iterative, attention-guided table traversal without the need for task-specific training.

*   **Significance:** ROT has the potential to significantly improve table reasoning capabilities for LLMs, particularly those with limited training data or computational resources. By alleviating hallucinations and reducing training costs, ROT makes table reasoning more accessible and reliable. The empirical results, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on benchmark datasets with comparable models, highlight the significance of this approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Training-free:** ROT's ability to function effectively without task-specific training is a major strength, reducing the cost and complexity associated with fine-tuning LLMs.
    *   **Hallucination Mitigation:**  The row-wise traversal effectively guides attention to tabular content, mitigating the common problem of hallucinations in Long CoT reasoning.
    *   **Performance Improvement:**  ROT consistently outperforms Long CoT and achieves state-of-the-art results on multiple datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The analysis demonstrating ROT's superior performance with fewer reasoning tokens than Long CoT highlights its improved efficiency.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper includes thorough ablation studies, error analysis, and experiments exploring the impact of various factors, providing valuable insights into ROT's behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:**  The experiments are primarily conducted on English datasets with relatively simple table structures. Further research is needed to assess its effectiveness on multi-turn question-answering tasks, datasets in other languages, and tables with more complex layouts or relational structures.
    *   **Dependence on LLM Reflection Capabilities:** ROT heavily relies on the reflection abilities of the underlying LLMs. If the LLM has weak or poorly calibrated reflection capabilities, the performance of ROT could be limited.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** Although the paper states that the model dynamically terminates the iteration process, there may be implicit hyperparameters that still need to be tuned in certain situations.

*   **Potential Influence:** ROT could influence future research in table reasoning by highlighting the importance of attention-guided traversal and reflection-based refinement. It could also serve as a foundation for developing more efficient and reliable table reasoning methods for LLMs.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** Although the results are compelling, the novelty in mechanism is marginal, and the improvement is not huge compared to others models. Further study is needed for more datasets with diverse table structures and in different languages to prove the reliability.

**Score: 7.0**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### An Empirical Study on Reinforcement Learning for Reasoning-Search Interleaved LLM Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15117v1)
- **Authors**: Bowen Jin, Jinsung Yoon, Priyanka Kargupta, Sercan O. Arik, Jiawei Han
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has demonstrated strong potential in training large language models (LLMs) capable of complex reasoning for real-world problem solving. More recently, RL has been leveraged to create sophisticated LLM-based search agents that adeptly combine reasoning with search engine use. While the use of RL for training search agents is promising, the optimal design of such agents remains not fully understood. In particular, key factors -- such as (1) reward formulation, (2) the choice and characteristics of the underlying LLM, and (3) the role of the search engine in the RL process -- require further investigation. In this work, we conduct comprehensive empirical studies to systematically investigate these and offer actionable insights. We highlight several key findings: format rewards are effective in improving final performance, whereas intermediate retrieval rewards have limited impact; the scale and initialization of the LLM (general-purpose vs. reasoning-specialized) significantly influence RL outcomes; and the choice of search engine plays a critical role in shaping RL training dynamics and the robustness of the trained agent during inference. These establish important guidelines for successfully building and deploying LLM-based search agents in real-world applications. Code is available at https://github.com/PeterGriffinJin/Search-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper presents an empirical study on the design of Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents that interact with search engines. It investigates three key factors: reward function design (specifically the role of format and intermediate retrieval rewards), the characteristics of the underlying Large Language Model (LLM), and the choice of search engine. The study provides insights into how these factors influence RL training dynamics, final performance, and robustness during inference. The authors find that format rewards significantly improve performance, particularly for base LLMs, while intermediate retrieval rewards have limited impact. General-purpose LLMs outperform reasoning-specialized LLMs in RL settings, and the quality of the search engine during training significantly impacts RL dynamics and downstream performance.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic and comprehensive empirical investigation of factors influencing the RL training of LLM-based search agents. While individual components have been explored in previous works, this study provides a valuable consolidation and side-by-side comparison. It's not revolutionary, but it offers practical guidance. The investigation of format rewards is incrementally novel but provides useful findings about their efficacy, especially for base LLMs without strong instruction-following capabilities. The analysis of LLM type (general vs. reasoning-specialized) also contributes to a more nuanced understanding.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. Its findings provide actionable guidelines for building and deploying effective LLM-based search agents in real-world applications. The insights on reward function design can reduce experimentation costs. Understanding how the underlying LLM impacts RL outcomes enables more informed model selection. The analysis of search engine choice clarifies trade-offs between training stability and final performance, and highlights the importance of robustness during inference. The results also provide a more detailed understanding of the performance boost that RL-training provides, rather than simple prompt-engineering or fine-tuning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Evaluation:**  The paper conducts thorough experiments across multiple datasets, LLM sizes, RL algorithms, and search engines.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides specific recommendations for designing and training LLM-based search agents.
    *   **Well-Organized and Clearly Written:** The paper is easy to follow and understand.
    *   **Open-source code**: The code is available which allows others to replicate the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The paper primarily focuses on empirical findings and lacks deep theoretical analysis of the observed phenomena.  It doesn't offer substantial explanations for why some interventions are more successful than others.
    *   **Incremental Contributions:** While the findings are useful, the improvements achieved are not transformative.  The study largely confirms existing intuitions while providing more granular details.
    *   **Specific Task Focus:** The scope is limited to search agent scenarios, with a focus on factual question answering.  The generalizability of the findings to other agentic tasks remains unclear.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings are likely to be relevant to researchers and practitioners working on LLM-based agents, retrieval-augmented generation, and RL for language. The actionable guidelines can help improve the efficiency and effectiveness of these applications. However, the incremental nature of the contributions limits its overall impact on the broader field.

**Justification for Score**

The paper is valuable due to its systematic empirical investigation and actionable insights, but its limited theoretical depth and incremental contributions prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the findings offer practical guidance for building LLM-based search agents, they don't represent a groundbreaking advancement in the field. Other works such as ReAct offer potentially more powerful alternatives.

Score: 6

- **Score**: 6/10

### The Unreasonable Effectiveness of Entropy Minimization in LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15134v1)
- **Authors**: Shivam Agarwal, Zimin Zhang, Lifan Yuan, Jiawei Han, Hao Peng
- **Abstract**: Entropy minimization (EM) trains the model to concentrate even more probability mass on its most confident outputs. We show that this simple objective alone, without any labeled data, can substantially improve large language models' (LLMs) performance on challenging math, physics, and coding tasks. We explore three approaches: (1) EM-FT minimizes token-level entropy similarly to instruction finetuning, but on unlabeled outputs drawn from the model; (2) EM-RL: reinforcement learning with negative entropy as the only reward to maximize; (3) EM-INF: inference-time logit adjustment to reduce entropy without any training data or parameter updates. On Qwen-7B, EM-RL, without any labeled data, achieves comparable or better performance than strong RL baselines such as GRPO and RLOO that are trained on 60K labeled examples. Furthermore, EM-INF enables Qwen-32B to match or exceed the performance of proprietary models like GPT-4o, Claude 3 Opus, and Gemini 1.5 Pro on the challenging SciCode benchmark, while being 3x more efficient than self-consistency and sequential refinement. Our findings reveal that many pretrained LLMs possess previously underappreciated reasoning capabilities that can be effectively elicited through entropy minimization alone, without any labeled data or even any parameter updates.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Unreasonable Effectiveness of Entropy Minimization in LLM Reasoning" explores using entropy minimization (EM) as a training and inference technique for Large Language Models (LLMs) on reasoning tasks.  The authors introduce three methods: EM-FT (finetuning), EM-RL (reinforcement learning), and EM-INF (inference-time scaling).  EM-FT and EM-RL minimize token-level entropy during training without labeled data, while EM-INF optimizes logits during inference, also without training.  They demonstrate that EM alone can significantly improve performance on math, physics, and coding tasks, and even surpass supervised and RL-based baselines in some cases. The paper also highlights the limitations of EM, such as its reliance on a model's initial capabilities and potential ineffectiveness in tasks where confidence does not correlate with correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of using entropy minimization as a standalone objective for LLM finetuning and inference *has some degree of novelty*, *but it's not entirely groundbreaking*. EM has a history in semi-supervised learning and domain adaptation, but *its application as the primary and, in some cases, only training signal for LLMs*, especially in the context of complex reasoning tasks and in conjunction with RL, is relatively new. The specific implementations of EM-FT, EM-RL, and EM-INF, and the empirical exploration of their capabilities, add to this novelty.  However, the core concept of minimizing uncertainty to improve performance is well-established.  The biggest novelty arguably lies in demonstrating its effectiveness on reasoning tasks *without* any labeled data. *However, some components, like the use of entropy regularization in RL, are based on more established principles, reducing the relative novelty of the approach*.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its *surprising* finding that EM can unlock previously underappreciated reasoning abilities in pre-trained LLMs, even *without* labeled data or parameter updates (EM-INF).  This suggests that current LLMs are not being fully exploited for their reasoning potential. The results present a cost-effective pathway to enhance LLM performance on challenging tasks. Furthermore, the paper advocates for using EM as a baseline in future LLM research, to better understand what the algorithmic innovations add as well as the models intrinsic capabilities.  This is a good suggestion. *However, the practical impact might be limited by the reliance on strong pre-trained models and its ineffectiveness in certain domains, potentially requiring significant pre-training effort anyway*. *And, the technique, while successful on some specific tasks, might not generalize to all domains of LLM application, which would also somewhat limits its immediate impact on the field*.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results across a variety of tasks, model sizes, and approaches. The comparisons against strong baselines (including supervised and RL methods) are convincing.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The methods are well-defined and explained, making them reproducible.
*   **Important Insights:**  The findings challenge the common assumption that labeled data is *always* necessary for improving LLM reasoning. The identification of limitations (model capability, confidence-correctness correlation) is crucial for understanding the method's applicability.
*   **EM-INF is particularly appealing as a practical and compute-efficient inference scaling method, because it does not require training.**

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generality:** The effectiveness of EM appears to be task-dependent and model-dependent. The negative results on individualistic value reasoning and Llama-3.1 highlight these limitations. More research is needed to understand the conditions under which EM is most effective.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The paper is primarily empirical. A theoretical analysis of why EM works, and under what conditions it is expected to work, would strengthen the contribution.
*   **Presentation:** Although clear, the organization is not optimal: related work is presented after the methodology which is suboptimal.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel application of an established technique (entropy minimization) to a contemporary problem (LLM reasoning). The results are compelling and the insights are valuable. The identification of the limitations helps qualify and contextualize the contribution. EM-INF's practicality adds a significant dimension. Given the strengths and the existing work of EM (not entirely new), but considering the *significant* and often *surprising* empirical findings regarding its effectiveness as a standalone method in LLM reasoning, especially EM-INF's inference scaling advantage, the paper is above average. The identified weaknesses temper the overall assessment.

### Hybrid Audio Detection Using Fine-Tuned Audio Spectrogram Transformers: A Dataset-Driven Evaluation of Mixed AI-Human Speech
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15136v1)
- **Authors**: Kunyang Huang, Bin Hu
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of artificial intelligence (AI) has enabled sophisticated audio generation and voice cloning technologies, posing significant security risks for applications reliant on voice authentication. While existing datasets and models primarily focus on distinguishing between human and fully synthetic speech, real-world attacks often involve audio that combines both genuine and cloned segments. To address this gap, we construct a novel hybrid audio dataset incorporating human, AI-generated, cloned, and mixed audio samples. We further propose fine-tuned Audio Spectrogram Transformer (AST)-based models tailored for detecting these complex acoustic patterns. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms existing baselines in mixed-audio detection, achieving 97\% classification accuracy. Our findings highlight the importance of hybrid datasets and tailored models in advancing the robustness of speech-based authentication systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the vulnerability of voice authentication systems to hybrid attacks, where real and AI-generated speech segments are combined. To tackle this, the authors construct a novel hybrid audio dataset containing human, AI-generated, cloned, and mixed audio samples. They then fine-tune Audio Spectrogram Transformer (AST)-based models specifically for detecting these complex acoustic patterns. Experimental results demonstrate a significant improvement in mixed-audio detection accuracy compared to existing baselines, reaching 97% classification accuracy. The paper highlights the importance of hybrid datasets and tailored models in enhancing the robustness of speech-based authentication systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a hybrid audio dataset. While existing datasets focus on differentiating between genuine and fully synthetic speech, this paper directly addresses the more realistic scenario of combined real and AI-generated audio, which more closely resembles real-world attack vectors. The fine-tuning of AST models is a logical step given the architecture's success in audio classification, but the primary contribution is the dataset itself. The creation of composite audio, fine-grained annotations of tampering, and controlled utterance lengths is definitely a beneficial contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical implications for securing voice authentication systems. The identified vulnerability is real, and the improved detection accuracy demonstrated by the proposed approach is a positive step. The construction of a publicly available hybrid dataset would also benefit the research community and help advance the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Realistic Threat Modeling:** The paper effectively models real-world attacks by considering hybrid audio scenarios.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The created dataset is well-defined with detailed annotations and controlled conditions, making it a valuable resource.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The fine-tuned AST models demonstrate a notable improvement in detection accuracy, validating the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Spoofing Coverage:** The dataset primarily focuses on TTS synthesis and AI-based voice cloning, neglecting other potential attack vectors like voice conversion and adversarial examples.
    *   **Controlled Acoustic Environments:** The clean speech recordings were collected under controlled conditions, limiting the dataset's realism and generalizability to noisy environments.
    *   **Limited Speaker Pool:** A speaker pool of only 12 individuals may not reflect the diversity needed for robust model training and evaluation.
    *   **Lack of Annotation Granularity:** Although including hybrid composition is positive, segment-level localisation of tampered audio would enhance its capabilities.

*   **Potential Influence:** This dataset and research effort can influence future research by shifting the focus towards more realistic attack scenarios and encouraging the development of robust anti-spoofing methods. This work can also contribute to the development of new datasets and better evaluation metrics.

The paper has a clearly defined problem statement and presents a sensible solution. The limitations section is comprehensive and well-written. However, the significance and novelty are slightly hampered by the restricted speaker set and limited analysis across alternative spoofing techniques. The results reported are strong, and it builds upon prior work in a well-structured manner.

- **Score**: 7/10

### BanditSpec: Adaptive Speculative Decoding via Bandit Algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15141v1)
- **Authors**: Yunlong Hou, Fengzhuo Zhang, Cunxiao Du, Xuan Zhang, Jiachun Pan, Tianyu Pang, Chao Du, Vincent Y. F. Tan, Zhuoran Yang
- **Abstract**: Speculative decoding has emerged as a popular method to accelerate the inference of Large Language Models (LLMs) while retaining their superior text generation performance. Previous methods either adopt a fixed speculative decoding configuration regardless of the prefix tokens, or train draft models in an offline or online manner to align them with the context. This paper proposes a training-free online learning framework to adaptively choose the configuration of the hyperparameters for speculative decoding as text is being generated. We first formulate this hyperparameter selection problem as a Multi-Armed Bandit problem and provide a general speculative decoding framework BanditSpec. Furthermore, two bandit-based hyperparameter selection algorithms, UCBSpec and EXP3Spec, are designed and analyzed in terms of a novel quantity, the stopping time regret. We upper bound this regret under both stochastic and adversarial reward settings. By deriving an information-theoretic impossibility result, it is shown that the regret performance of UCBSpec is optimal up to universal constants. Finally, extensive empirical experiments with LLaMA3 and Qwen2 demonstrate that our algorithms are effective compared to existing methods, and the throughput is close to the oracle best hyperparameter in simulated real-life LLM serving scenarios with diverse input prompts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BANDITSPEC: Adaptive Speculative Decoding via Bandit Algorithms":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BANDITSPEC, a novel training-free online learning framework designed to adaptively select optimal configurations for speculative decoding, a technique used to accelerate large language model (LLM) inference.  The core idea is to formulate hyperparameter selection as a Multi-Armed Bandit (MAB) problem. The authors design two bandit-based algorithms, UCBSPEC and EXP3SPEC, and provide theoretical analysis including stopping time regret bounds under stochastic and adversarial reward settings. Empirical results using LLaMA3 and Qwen2 models demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach, achieving throughput comparable to an oracle-best hyperparameter setting in realistic LLM serving scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of using a bandit algorithm to adaptively select speculative decoding hyperparameters is novel. Prior work either used fixed configurations or trained draft models. Applying online learning in this way offers a flexible, training-free approach. The theoretical analysis provides solid footing for the algorithms, though the assumptions should be carefully considered.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the efficiency of LLM inference. Speculative decoding is already a widely adopted technique; by optimizing its configuration adaptively, BANDITSPEC can contribute to faster and more efficient LLM deployment. The experiments, though limited to smaller models and a specific hardware setup, show promising results, suggesting its practical relevance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Training-free approach:** Avoids the complexities and resource requirements of training or fine-tuning draft models.
    * **Adaptive configuration:** Selects an optimal hyperparameter on the fly, eliminating the need to train specific scenarios.
    * **Theoretical analysis:** Provides strong theoretical guarantees on the performance of the algorithms.
    * **Empirical validation:** Validates the theoretical findings with comprehensive experiments.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Model and Hardware:** The experiments primarily use LLaMA3-8B and Qwen2-7B on A100 GPUs (with some tests on a RTX 4090). Scaling the approach to larger models and other hardware configurations requires more investigation.
    * **Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis relies on certain assumptions (e.g., stationary mean values, finite generation length, stopping time assumption) that might not always hold in practice, potentially limiting the applicability of the theoretical guarantees. Further discussion about each assumptions could make the paper stronger.
    * **Complexity:** While training-free, the bandit algorithms themselves add complexity to the decoding process. It is also not clear how much overhead time the bandit algorithm takes at each step.

* **Impact:** This work could influence future research on LLM inference optimization. It could also lead to the development of more adaptive and efficient LLM serving systems. Further investigation on the limitations, however, should be done before actual implemention of the method.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel approach with strong theoretical backing and promising results, the reliance on assumptions and limited scope of the experiments prevents it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution *at this stage*. The novelty is solid, and the potential impact is significant. The experiments show improvement over baselines, but the gap isn't so overwhelmingly large as to declare the problem solved or a complete paradigm shift in speculative decoding.

### CineTechBench: A Benchmark for Cinematographic Technique Understanding and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15145v1)
- **Authors**: Xinran Wang, Songyu Xu, Xiangxuan Shan, Yuxuan Zhang, Muxi Diao, Xueyan Duan, Yanhua Huang, Kongming Liang, Zhanyu Ma
- **Abstract**: Cinematography is a cornerstone of film production and appreciation, shaping mood, emotion, and narrative through visual elements such as camera movement, shot composition, and lighting. Despite recent progress in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and video generation models, the capacity of current models to grasp and reproduce cinematographic techniques remains largely uncharted, hindered by the scarcity of expert-annotated data. To bridge this gap, we present CineTechBench, a pioneering benchmark founded on precise, manual annotation by seasoned cinematography experts across key cinematography dimensions. Our benchmark covers seven essential aspects-shot scale, shot angle, composition, camera movement, lighting, color, and focal length-and includes over 600 annotated movie images and 120 movie clips with clear cinematographic techniques. For the understanding task, we design question answer pairs and annotated descriptions to assess MLLMs' ability to interpret and explain cinematographic techniques. For the generation task, we assess advanced video generation models on their capacity to reconstruct cinema-quality camera movements given conditions such as textual prompts or keyframes. We conduct a large-scale evaluation on 15+ MLLMs and 5+ video generation models. Our results offer insights into the limitations of current models and future directions for cinematography understanding and generation in automatically film production and appreciation. The code and benchmark can be accessed at https://github.com/PRIS-CV/CineTechBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CineTechBench, a novel benchmark for evaluating the understanding and generation capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and video generation models specifically concerning cinematographic techniques. The benchmark consists of manually annotated movie images and clips, categorized according to seven core cinematographic dimensions: shot scale, shot angle, composition, camera movement, lighting, color, and focal length. The benchmark is designed to assess the ability of models to: a) understand and interpret cinematographic techniques through question-answering and descriptive captioning, and b) generate video content that accurately reflects specific cinematographic intentions. The authors conduct extensive evaluations of existing MLLMs and video generation models using CineTechBench, highlighting limitations in their understanding and reproduction of nuanced cinematographic styles and camera movements. The paper concludes by emphasizing the need for further research in this area and providing the benchmark as a resource to drive progress.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in the creation of CineTechBench itself. Existing benchmarks for movie and video understanding primarily focus on high-level semantic information (plot, genre, etc.). CineTechBench directly addresses a crucial gap by focusing on the lower-level, but fundamentally important, cinematographic aspects. The precise manual annotation from domain experts significantly increases the benchmark quality. The taxonomy of cinematographic techniques provides a structured approach for evaluating models.

*   **Significance:** The ability of AI models to understand and generate cinematographic techniques has several important implications:

    *   **Automated Film Production:** Could lead to AI-assisted tools for filmmakers, automating tasks like camera direction, lighting design, and post-production visual style.
    *   **Content Creation & Analysis:** Enables AI to analyze films at a deeper level, identifying stylistic patterns, understanding directorial intent, and potentially even generating novel content based on learned cinematographic principles.
    *   **Accessibility:** More sophisticated video editing tools could democratize content creation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Taxonomy:** The proposed taxonomy is comprehensive, covering essential dimensions of cinematography.
    *   **Rigorous Annotation:** Manual annotation by experts ensures high data quality.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation covers a wide range of MLLMs and video generation models, providing a broad overview of the current state of the art.
    *   **Clear Identification of Limitations:** The paper highlights the specific areas where current models struggle (e.g., recognizing fixed shots, camera rotation with high amplitude, nuanced lighting conditions), providing direction for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale:** While the manual annotation ensures quality, the dataset size (600 images, 120 clips) might be a limiting factor in capturing the full diversity of cinematographic styles. Future work to scale annotation, even with semi-supervised methods, would be greatly beneficial.
    *   **Camera Trajectory Estimation:** The use of open-source tools for camera trajectory estimation is a potential source of error, which is acknowledged by the authors. Better trajectory estimation algorithms would enhance the benchmark's robustness.
    *   **Subjectivity in Annotation:** Despite expert annotation, some inherent subjectivity remains in categorizing cinematographic techniques. Addressing this with multiple annotators and inter-rater reliability metrics would strengthen the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** CineTechBench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark in the field of multimedia AI and computer vision. It provides a necessary tool for evaluating and driving progress in the development of AI systems that can truly understand and generate cinematic content. The results presented already suggest clear areas where current models need to improve. The study can further inspire research into more effective techniques for camera trajectory estimation and semi-automated annotation.

*   **Justification:** While the novelty of creating a specialized benchmark is strong, there have been related efforts. The benchmark size could be larger, and there's room to improve the methods for evaluating camera movement. The main influence, then, becomes the structured categorization and the pinpointing of the limitations of LLMs as that enables future work.

### Time Tracker: Mixture-of-Experts-Enhanced Foundation Time Series Forecasting Model with Decoupled Training Pipelines
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15151v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaohou Shi, Ke Li, Aobo Liang, Yan Sun
- **Abstract**: In the past few years, time series foundation models have achieved superior predicting accuracy. However, real-world time series often exhibit significant diversity in their temporal patterns across different time spans and domains, making it challenging for a single model architecture to fit all complex scenarios. In addition, time series data may have multiple variables exhibiting complex correlations between each other. Recent mainstream works have focused on modeling times series in a channel-independent manner in both pretraining and finetuning stages, overlooking the valuable inter-series dependencies. To this end, we propose \textbf{Time Tracker} for better predictions on multivariate time series data. Firstly, we leverage sparse mixture of experts (MoE) within Transformers to handle the modeling of diverse time series patterns, thereby alleviating the learning difficulties of a single model while improving its generalization. Besides, we propose Any-variate Attention, enabling a unified model structure to seamlessly handle both univariate and multivariate time series, thereby supporting channel-independent modeling during pretraining and channel-mixed modeling for finetuning. Furthermore, we design a graph learning module that constructs relations among sequences from frequency-domain features, providing more precise guidance to capture inter-series dependencies in channel-mixed modeling. Based on these advancements, Time Tracker achieves state-of-the-art performance in predicting accuracy, model generalization and adaptability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Time Tracker: Mixture-of-Experts-Enhanced Foundation Time Series Forecasting Model with Decoupled Training Pipelines":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Time Tracker, a new foundation model architecture for time series forecasting designed to handle the inherent heterogeneity present in real-world time series data.  It leverages a mixture-of-experts (MoE) within a Transformer to better model diverse temporal patterns, an "Any-variate Attention" (AVA) mechanism for handling both univariate and multivariate time series in a unified manner, and a frequency-based graph learning module to capture inter-series dependencies during fine-tuning. The pre-training stage employs a channel-independent approach for generalization, while fine-tuning utilizes the AVA and graph learning module to adapt to specific datasets. The paper claims state-of-the-art performance in predicting accuracy, generalization, and adaptability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces several components that, while not entirely groundbreaking individually, are combined in a novel way to address a specific problem in time series forecasting:
    * **MoE for Time Series Heterogeneity:**  Applying MoE to time series isn't entirely new, but the paper's justification for using it to handle diverse temporal patterns within time series and across variables is well-reasoned.  Specifically, the idea of assigning sequence tokens based on data distribution to specific experts is a sound approach.
    * **Any-Variate Attention:**  The concept of AVA to handle both univariate and multivariate data in a unified model is a significant contribution.  This simplifies the training and deployment process. It is innovative in its approach of handling channel dependency in a generative fashion.
    * **Frequency-Based Graph Learning:** This is perhaps the most innovative aspect. Using the frequency domain to determine relationships between time series and create an adaptive adjacency matrix is a unique approach to capturing inter-series dependencies. The combination with causal attention enhances the method significantly.
    * **Decoupled Training:**  The approach of channel-independent pre-training followed by channel-aware fine-tuning, while conceptually straightforward, is critical to adapt to data dependencies in a generative fashion.

* **Significance:**
    * **Addressing Real-World Complexity:**  The paper tackles the core problem of heterogeneity in real-world time series data, which often limits the performance of single-model architectures. The results presented show improvements over existing state-of-the-art methods, indicating a valuable contribution to the field.
    * **Improved Generalization and Adaptability:**  The decoupled training approach, combined with the MoE and adaptive graph learning, makes the model better at adapting to new datasets with potentially different inter-series dependencies.  This is essential for real-world applications.
    * **Practical Implications:**  A single, adaptable model that can be fine-tuned for various time series forecasting tasks would be highly valuable in practice.  The performance gains demonstrated in the experiments make the model a strong contender for real-world deployment.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of heterogeneity and inter-series dependencies in time series forecasting.
    * **Well-Justified Design:**  The components of Time Tracker are carefully designed and justified based on the identified problems.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of Time Tracker compared to existing state-of-the-art models across multiple datasets in terms of predicting accuracy, model generalization and adaptabilty.
    * **Detailed Methodology:** The method section clearly describes the design of the proposed method.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Complexity:** The addition of the MoE layers and the graph learning module likely increases the computational complexity of the model. The paper does not provide a detailed analysis of the trade-off between accuracy and computational cost.
    * **Over-Parameterization:** While MoE addresses the scalability issues, adding a graph learning module could potentially lead to over-parameterization and overfitting.
    * **Scalability analysis** The analysis is not sufficient to support the claim on scalability.

* **Potential Impact:**
    *  The research can pave the way for more advanced time series modeling.
    * The research can be used in different practical fields.

**Justification for Score:**

The "Time Tracker" paper presents a significant advance in time series foundation models. The combined use of MoE, Any-variate Attention, adaptive graph learning, and decoupled training effectively addresses the challenges of heterogeneity and inter-series dependencies. While individual components have been explored before, the novel combination, coupled with strong empirical results demonstrating state-of-the-art performance, justifies a high score. However, a greater analysis on computational complexity of the models is warranted.

### Sculpting Features from Noise: Reward-Guided Hierarchical Diffusion for Task-Optimal Feature Transformation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15152v1)
- **Authors**: Nanxu Gong, Zijun Li, Sixun Dong, Haoyue Bai, Wangyang Ying, Xinyuan Wang, Yanjie Fu
- **Abstract**: Feature Transformation (FT) crafts new features from original ones via mathematical operations to enhance dataset expressiveness for downstream models. However, existing FT methods exhibit critical limitations: discrete search struggles with enormous combinatorial spaces, impeding practical use; and continuous search, being highly sensitive to initialization and step sizes, often becomes trapped in local optima, restricting global exploration. To overcome these limitations, DIFFT redefines FT as a reward-guided generative task. It first learns a compact and expressive latent space for feature sets using a Variational Auto-Encoder (VAE). A Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) then navigates this space to generate high-quality feature embeddings, its trajectory guided by a performance evaluator towards task-specific optima. This synthesis of global distribution learning (from LDM) and targeted optimization (reward guidance) produces potent embeddings, which a novel semi-autoregressive decoder efficiently converts into structured, discrete features, preserving intra-feature dependencies while allowing parallel inter-feature generation. Extensive experiments on 14 benchmark datasets show DIFFT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in predictive accuracy and robustness, with significantly lower training and inference times.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DIFFT, a novel reward-guided hierarchical diffusion framework for feature transformation (FT).  FT aims to create new, more expressive features from existing ones to improve the performance of downstream machine learning models. DIFFT addresses limitations in existing FT methods (discrete and continuous search) by framing FT as a reward-guided generative task. It uses a Variational Auto-Encoder (VAE) to learn a compact latent space for feature sets. A Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) then navigates this space, guided by a performance evaluator, to generate high-quality feature embeddings. Finally, a novel semi-autoregressive decoder converts these embeddings into structured, discrete features. Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate that DIFFT outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in terms of accuracy, robustness, and efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in the integration of several techniques into a cohesive framework for feature transformation. Specifically:
    *   **Diffusion for FT:** The use of diffusion models for feature transformation is a relatively new approach. While diffusion models are prevalent in image and natural language processing, their application to structured tabular data and feature engineering is less explored.
    *   **Reward-Guided Generation:** Guiding the diffusion process using a performance evaluator to optimize for task-specific performance is a strong component. This allows for targeted exploration of the feature space and addresses limitations of gradient-based optimization methods (local optima).
    *   **Semi-Autoregressive Decoder:** The proposed decoder balances efficiency and accuracy. Compared to fully autoregressive and non-autoregressive models, the semi-autoregressive approach offers a good compromise between speed and the ability to capture dependencies within feature chunks.
    *   **Overall Integration:** DIFFT successfully combines VAE, LDM, reward-guided generation, and a novel decoder into a single cohesive framework.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to be significant for several reasons:

    *   **Improved Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements over existing FT methods across a variety of datasets. This suggests that DIFFT is a promising approach for enhancing the performance of machine learning models.
    *   **Increased Robustness:** The ablation studies highlight the importance of each component of DIFFT, particularly the reward guidance and semi-autoregressive decoding, to robustness.
    *   **Enhanced Efficiency:** The framework demonstrates significant improvements in training and inference time compared to other feature transformation methods.
    *   **Automated Feature Engineering:** DIFFT contributes to the field of automated feature engineering, reducing the need for manual feature selection and transformation. This could make machine learning more accessible to non-experts and accelerate the development of machine learning applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach.
    *   The experimental design is comprehensive and includes a variety of datasets and baselines.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of each component of DIFFT.
    *   The code and data are publicly available, which promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Complexity:** While the paper demonstrates improved efficiency compared to other FT methods, the computational cost of training and deploying diffusion models can still be significant. The practical applicability of DIFFT may be limited by computational resources. The performance is measured without considering hyperparameter selection of the downstream classifiers.
    *   **Black-Box Nature:** Like other deep learning models, DIFFT can be difficult to interpret. The feature transformations generated by DIFFT may not be easily understandable or explainable. This can limit the trust and adoption of the framework in certain applications.
    *   **Domain Specificity**: While the paper tests 14 benchmark datasets, it is not clear how the same hyperparameters of random forest as the downstream model would perform in a general situation across domains.

*   **Overall:**

    The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of feature transformation. The integration of diffusion models and reward-guided generation is a promising approach that has the potential to improve the performance, robustness, and efficiency of machine learning models. The experimental results are compelling, and the code and data are publicly available. The primary weakness is the computational complexity of diffusion models and interpretability of the transformed features. However, the benefits of DIFFT outweigh its limitations, making it a valuable contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper is clearly above average and makes a genuine contribution to the field. However, it falls short of being truly exceptional due to the identified weaknesses. Specifically, the limitations in real-world applicability, computational cost and the lack of interpretability hinder its transformative potential. The framework demonstrates solid empirical results and addresses limitations in existing methodologies by reframing the feature transformation problem as a guided generative task using diffusion models. However, its novelty and the aforementioned factors limit it from a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prolonged Reasoning Is Not All You Need: Certainty-Based Adaptive Routing for Efficient LLM/MLLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15154v1)
- **Authors**: Jinghui Lu, Haiyang Yu, Siliang Xu, Shiwei Ran, Guozhi Tang, Siqi Wang, Bin Shan, Teng Fu, Hao Feng, Jingqun Tang, Han Wang, Can Huang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reasoning have significantly enhanced the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across diverse tasks. However, excessive reliance on chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can impair model performance and brings unnecessarily lengthened outputs, reducing efficiency. Our work reveals that prolonged reasoning does not universally improve accuracy and even degrade performance on simpler tasks. To address this, we propose Certainty-based Adaptive Reasoning (CAR), a novel framework that dynamically switches between short answers and long-form reasoning based on the model perplexity. CAR first generates a short answer and evaluates its perplexity, triggering reasoning only when the model exhibits low confidence (i.e., high perplexity). Experiments across diverse multimodal VQA/KIE benchmarks and text reasoning datasets show that CAR outperforms both short-answer and long-form reasoning approaches, striking an optimal balance between accuracy and efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Certainty-based Adaptive Reasoning (CAR), a novel framework for Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) that dynamically switches between short answers and long-form reasoning (Chain-of-Thought, CoT) based on the model's confidence. CAR uses perplexity (PPL) as a measure of model uncertainty. It first generates a short answer and assesses its PPL. If the PPL indicates low confidence (high perplexity), CAR triggers a more elaborate reasoning process. The paper shows experimentally that CAR outperforms both purely short-answer and CoT-based approaches across a range of multimodal and text-based reasoning tasks, achieving a better balance of accuracy and efficiency. CAR demonstrates improvements over strong baselines, especially in scenarios where reasoning is not always necessary or helpful. The paper also explores combining CAR with token reduction methods like TALE, demonstrating synergistic effects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptively selecting reasoning strategies based on model confidence (estimated via perplexity) is reasonably novel. Existing works focus primarily on either always using CoT or on reducing the token cost *within* the CoT reasoning process. CAR addresses a different aspect: *when* to reason at all.  The use of PPL for confidence estimation, while not entirely new, is cleverly applied in this context to drive the adaptive reasoning.  The combination of CAR with existing token reduction methods is a valuable extension.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a real problem: CoT reasoning, while powerful, can be overkill and inefficient for simpler tasks.  CAR's ability to improve both accuracy and efficiency makes it a potentially valuable contribution to the field.  The empirical results are compelling, showing consistent improvements across different datasets and models. The ability to combine CAR with other token reduction techniques further enhances its practical significance. However, the absolute gains on some tasks (especially the complex reasoning ones) are not always dramatic, suggesting that there is still room for improvement.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-motivated problem statement.
    *   Simple and elegant approach that is easy to understand and implement.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on a diverse set of tasks.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in both accuracy and efficiency.
    *   Exploration of combining CAR with existing token reduction methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on PPL as a confidence measure might be a limitation. PPL is known to have its own issues and might not always be a perfect indicator of model certainty.  Exploring other confidence measures could be beneficial.
    *   While the paper shows improvements, the gains on some complex reasoning tasks are not as pronounced.  This suggests that CAR might be more effective for tasks where reasoning is only sometimes needed.
    *   The computational overhead of computing PPL for the short answer before deciding on the reasoning path is a potential concern that needs to be properly addressed.  How significant is this overhead?  Is the gain in efficiency consistently greater than the overhead across various model sizes and hardware configurations? The paper can benefit from detailed analysis of this.
    *   The method for modeling the PPL distributions (Gaussian) could be further explored, there are also other potentially relevant methods. A more sophisticated estimation method that takes into account task specific information might be more appropriate.

*   **Potential Influence:**  CAR has the potential to influence the design of more efficient and adaptable LLM/MLLM reasoning frameworks. It offers a principled approach to address the "one-size-fits-all" problem of CoT reasoning. Further research might explore more sophisticated confidence measures, different adaptive reasoning strategies, and tighter integration with other optimization techniques.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a novel and practical approach to adaptive reasoning in LLMs/MLLMs. While not a groundbreaking theoretical breakthrough, the paper makes a solid engineering contribution by addressing an important practical issue and demonstrating its effectiveness through comprehensive experiments.  The potential for follow-up work and the clear benefits in terms of efficiency and accuracy justify a positive assessment.

Score: 7
