### R&D-Agent-Quant: A Multi-Agent Framework for Data-Centric Factors and Model Joint Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15155v1)
- **Authors**: Yuante Li, Xu Yang, Xiao Yang, Minrui Xu, Xisen Wang, Weiqing Liu, Jiang Bian
- **Abstract**: Financial markets pose fundamental challenges for asset return prediction due to their high dimensionality, non-stationarity, and persistent volatility. Despite advances in large language models and multi-agent systems, current quantitative research pipelines suffer from limited automation, weak interpretability, and fragmented coordination across key components such as factor mining and model innovation. In this paper, we propose R&D-Agent for Quantitative Finance, in short RD-Agent(Q), the first data-centric multi-agent framework designed to automate the full-stack research and development of quantitative strategies via coordinated factor-model co-optimization. RD-Agent(Q) decomposes the quant process into two iterative stages: a Research stage that dynamically sets goal-aligned prompts, formulates hypotheses based on domain priors, and maps them to concrete tasks, and a Development stage that employs a code-generation agent, Co-STEER, to implement task-specific code, which is then executed in real-market backtests. The two stages are connected through a feedback stage that thoroughly evaluates experimental outcomes and informs subsequent iterations, with a multi-armed bandit scheduler for adaptive direction selection. Empirically, RD-Agent(Q) achieves up to 2X higher annualized returns than classical factor libraries using 70% fewer factors, and outperforms state-of-the-art deep time-series models on real markets. Its joint factor-model optimization delivers a strong balance between predictive accuracy and strategy robustness. Our code is available at: https://github.com/microsoft/RD-Agent.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "R&D-Agent-Quant: A Multi-Agent Framework for Data-Centric Factors and Model Joint Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R&D-Agent(Q), a novel multi-agent framework designed to automate the full research and development lifecycle of quantitative trading strategies. It addresses limitations in existing quantitative research pipelines, namely limited automation, poor interpretability, and fragmented optimization. R&D-Agent(Q) decomposes the quant process into Research and Development stages, connected by a feedback loop. The Research stage generates goal-aligned prompts and hypotheses using domain priors, while the Development stage employs a code-generation agent (Co-STEER) to implement task-specific code. Real-market backtests are conducted, and the results inform subsequent iterations through a multi-armed bandit scheduler. Empirical results demonstrate that R&D-Agent(Q) achieves higher annualized returns with fewer factors compared to classical factor libraries, outperforming state-of-the-art deep time-series models. The joint factor-model optimization balances predictive accuracy and strategy robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its holistic approach to automating the quantitative research pipeline using a multi-agent system. While individual components (LLM-based code generation, multi-armed bandits, factor mining, model training) have been explored previously, their integration into a closed-loop, self-improving system is innovative.  The introduction of Co-STEER, a specialized code-generation agent tailored for data-centric tasks, also adds to the novelty. It marks a departure from purely manual or semi-automated approaches.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of R&D-Agent(Q) on quantitative finance is substantial. By automating and streamlining the development process, it could democratize access to advanced quantitative strategies, reduce reliance on expert knowledge, and accelerate the pace of innovation. The increased interpretability due to the framework's structure is also a valuable contribution in a field where black-box models are often met with skepticism. The claims of superior performance on real-world datasets and smaller resource footprints further enhance its significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper presents a well-defined and structured framework with clear separation of concerns.
    *   **Technical Contributions:** The introduction of Co-STEER and the multi-armed bandit scheduler are technically sound. The framework's modular design facilitates future extensions.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are extensive and use real-world datasets, providing strong evidence for the framework's effectiveness. Comparison against relevant baselines strengthens the results.
    *   **Addresses Key Limitations:** The framework tackles critical limitations in existing quantitative research pipelines, enhancing automation, interpretability, and coordinated optimization.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency:** The framework relies heavily on the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The potential for hallucination and biases in LLMs could affect the quality and robustness of generated strategies. More detailed analysis of the sensitivity to different LLM architectures and prompt engineering strategies would be beneficial.
    *   **Generalizability Concerns:**  While the paper evaluates on the CSI 300 dataset, it's important to consider the generalizability of the findings to other markets and asset classes. Financial markets exhibit significant structural differences, and further validation on diverse datasets would be necessary.
    *   **Limited Insights into Strategy Logic:**  The paper focuses primarily on the framework's architecture and performance. A deeper dive into the kinds of trading strategies generated by R&D-Agent(Q), their underlying economic rationales, and their risk characteristics would be valuable.
    *   **Cost Analysis:** While the paper mentions cost efficiency, it could be expanded with a more in-depth cost analysis.

*   **Overall Assessment:**
    The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of quantitative finance by introducing a novel and effective framework for automating strategy development. While limitations exist regarding LLM dependency and generalizability, the strengths of the paper, including its comprehensive design, technical contributions, and empirical validation, outweigh the weaknesses. The study establishes an important basis for future development of intelligent system for quantitative finance.

### Cascaded Diffusion Models for Neural Motion Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15157v1)
- **Authors**: Mohit Sharma, Adam Fishman, Vikash Kumar, Chris Paxton, Oliver Kroemer
- **Abstract**: Robots in the real world need to perceive and move to goals in complex environments without collisions. Avoiding collisions is especially difficult when relying on sensor perception and when goals are among clutter. Diffusion policies and other generative models have shown strong performance in solving local planning problems, but often struggle at avoiding all of the subtle constraint violations that characterize truly challenging global motion planning problems. In this work, we propose an approach for learning global motion planning using diffusion policies, allowing the robot to generate full trajectories through complex scenes and reasoning about multiple obstacles along the path. Our approach uses cascaded hierarchical models which unify global prediction and local refinement together with online plan repair to ensure the trajectories are collision free. Our method outperforms (by ~5%) a wide variety of baselines on challenging tasks in multiple domains including navigation and manipulation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel cascaded diffusion model architecture for robot motion planning from raw sensory data in complex environments. It aims to address the limitations of existing learning-based methods that struggle with long-horizon planning and constraint satisfaction (e.g., collision avoidance). The proposed approach utilizes a hierarchy of diffusion models operating at different temporal resolutions: a high-level model generates coarse plans, and lower-level models refine these plans locally, incorporating sensor data and handling constraints. A plan refinement strategy is also employed to correct constraint violations. The approach is evaluated in 2D navigation and 7D manipulator planning domains, demonstrating improved performance compared to various baselines, including other diffusion-based methods and classical planners, especially in challenging, cluttered environments. A key contribution is the ability to plan directly from raw perceptual input (point clouds or images) without privileged information like full scene geometry.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the hierarchical application of diffusion models to motion planning, coupled with a plan refinement strategy. While diffusion models have been used for motion planning before, this cascaded approach, explicitly designed to address long-horizon and local constraint satisfaction issues, is a distinct contribution. The emphasis on planning *directly* from raw sensor data, avoiding the need for intermediate representations or privileged information, is also valuable.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is considerable. Robot motion planning in complex, unstructured environments is a fundamental problem in robotics. The ability to learn motion plans directly from raw sensor data, bypassing the need for explicit world models or heuristics, could significantly improve the autonomy and adaptability of robots in real-world settings. The demonstrated performance gains over existing methods, particularly in challenging environments, suggests that this approach could lead to more robust and efficient motion planning systems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Hierarchical architecture:** The cascaded approach effectively separates global planning and local refinement, addressing the limitations of single-level diffusion models.
    *   **Raw sensory input:**  Directly planning from raw data reduces reliance on handcrafted features or privileged information.
    *   **Plan refinement:** Addressing the shortcomings of high-level coarse plans by refining and repairing them locally improves reliability.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  Evaluations across multiple domains and comparison against diverse baselines provide strong evidence of the method's effectiveness. The analysis of runtime and success rates paints a balanced picture.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational cost:** Diffusion models are inherently computationally expensive. While the cascaded approach might improve efficiency compared to a single large diffusion model, it still has a larger planning time compared to methods like MπNets. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational complexity and optimization strategies for real-time performance.
    *   **Out-of-distribution generalization:** The paper acknowledges the limitation in completely novel environments. While imitation learning provides some degree of generalization, performance degradation in unseen scenarios is likely. Future work should explore techniques like meta-learning or domain adaptation to improve generalization.
    *   **Reliance on imitation learning:** The approach is based on imitation learning from optimal plans generated by classical planners.  While imitation learning is practical, it is limited by the quality of the expert data and may not discover truly novel or optimal solutions.
    *   **Limited depth of Hierarchy:** Experiments are limited to a hierarchy of 2 models, so it's uncertain if performance continues to improve by adding additional diffusion models.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence research in neural motion planning and diffusion-based robot learning. It provides a compelling demonstration of the benefits of hierarchical modeling and raw sensory input. The method's performance and robustness could encourage further exploration of diffusion models for challenging robotics tasks.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant approach to motion planning. However, its limitations in computational cost and out-of-distribution generalization prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The experimental results are compelling, but the method's practical applicability in real-time robotics applications remains a challenge. Given these considerations, a score of 7 seems appropriate.

### ALN-P3: Unified Language Alignment for Perception, Prediction, and Planning in Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15158v1)
- **Authors**: Yunsheng Ma, Burhaneddin Yaman, Xin Ye, Mahmut Yurt, Jingru Luo, Abhirup Mallik, Ziran Wang, Liu Ren
- **Abstract**: Recent advances have explored integrating large language models (LLMs) into end-to-end autonomous driving systems to enhance generalization and interpretability. However, most existing approaches are limited to either driving performance or vision-language reasoning, making it difficult to achieve both simultaneously. In this paper, we propose ALN-P3, a unified co-distillation framework that introduces cross-modal alignment between "fast" vision-based autonomous driving systems and "slow" language-driven reasoning modules. ALN-P3 incorporates three novel alignment mechanisms: Perception Alignment (P1A), Prediction Alignment (P2A), and Planning Alignment (P3A), which explicitly align visual tokens with corresponding linguistic outputs across the full perception, prediction, and planning stack. All alignment modules are applied only during training and incur no additional costs during inference. Extensive experiments on four challenging benchmarks-nuScenes, Nu-X, TOD3Cap, and nuScenes QA-demonstrate that ALN-P3 significantly improves both driving decisions and language reasoning, achieving state-of-the-art results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ALN-P3, a novel co-distillation framework designed to enhance both the driving performance ("fast" vision-based systems) and language reasoning ("slow" language-driven modules) of autonomous driving systems. ALN-P3 achieves this through three key alignment mechanisms: Perception Alignment (P1A), Prediction Alignment (P2A), and Planning Alignment (P3A). These mechanisms explicitly align visual tokens with corresponding linguistic outputs across the entire perception, prediction, and planning stack. The alignment is done only during training, incurring no additional computational cost during inference, making it practical for real-world deployment. Experiments on nuScenes, Nu-X, TOD3Cap, and nuScenes QA demonstrate that ALN-P3 improves both driving decisions and language reasoning, achieving state-of-the-art results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the unified co-distillation framework that aligns "fast" and "slow" autonomous driving systems across the full perception-prediction-planning stack. While the individual components (e.g., using LLMs, knowledge distillation, alignment losses) are not entirely new, the way they are integrated and applied to the AD stack to improve both driving performance and reasoning is innovative. The idea of separate vision-based and language-based systems is known ("slow and fast systems"). The *explicit* alignment of those is the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation in current autonomous driving systems: the trade-off between real-time performance and interpretability/reasoning. By improving both driving performance (reduced collision rate) and reasoning capabilities (improved CIDEr score on explanation tasks), ALN-P3 contributes significantly to building safer and more understandable autonomous vehicles. The performance boost across multiple datasets is noteworthy.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive approach: The alignment spans the entire P3 stack, unlike some prior works that focus on individual components.
    *   Practical design: The training-only alignment ensures no additional computational burden during inference, making it suitable for real-world deployment.
    *   Strong experimental results: Consistent improvements across diverse datasets and metrics demonstrate the effectiveness of the framework.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on Intermediate Representations: The framework depends on accessing intermediate representations (track, motion, planning tokens) from the "fast" system, which may not be feasible in all AD systems (e.g., black-box systems). The paper acknowledges this in the limitations section.
    *   Category-specific prompt selection:  This aspect of the implementation might limit the generalization ability to more open-ended queries. A more adaptive or dynamic prompt selection mechanism could be explored.
    *   Incremental tuning: The approach still relies on tuning when transferred to new AD architectures and domains. Further research should strive to generalize the architecture for improved adoption.

**Overall:**

The ALN-P3 framework presents a valuable contribution by effectively integrating visual and language reasoning capabilities in autonomous driving systems without sacrificing real-time performance. The explicit alignment across the entire perception, prediction, and planning stack is a significant step towards building more robust and interpretable autonomous vehicles. Despite the limitations regarding intermediate representations and prompt selection, the paper is well-written, the approach is clearly explained, and the experimental results are compelling.

### Lossless Token Merging Even Without Fine-Tuning in Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15160v1)
- **Authors**: Jaeyeon Lee, Dong-Wan Choi
- **Abstract**: Although Vision Transformers (ViTs) have become the standard architecture in computer vision, their massive sizes lead to significant computational overhead. Token compression techniques have attracted considerable attention to address this issue, but they often suffer from severe information loss, requiring extensive additional training to achieve practical performance. In this paper, we propose Adaptive Token Merging (ATM), a novel method that ensures lossless token merging, eliminating the need for fine-tuning while maintaining competitive performance. ATM adaptively reduces tokens across layers and batches by carefully adjusting layer-specific similarity thresholds, thereby preventing the undesirable merging of less similar tokens with respect to each layer. Furthermore, ATM introduces a novel token matching technique that considers not only similarity but also merging sizes, particularly for the final layers, to minimize the information loss incurred from each merging operation. We empirically validate our method across a wide range of pretrained models, demonstrating that ATM not only outperforms all existing training-free methods but also surpasses most training-intensive approaches, even without additional training. Remarkably, training-free ATM achieves over a 30% reduction in FLOPs for the DeiT-T and DeiT-S models without any drop in their original accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Lossless Token Merging Even Without Fine-Tuning in Vision Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adaptive Token Merging (ATM), a novel training-free token compression technique for Vision Transformers (ViTs). ATM aims to reduce the computational cost of ViTs by adaptively merging similar tokens across layers and batches. The key innovations include layer-dependent thresholding (adjusting similarity thresholds based on layer depth to minimize information loss), size-distinctive token matching (considering the accumulated merging size of tokens when merging, particularly in later layers), and batch-adaptive merging (allowing adaptive token compression within a batched inference setting). The authors demonstrate through experiments on various pretrained models that ATM achieves significant FLOPs reduction (over 30% on DeiT-T and DeiT-S) without any accuracy degradation, outperforming existing training-free methods and even surpassing some training-intensive approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a genuinely novel combination of techniques for training-free token merging. While individual concepts like similarity-based merging aren't entirely new, the integration of layer-dependent thresholding, size-distinctive matching, and batch-adaptive merging constitutes a significant advancement. The theoretical justification for size-distinctive matching, while not mathematically profound, provides a practical and insightful approach to minimizing information loss. The layer-dependent thresholding is also an important practical contribution. The combination of techniques creates a highly effective system.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in the ViT landscape: the high computational cost associated with large models. Training-free compression techniques are particularly valuable because they allow for efficient deployment of pretrained models without the need for resource-intensive fine-tuning. Achieving substantial FLOPs reduction without accuracy loss is a highly significant result. Surpassing training-intensive methods in some cases further highlights the practical value of ATM. The potential impact on reducing the energy footprint and deployment cost of ViTs is substantial. Also, the method is easy to implement and can be applied to existing pretrained models, which is a huge advantage.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical results:**  The paper presents extensive experiments on a variety of ViT architectures and datasets, demonstrating the consistent effectiveness of ATM. The ablation studies convincingly validate the contributions of each component.
    *   **Clear and well-motivated approach:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing methods and provides a solid rationale for the design choices in ATM.
    *   **Practicality:**  The training-free nature and ease of implementation make ATM a highly practical solution for reducing the computational cost of ViTs. The batch-adaptive implementation adds to the applicability of the method.
    *   **Theoretical justification:** The derivation of merging error and its relation to merging size, although simple, helps to motivate the size-distinctive matching.
    *   **Visualizations:** The visualizations provide helpful qualitative insights into how ATM adapts to image complexity.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Theoretical depth:** The theoretical analysis is somewhat limited in scope. While insightful, it doesn't delve deeply into the theoretical properties of token merging or provide guarantees on performance. The theorem provided is relatively simple.
    *   **Architecture-specific tuning:** The hyperparameters for layer-dependent thresholding need to be tuned, even though the ATM strategy as a whole is training-free.
    *   **Limited comparisons:** While the paper presents a lot of comparisons, a direct comparison to knowledge distillation could provide even more insights.
    *   **Complexity:** Although the method is well explained, it can appear complex due to the combination of techniques used.

*   **Potential Influence:** ATM has the potential to become a widely adopted technique for compressing ViTs, particularly in resource-constrained environments. It could also inspire further research into adaptive and training-free compression methods. The combination of techniques in ATM creates a highly effective and practical system.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, empirical validation, and practicality of ATM, alongside the relatively minor weaknesses, the paper warrants a high score. While the theoretical contribution isn't groundbreaking, the *engineering innovation* of combining these techniques into a practical and highly effective system warrants a high score.

### Harnessing Caption Detailness for Data-Efficient Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15172v1)
- **Authors**: Xinran Wang, Muxi Diao, Yuanzhi Liu, Chunyu Wang, Kongming Liang, Zhanyu Ma, Jun Guo
- **Abstract**: Training text-to-image (T2I) models with detailed captions can significantly improve their generation quality. Existing methods often rely on simplistic metrics like caption length to represent the detailness of the caption in the T2I training set. In this paper, we propose a new metric to estimate caption detailness based on two aspects: image coverage rate (ICR), which evaluates whether the caption covers all regions/objects in the image, and average object detailness (AOD), which quantifies the detailness of each object's description. Through experiments on the COCO dataset using ShareGPT4V captions, we demonstrate that T2I models trained on high-ICR and -AOD captions achieve superior performance on DPG and other benchmarks. Notably, our metric enables more effective data selection-training on only 20% of full data surpasses both full-dataset training and length-based selection method, improving alignment and reconstruction ability. These findings highlight the critical role of detail-aware metrics over length-based heuristics in caption selection for T2I tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Harnessing Caption Detailness for Data-Efficient Text-to-Image Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of data efficiency in text-to-image (T2I) generation, arguing that simply using longer captions isn't the most effective way to train models to generate detailed images.  The authors propose a new metric, Caption Detailness (CD), which considers two aspects: Image Coverage Rate (ICR), which measures how well the caption covers objects/regions in the image, and Average Object Detailness (AOD), which quantifies the level of detail in the description of each object.  They demonstrate through experiments on the COCO dataset (using ShareGPT4V captions) that T2I models trained on high-ICR and -AOD captions achieve superior performance on the Dense Prompt Graph (DPG) benchmark and other benchmarks.  A key finding is that training on a carefully selected 20% of the dataset, chosen using their CD metric, outperforms training on the entire dataset or using length-based selection. The authors' claim is that detail-aware metrics are significantly better than length-based heuristics for caption selection in T2I tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies primarily in the proposed Caption Detailness metric, which combines Image Coverage Rate (ICR) and Average Object Detailness (AOD).  While the idea of using more detailed captions for T2I training is not entirely new, the formalization of detailness through these two specific components, and the method for calculating them using scene graph parsing, contributes a clear and measurable approach. The crucial differentiation is moving beyond simple caption length as a proxy for detail. However, scene graph parsing and segmentation models are existing technologies. The integration of these existing components to create a new metric tailored for T2I data selection constitutes a moderate level of innovation.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is in its potential to improve the data efficiency of T2I model training. The demonstrated ability to achieve superior performance using only 20% of the data has substantial practical implications, reducing computational costs and enabling training with limited resources.  The focus on detail-aware selection addresses a key limitation of current T2I training pipelines, which often rely on massive datasets with varying caption quality. Moreover, the paper uncovers the complex interaction between ICR, AOD, and semantic correctness, highlighting the fact that very detailed captions may contain noise and thus lead to performance degradation.  This is an important contribution to the field that encourages a more nuanced approach to using MLLMs to generate training data.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The CD metric is well-defined and motivated.
    *   The experimental results are clear and convincing. The paper provides detailed comparison against competitive baselines across multiple relevant metrics.
    *   The analysis of the impact of ICR and AOD on different dimensions of image generation (e.g., object attributes vs. overall scene) provides a more granular understanding of the problem.
    *   The recommendation for a data selection approach that emphasizes combining semantic pruning (ITM scores) with the novel CD metric provides strong practical value to researchers interested in improving training dataset quality and efficiency.
    *   There's thorough ablation analysis to investigate the influence of ITM/ICR/AOD in training T2I models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on Llama3-70B for scene graph parsing, and LISA for segmentation. The quality of the metrics depend on the quality of tools used. The authors mentioned including the design instruction as the scene graph parser, but providing more detail on the specifics of the instructions and how they were designed could strengthen the discussion of the methodology. It would be beneficial to see the robustness of CD across different scene graph parsers, or segmentation models.
    *   The paper uses one base model (Lumina-Next-T2I). While the results are significant, expanding the evaluation to multiple foundation T2I models would increase the generalizability of the approach.
    *   The paper might overstate the "positive correlation" between ICR/AOD and T2I performance, as there are diminishing returns in certain ranges of performance. While the authors acknowledge there might be trade-offs, the discussion could be more nuanced.
    *   The experimental evaluations are primarily focused on COCO dataset. Additional experiments across a more diverse set of datasets would boost confidence in generalizability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on data-efficient T2I training and encourage the development of more sophisticated metrics for caption quality assessment. The use of CD as a data selection criterion could become a standard practice in the field. The finding that semantic correctness and detailness need to be carefully balanced is an important insight that could influence how MLLMs are used for training data augmentation.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated metric for evaluating caption detailness in the context of T2I generation. The experimental results are solid, and the practical implications of the work for data efficiency are significant. However, the paper could be strengthened by providing a more comprehensive evaluation of the CD metric, including robustness analysis across different base models, and scene graph parsers. Additionally, expanding the evaluation to more datasets and providing a more nuanced discussion about the positive correlation of ICR/AOD can raise the paper’s score. Considering these factors, a score of 7.5 reflects the value of the work, while acknowledging the areas where further research is needed.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DUSK: Do Not Unlearn Shared Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15209v1)
- **Authors**: Wonje Jeung, Sangyeon Yoon, Hyesoo Hong, Soeun Kim, Seungju Han, Youngjae Yu, Albert No
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, raising concerns about the unauthorized use of copyrighted or sensitive data. Machine unlearning aims to remove such 'forget' data while preserving utility and information from the 'retain' set. However, existing evaluations typically assume that forget and retain sets are fully disjoint, overlooking realistic scenarios where they share overlapping content. For instance, a news article may need to be unlearned, even though the same event, such as an earthquake in Japan, is also described factually on Wikipedia. Effective unlearning should remove the specific phrasing of the news article while preserving publicly supported facts. In this paper, we introduce DUSK, a benchmark designed to evaluate unlearning methods under realistic data overlap. DUSK constructs document sets that describe the same factual content in different styles, with some shared information appearing across all sets and other content remaining unique to each. When one set is designated for unlearning, an ideal method should remove its unique content while preserving shared facts. We define seven evaluation metrics to assess whether unlearning methods can achieve this selective removal. Our evaluation of nine recent unlearning methods reveals a key limitation: while most can remove surface-level text, they often fail to erase deeper, context-specific knowledge without damaging shared content. We release DUSK as a public benchmark to support the development of more precise and reliable unlearning techniques for real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DUSK, a new benchmark designed to evaluate machine unlearning methods in a more realistic setting where the "forget" and "retain" datasets share overlapping content.  Existing benchmarks often assume these sets are disjoint, which isn't true in many real-world scenarios (e.g., a news article that needs to be unlearned might describe the same event as a Wikipedia entry). DUSK constructs datasets of professor profiles in various narrative styles, with some profiles unique to a specific document (to be forgotten) and others shared across multiple documents (to be retained).  The paper defines seven evaluation metrics to assess the ability of unlearning methods to selectively remove unique content while preserving shared knowledge. The authors evaluated nine existing unlearning methods on DUSK and found that they often struggle to disentangle forget-specific information from retained knowledge, leading to the degradation of shared knowledge and overall utility. DUSK is released as a public benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the realistic evaluation scenario of overlapping forget and retain data. This is a significant departure from prior benchmarks that often make simplifying assumptions.  The construction of the dataset, with its controlled content attribution (unique vs. shared knowledge), is also a strong point.  While synthetic datasets aren't always ideal, the controlled nature allows for precise evaluation, addressing a gap in the current literature. The comprehensive set of seven evaluation metrics also contributes to the novelty by offering a more detailed assessment of unlearning methods.  The explicit emphasis on *preserving* shared knowledge is a welcome shift.

**Significance:**

The paper highlights a crucial limitation of current unlearning methods. The finding that these methods struggle to separate forget-specific knowledge from shared information has significant implications for real-world deployment, where data sources are rarely perfectly disjoint.  The release of DUSK as a public benchmark is a valuable contribution, as it provides a standardized way to evaluate and compare unlearning methods under more realistic conditions. This could drive the development of more robust and reliable unlearning techniques.

**Strengths:**

*   **Realistic Scenario:** Addresses a crucial gap in existing unlearning benchmarks by focusing on overlapping data.
*   **Controlled Dataset:** Synthetic data allows for precise control over content attribution, enabling detailed evaluation.
*   **Comprehensive Metrics:** Seven evaluation metrics provide a holistic assessment of unlearning performance.
*   **Clear Findings:** The paper clearly demonstrates the limitations of current unlearning methods in preserving shared knowledge.
*   **Public Benchmark:** The release of DUSK promotes further research and development in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Data:** While controlled, the synthetic nature of the data may not fully capture the complexities of real-world data. It would be good to see experiments on real-world data.
*   **Limited Model Scale:** The evaluation is performed on Llama-3-8B, a relatively small model. While this is justifiable for resource constraints, the results might not directly translate to larger, more complex models. The scalability of the analysis is unclear.
*   **Lack of Comparison to a Truly 'Ideal' Oracle:** While a 'Retrain' model is included, a more informative baseline would be an "oracle" model that *perfectly* removes only the information from the forget set. It would be a model retrained with the retain set and *all* the shared knowledge that has been successfully removed from the forget set. Such a benchmark would help quantify how far the models are to something close to perfect performance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a significant and overlooked issue in the field of machine unlearning, providing a valuable benchmark and insightful analysis. However, the use of synthetic data and the scale of the models used for experimentation limit the generalizability of the results. While the release of DUSK is a positive step, its long-term impact will depend on its adoption by the research community and its ability to stimulate the development of more effective unlearning methods. Further experimentation on larger models and real-world datasets is crucial.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Deliberation on Priors: Trustworthy Reasoning of Large Language Models on Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15210v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Ma, Ning Qu, Zhitao Gao, Rui Xing, Jun Liu, Hongbin Pei, Jiang Xie, Linyun Song, Pinghui Wang, Jing Tao, Zhou Su
- **Abstract**: Knowledge graph-based retrieval-augmented generation seeks to mitigate hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) caused by insufficient or outdated knowledge. However, existing methods often fail to fully exploit the prior knowledge embedded in knowledge graphs (KGs), particularly their structural information and explicit or implicit constraints. The former can enhance the faithfulness of LLMs' reasoning, while the latter can improve the reliability of response generation. Motivated by these, we propose a trustworthy reasoning framework, termed Deliberation over Priors (DP), which sufficiently utilizes the priors contained in KGs. Specifically, DP adopts a progressive knowledge distillation strategy that integrates structural priors into LLMs through a combination of supervised fine-tuning and Kahneman-Tversky optimization, thereby improving the faithfulness of relation path generation. Furthermore, our framework employs a reasoning-introspection strategy, which guides LLMs to perform refined reasoning verification based on extracted constraint priors, ensuring the reliability of response generation. Extensive experiments on three benchmark datasets demonstrate that DP achieves new state-of-the-art performance, especially a Hit@1 improvement of 13% on the ComplexWebQuestions dataset, and generates highly trustworthy responses. We also conduct various analyses to verify its flexibility and practicality. The code is available at https://github.com/reml-group/Deliberation-on-Priors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Deliberation on Priors: Trustworthy Reasoning of Large Language Models on Knowledge Graphs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) when reasoning over knowledge graphs (KGs). It proposes a framework called Deliberation over Priors (DP) that aims to enhance the trustworthiness of LLM reasoning by explicitly leveraging the structural information and constraints embedded within KGs. DP operates in two stages: offline and online. In the offline stage, it uses a progressive knowledge distillation strategy, combining supervised fine-tuning and Kahneman-Tversky optimization, to instill KG structural priors into the LLM. The online stage employs a reasoning-introspection strategy, where the LLM generates and verifies relation paths against extracted constraint priors, enabling backtracking and refined response generation.  The authors conduct experiments on WebQSP, ComplexWebQuestions (CWQ), and MetaQA, demonstrating that DP achieves state-of-the-art results and generates more trustworthy responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to enhance LLM reasoning over KGs. While retrieval-augmented generation is a well-established area, the specific combination of progressive knowledge distillation, Kahneman-Tversky optimization for structural priors, and the reasoning-introspection strategy using constraint priors provides incremental novelty. The emphasis on explicitly leveraging KG constraints is a key differentiator from other methods. However, the individual components (fine-tuning, KTO, constraint extraction) are not entirely novel themselves, but their integration is. The use of Kahneman-Tversky optimization in this context is interesting but the exact adaptation and effectiveness could benefit from further analysis and ablations.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem – hallucinations in LLMs, particularly in high-stakes domains. Improving the trustworthiness and reliability of LLMs is crucial for their wider adoption. The experimental results suggest that DP can substantially improve performance on KGQA datasets, especially in terms of accuracy (Hit@1). However, the impact might be limited by the reliance on manually predefined constraint types, which could hinder scalability to more diverse KGs and domains without significant engineering effort. Also, the choice of datasets (WebQSP, CWQ, MetaQA) are standard benchmarks, so significant improvements can have a real impact in the community. A rigorous assessment of fairness or bias, a dimension relevant for trustworthy AI, is absent.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined framework with distinct components.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation on multiple datasets.
    *   Significant performance gains compared to baselines.
    *   Demonstration of the framework's flexibility with different LLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on manually defined constraint types limits scalability. The constraint extraction heavily relies on pre-defined constraints, and the paper's analysis doesn't explore how to automatically discover or adapt those constraints based on the Knowledge Graph.
    *   Limited analysis of the Kahneman-Tversky optimization component.
    *   Lack of discussion on computational costs. Although practical considerations are briefly mentioned, a quantitative analysis of the overhead compared to simpler retrieval-augmented approaches is lacking.
    *   Lack of assessment of fairness and bias.
    *   The gains and benefits of the proposed method must be weighed against its complexity.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the KGQA research community and inspire further work on incorporating structural and constraint priors into LLM reasoning. The framework's modularity allows for potential extensions and improvements. Also, given the community's interest in reliable reasoning, the ideas presented here may have practical significance.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper provides a solid contribution to the field with its novel framework for trustworthy LLM reasoning over KGs.  The performance gains are significant, and the modular design allows for flexibility. However, the reliance on manually defined constraints is a limitation that impacts scalability and generality. Furthermore, a deeper analysis and ablation studies of individual components would have strengthened the paper. While the results are promising, a more detailed analysis of the trade-offs, particularly regarding scalability and computational cost, is warranted.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### R-TOFU: Unlearning in Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15214v1)
- **Authors**: Sangyeon Yoon, Wonje Jeung, Albert No
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) embed private or copyrighted information not only in their final answers but also throughout multi-step chain-of-thought (CoT) traces, making reliable unlearning far more demanding than in standard LLMs. We introduce Reasoning-TOFU (R-TOFU), the first benchmark tailored to this setting. R-TOFU augments existing unlearning tasks with realistic CoT annotations and provides step-wise metrics that expose residual knowledge invisible to answer-level checks. Using R-TOFU, we carry out a comprehensive comparison of gradient-based and preference-optimization baselines and show that conventional answer-only objectives leave substantial forget traces in reasoning. We further propose Reasoned IDK, a preference-optimization variant that preserves coherent yet inconclusive reasoning, achieving a stronger balance between forgetting efficacy and model utility than earlier refusal styles. Finally, we identify a failure mode: decoding variants such as ZeroThink and LessThink can still reveal forgotten content despite seemingly successful unlearning, emphasizing the need to evaluate models under diverse decoding settings. Together, the benchmark, analysis, and new baseline establish a systematic foundation for studying and improving unlearning in LRMs while preserving their reasoning capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "R-TOFU," a new benchmark designed to evaluate machine unlearning in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs).  LRMs, unlike standard LLMs, generate explicit reasoning traces, making unlearning more complex as sensitive information can be embedded not just in final answers, but also in the reasoning steps. R-TOFU augments existing unlearning tasks with realistic chain-of-thought (CoT) annotations and provides step-wise metrics to detect residual knowledge in these reasoning traces. The authors compare gradient-based and preference-optimization unlearning baselines and propose a novel preference-optimization method called "Reasoned IDK" that aims for coherent, yet inconclusive reasoning. The paper also identifies a vulnerability where specific decoding strategies (ZeroThink, LessThink) can reveal forgotten content even after seemingly successful unlearning. The benchmark, analysis, and new baseline provide a foundation for studying and improving unlearning in LRMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in recognizing and addressing the unique challenges of unlearning in *reasoning* models.  While unlearning has been extensively studied in standard LLMs, the presence of CoT makes the task significantly different.  The R-TOFU benchmark is a valuable contribution as it is explicitly designed to measure this aspect.  The "Reasoned IDK" approach is also a novel attempt to balance unlearning efficacy and model utility in the CoT setting.  Identifying the vulnerability to "ZeroThink" and "LessThink" decoding strategies is an important observation. However, each of these innovations builds significantly from pre-existing works. R-TOFU utilizes a similar structure to existing TOFU and the proposed methods for unlearning rely largely on PO/GA already well established within the field.
* **Significance:** The work is significant because it highlights a critical gap in the current unlearning research landscape.  As LRMs become more prevalent, the need to reliably remove sensitive information from their reasoning traces becomes paramount.  The paper's findings demonstrate that existing answer-level unlearning methods are insufficient for LRMs. This makes the task of effectively forgetting from LRM a practical necessity and the benchmark provided by the authors will enable further research in this space.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of unlearning in LRMs.
    * **Practical Benchmark:** The R-TOFU benchmark is a useful resource for the research community, providing a concrete way to evaluate unlearning methods for LRMs.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors perform a thorough evaluation of various unlearning methods.
    * **Reasoned IDK:** Reasoned IDK is a sensible approach to mitigate unintended knowledge leakages.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Synthetic Data:** R-TOFU utilizes synthetic CoT traces generated by GPT-4, which could limit its realism and generalizability. It would be beneficial to include real-world CoT data in future versions.
    * **Limited scope:** Experiments are only run on a single model architecture, DeepSeek-R1, making it difficult to know how broadly the results apply.
    * **Incremental contribution to established techniques:**  While identifying specific challenges is new, proposed methods are incremental innovations. The paper primarily builds upon existing techniques such as parameter optimization and preference learning, rather than introducing radically new theoretical frameworks for unlearning.

* **Potential influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of unlearning research, shifting the focus towards reasoning-aware unlearning methods. The R-TOFU benchmark could become a standard evaluation tool in the field. The identified vulnerability in decoding strategies should encourage researchers to consider robustness more broadly.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying a critical limitation of existing unlearning methods when applied to LRMs. The construction of R-TOFU addresses the growing need for LRM privacy and provides a practical way to evaluate unlearning in LRMs. However, much of the method relies on an existing backbone and only introduces incremental improvements. The lack of extensive experiments, limited experimental scope, and use of synthetic data somewhat reduce the impact and generalizability. Despite these limitations, the paper is an important first step in a relatively unexplored area, has the potential to guide future research in unlearning for reasoning models, and the novel benchmark provides a useful tool for future research.
