### Multilingual Prompting for Improving LLM Generation Diversity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15229v1)
- **Authors**: Qihan Wang, Shidong Pan, Tal Linzen, Emily Black
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are known to lack cultural representation and overall diversity in their generations, from expressing opinions to answering factual questions. To mitigate this problem, we propose multilingual prompting: a prompting method which generates several variations of a base prompt with added cultural and linguistic cues from several cultures, generates responses, and then combines the results. Building on evidence that LLMs have language-specific knowledge, multilingual prompting seeks to increase diversity by activating a broader range of cultural knowledge embedded in model training data. Through experiments across multiple models (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, LLaMA 70B, and LLaMA 8B), we show that multilingual prompting consistently outperforms existing diversity-enhancing techniques such as high-temperature sampling, step-by-step recall, and personas prompting. Further analyses show that the benefits of multilingual prompting vary with language resource level and model size, and that aligning the prompting language with the cultural cues reduces hallucination about culturally-specific information.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Multilingual Prompting for Improving LLM Generation Diversity":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces multilingual prompting as a novel technique to enhance diversity in Large Language Model (LLM) generations. Addressing the known limitations of LLMs in cultural representation and overall diversity, the authors propose generating variations of a base prompt with cultural and linguistic cues from different cultures, generating responses, and combining these results. The core idea leverages the language-specific knowledge already embedded within LLMs. Through experiments across several models (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, LLaMA 70B, and LLaMA 8B), the study demonstrates that multilingual prompting consistently outperforms existing diversity-enhancing methods, including high-temperature sampling, step-by-step recall, and persona prompting.  Further analysis explores how the benefits of multilingual prompting vary based on language resource level and model size. The paper finds that using the language associated with specific cultural cues reduces hallucinations regarding culturally-specific information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The central idea of using multilingual prompts is a valuable and non-trivial extension to current prompting techniques. While the concept of cultural prompting exists, the paper distinguishes itself by integrating multiple languages *simultaneously* and demonstrating its superior performance compared to monocultural (English) prompting with cultural cues. The finding that language itself, beyond cultural cues, is a crucial factor in eliciting diversity is significant. The concept of improving factual accurace with respect to specific cultures, by leveraging their corresponding languages is also significant.

**Significance:**  The paper addresses a well-acknowledged and important problem in the LLM field: the lack of diversity in generations. This lack can lead to biased outputs, unfair representation, and the perpetuation of dominant cultural perspectives. By offering a method to mitigate this, the work has the potential to influence how LLMs are used in various applications, from information retrieval and content creation to user studies and opinion surveys. The ability to generate more diverse and culturally sensitive content can contribute to more equitable and representative AI systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The study employs a rigorous experimental design, testing the proposed method across multiple LLMs, datasets, and evaluation metrics. The inclusion of both demographic and social norms tasks strengthens the findings.
*   **Clear Comparisons:**  The paper provides clear comparisons between multilingual prompting and several state-of-the-art diversity-enhancing techniques.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper goes beyond simply demonstrating effectiveness, delving into how performance varies with model size, language resource levels, and the inclusion of cultural cues.
*   **Addressing Hallucination:** The analysis on reducing culturally-relevant hallucinations is a very strong point. It shows that multilingual prompting is more than a diversity booster, it's also a method to increase factual correctness.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Aggregation Strategy:** The paper primarily uses concatenation as an aggregation strategy. While this simplifies diversity measurement, the authors acknowledge that summarization or random selection might be better choices for specific use cases. A more in-depth exploration and comparison of aggregation strategies would strengthen the paper.
*   **Translation as a Potential Source of Error:** The paper acknowledges that translation by GPT models might introduce nuances, but a more thorough error analysis specifically focused on the quality and impact of these translations would be valuable.  Perhaps human translation in a limited setting could be used for comparison.
*   **Limited Language Scope:**  The primary experiments focus on a few languages (English, Chinese, Japanese). While the results are promising, extending the study to include a broader range of languages and cultural contexts would increase generalizability.
*   **Limited Models:** Only GPT models and LLaMA models were tested. A larger collection of models would be beneficial.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to be highly influential because it directly addresses a prominent problem in LLMs and provides a practical, well-supported solution. The method is relatively simple to implement and can be readily integrated into existing LLM-based applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The score reflects a strong, but not revolutionary, contribution. The paper makes a clear, practical improvement to existing LLM capabilities.  It doesn't fundamentally redefine the field, but it provides a valuable technique that can be adopted by researchers and practitioners to create more equitable and diverse AI systems. The clear empirical evaluation and insights provided are strengths, while some aspects of the methodology and language scope leave room for future improvement.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Neural Collapse is Globally Optimal in Deep Regularized ResNets and Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15239v1)
- **Authors**: Peter Súkeník, Christoph H. Lampert, Marco Mondelli
- **Abstract**: The empirical emergence of neural collapse -- a surprising symmetry in the feature representations of the training data in the penultimate layer of deep neural networks -- has spurred a line of theoretical research aimed at its understanding. However, existing work focuses on data-agnostic models or, when data structure is taken into account, it remains limited to multi-layer perceptrons. Our paper fills both these gaps by analyzing modern architectures in a data-aware regime: we prove that global optima of deep regularized transformers and residual networks (ResNets) with LayerNorm trained with cross entropy or mean squared error loss are approximately collapsed, and the approximation gets tighter as the depth grows. More generally, we formally reduce any end-to-end large-depth ResNet or transformer training into an equivalent unconstrained features model, thus justifying its wide use in the literature even beyond data-agnostic settings. Our theoretical results are supported by experiments on computer vision and language datasets showing that, as the depth grows, neural collapse indeed becomes more prominent.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the emergence of neural collapse (NC) in deep regularized ResNets and Transformers. The authors prove that global optima of these architectures, trained with cross-entropy or mean squared error loss, are approximately collapsed, and the approximation gets tighter with increasing depth. They establish a formal reduction from deep ResNet/Transformer training to an equivalent unconstrained features model (UFM), justifying the use of UFM analysis even in data-aware settings. Experimental results on computer vision and language datasets support the theoretical findings, showing that neural collapse becomes more prominent with depth.  The paper extends previous results on neural collapse to modern architectures (ResNets, Transformers) while simultaneously accounting for data structure, a key gap in prior theoretical research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in extending the theoretical understanding of neural collapse to modern deep learning architectures (ResNets and Transformers) trained end-to-end with data-aware regularization. Previous works were primarily limited to MLPs or required strong assumptions. The reduction to the UFM is also a valuable contribution, providing theoretical justification for its use beyond data-agnostic settings. While the UFM is not entirely new, its application in this context and the justification thereof is novel. The paper also introduces several novel results and a precise characterization of feature representation.
*   **Significance:** Neural collapse has emerged as an important topic in understanding the generalization abilities of deep neural networks. By proving NC in realistic architectures, this work strengthens the theoretical foundation for applying NC insights to improve generalization, OOD detection, and transfer learning. The UFM reduction provides a practical tool for analyzing and designing deep networks. There are potential benefits that could arise from these advances to solve complex tasks.
*   **Strengths:** The paper is mathematically rigorous and provides clear theoretical proofs. The empirical results support the theoretical findings, adding credibility to the claims. The reduction to the UFM is a conceptually powerful result. The analysis is comprehensive.
*   **Weaknesses:** While the theoretical analysis is strong, the assumptions made (e.g. Assumption 4.4) and the constraints on the regularization strength potentially limit the practical applicability of the results. The rates of convergence obtained were not high. The assumptions that all training samples and the fact that the labels should be uniquely determined by the context (for transformers) limit its practical applications to certain environments.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a stronger theoretical justification for the UFM and expanding the understanding of neural collapse in modern architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the significant advancements in extending NC theory to ResNets and Transformers, and the introduction of the UFM, a score in the 7 to 9 range is warranted. However, due to the limiting assumptions, rates of convergence and constrains, I assign a 7.5 score, reflecting that while the paper presents important advances, these could be limited in certain implementations.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Plan-Execute Framework for Smart Contract Security Auditing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15242v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Wei, Jing Sun, Zijian Zhang, Zhe Hou, Zixiao Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown great promise in code analysis and auditing; however, they still struggle with hallucinations and limited context-aware reasoning. We introduce SmartAuditFlow, a novel Plan-Execute framework that enhances smart contract security analysis through dynamic audit planning and structured execution. Unlike conventional LLM-based auditing approaches that follow fixed workflows and predefined steps, SmartAuditFlow dynamically generates and refines audit plans based on the unique characteristics of each smart contract. It continuously adjusts its auditing strategy in response to intermediate LLM outputs and newly detected vulnerabilities, ensuring a more adaptive and precise security assessment. The framework then executes these plans step by step, applying a structured reasoning process to enhance vulnerability detection accuracy while minimizing hallucinations and false positives. To further improve audit precision, SmartAuditFlow integrates iterative prompt optimization and external knowledge sources, such as static analysis tools and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). This ensures audit decisions are contextually informed and backed by real-world security knowledge, producing comprehensive security reports. Extensive evaluations across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that SmartAuditFlow outperforms existing methods, achieving 100 percent accuracy on common and critical vulnerabilities, 41.2 percent accuracy for comprehensive coverage of known smart contract weaknesses in real-world projects, and successfully identifying all 13 tested CVEs. These results highlight SmartAuditFlow's scalability, cost-effectiveness, and superior adaptability over traditional static analysis tools and contemporary LLM-based approaches, establishing it as a robust solution for automated smart contract auditing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SmartAuditFlow, a novel Plan-Execute framework leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for automated smart contract security auditing. Unlike traditional LLM-based approaches that follow fixed workflows, SmartAuditFlow dynamically generates and refines audit plans based on the unique characteristics of each smart contract. The framework employs a multi-stage process: (1) initial analysis, (2) adaptive audit planning, (3) multi-faceted execution of sub-tasks with validation, (4) cross-cutting findings synthesis, and (5) comprehensive report generation. SmartAuditFlow integrates prompt optimization, static analysis tools, and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to improve accuracy and reduce hallucinations.  The paper presents empirical evaluations demonstrating SmartAuditFlow's superior performance compared to existing methods, including static analysis tools and standalone LLM applications, on several datasets, and claims a 100% detection rate for common vulnerabilities on a standard benchmark and enhanced performance on real-world contracts and CVEs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a system with several strengths but also faces certain limitations in terms of novelty and significance.

**Strengths:**

*   **Adaptive Planning:**  The core idea of dynamically adjusting audit plans based on the contract's characteristics is a valuable contribution. This contrasts with static approaches and allows for more focused and potentially more effective auditing.
*   **Workflow Integration:**  Structuring the auditing process into distinct stages with well-defined inputs and outputs is a significant advantage. It provides modularity, facilitates debugging, and allows for the easy integration of external tools and knowledge.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is extensive, covering multiple datasets and comparing against a range of baselines (static analysis tools and standalone LLMs). The use of metrics like top-N accuracy and MRR provides a good picture of both detection performance and ranking quality.
*   **External Knowledge Integration:** The use of RAG and static analysis tool integration is a solid approach to mitigate LLM hallucinations and enrich the audit with external information.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The paper heavily relies on known techniques such as plan-execute frameworks (which have been used extensively in agentic AI), prompt engineering, static analysis, and RAG. While the integration is well-executed, the individual components are not particularly novel on their own. The novelty lies more in the specific combination and application within the smart contract auditing domain.
*   **LLM Specificity:**  The claim that the approach improves LLM's capabilities is not fully convincing as different LLMs yield varying results and the prompt optimization may overfit the used LLM. Generalizability across LLMs is a concern.
*   **Limited Detail on Prompt Engineering:** While the paper mentions iterative prompt optimization, the details of the prompt design process, the specific meta-prompts used, and the scoring function are not fully detailed. This makes it difficult to reproduce the results or understand the specific contribution of the prompt engineering component.
*   **Real-world Complexity:** While the paper demonstrates improved performance on the Real-World Contracts Set, a 32.4% top-max accuracy, even with lower false positives, is still low in a practical sense.
*   **Cost Concerns:** The multi-stage nature and reliance on commercial LLM APIs can lead to increased costs, especially for complex contracts. This is a significant practical limitation that needs to be addressed.
*   **Threats to Validity:** A limited static analysis makes it hard to detect vulnerabilities that are only triggered during runtime, or state-dependent. Also, an overreliance of black-box LLM makes the framework to be difficult to explain.

**Significance:**

The paper offers a practical and well-engineered framework for automated smart contract auditing using LLMs. While the individual components are not groundbreaking, the specific combination and application of these techniques in the smart contract domain, along with the thorough evaluation, make a valuable contribution to the field. However, the paper's incremental nature should be acknowledged. The modularity and effectiveness in complex scenarios do signal its potential for further development and broader adoption.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:** The paper offers a competent integration of existing AI techniques to solve a problem in smart contract auditing. While it doesn't introduce radically new AI concepts, it demonstrates a valuable engineering effort and achieves promising results. The strengths in structured workflow, extensive evaluation, and incorporation of external knowledge are weighed against the weaknesses in incremental novelty, high cost of LLM inference and LLM-specific results. Despite these weaknesses, the results show a meaningful improvement and offers a strong foundation that could significantly enhance scalability in auditing. Overall, the SmartAuditFlow is a solid implementation of plan-execute framework that has potential in improving contract-auditing and reducing cost.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Explainable Temporal Reasoning in Large Language Models: A Structure-Aware Generative Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15245v1)
- **Authors**: Zihao Jiang, Ben Liu, Miao Peng, Wenjie Xu, Yao Xiao, Zhenyan Shan, Min Peng
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) show great potential in temporal reasoning, most existing work focuses heavily on enhancing performance, often neglecting the explainable reasoning processes underlying the results. To address this gap, we introduce a comprehensive benchmark covering a wide range of temporal granularities, designed to systematically evaluate LLMs' capabilities in explainable temporal reasoning. Furthermore, our findings reveal that LLMs struggle to deliver convincing explanations when relying solely on textual information. To address challenge, we propose GETER, a novel structure-aware generative framework that integrates Graph structures with text for Explainable TEmporal Reasoning. Specifically, we first leverage temporal knowledge graphs to develop a temporal encoder that captures structural information for the query. Subsequently, we introduce a structure-text prefix adapter to map graph structure features into the text embedding space. Finally, LLMs generate explanation text by seamlessly integrating the soft graph token with instruction-tuning prompt tokens. Experimental results indicate that GETER achieves state-of-the-art performance while also demonstrating its effectiveness as well as strong generalization capabilities. Our dataset and code are available at https://github.com/carryTatum/GETER.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of explainability in temporal reasoning with Large Language Models (LLMs). It introduces a new benchmark, ETR, to evaluate LLMs' ability to not only make accurate temporal predictions but also provide understandable explanations.  The paper identifies that LLMs struggle to produce convincing explanations using only text and proposes GETER, a novel structure-aware generative framework that incorporates temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs) to enhance explainability.  GETER uses a temporal encoder to capture structural information from TKGs, a structure-text prefix adapter to map graph features into the LLM's text space, and instruction-tuning to generate explanations that integrate graph information and text.  Experimental results show that GETER achieves state-of-the-art performance on the ETR benchmark, demonstrating improved accuracy and more convincing explanations.  Ablation studies validate the contributions of individual components of GETER.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by focusing on explainability in temporal reasoning with LLMs, an area that has been relatively under-explored compared to performance enhancement alone. The ETR benchmark is a significant contribution, providing a standardized way to evaluate both prediction accuracy and explanation quality. The GETER framework itself represents a novel architecture that effectively combines structured knowledge (TKGs) with LLMs. However, the core components of GETER (GNNs, adapters, instruction tuning) are established techniques, the *combination* and *application* to explainable temporal reasoning constitute the primary novelty.

*   **Significance:**  The work has the potential to significantly impact the field by encouraging a greater focus on explainability and trust in LLMs used for temporal reasoning.  This is crucial for real-world applications where understanding the reasoning process behind predictions is essential. The ETR benchmark will be a valuable resource for the community, fostering further research in this area. The improvements achieved by GETER demonstrate the value of integrating structured knowledge graphs with LLMs for explainable reasoning. The inclusion of negative and neutral sampling is strong and important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defines the problem and motivates the need for explainable temporal reasoning.
    *   Introduces a comprehensive and well-designed benchmark dataset (ETR).
    *   Proposes a novel structure-aware generative framework (GETER) that leverages TKGs effectively.
    *   Provides thorough experimental results demonstrating the superiority of GETER compared to various baselines.
    *   Includes ablation studies to validate the contribution of each component.
    *   Performs detailed analysis and a case study to provide further insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the architectural components are combined in a novel way, the individual techniques used in GETER (GNNs, adapters, LORA, instruction tuning) are well-established. The specific *implementation* and *tuning* for temporal reasoning contribute to the novelty, but the components themselves are not groundbreaking.
    *   The reliance on GPT-40 for explanation synthesis, while justified, introduces a degree of potential bias and might limit the reproducibility of the ETR dataset over the long term if GPT-40 changes.
    *   The computational cost associated with fine-tuning large LLMs remains a barrier to wider adoption and exploration of these methods. The paper acknowledges this but doesn't offer a substantial solution to reduce this overhead beyond using LoRA.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the types of reasoning errors that GETER is able to correct compared to textual-only approaches. A more granular analysis of failure cases would provide a clearer understanding of the benefits of the structural information.
    *   The evaluation metrics, while standard, focus primarily on semantic similarity between generated and reference explanations. While helpful, they don't directly assess the *plausibility* or *trustworthiness* of the explanations to human users. User studies could strengthen the evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in explainable AI and temporal reasoning. The ETR benchmark will become a common point of reference. The GETER framework provides a promising approach for integrating structured knowledge with LLMs to improve explainability, inspiring further exploration of similar techniques.

*Score: 7.5*

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of explainable temporal reasoning with LLMs. The ETR benchmark is a valuable resource, and the GETER framework demonstrates a promising approach for improving both accuracy and explainability. The experimental results are thorough and convincing. However, the incremental nature of the technical contributions (using established techniques within a new application) and the reliance on expensive LLM fine-tuning limit the paper's overall impact. Further improvements, especially methods to reduce computational cost and a thorough study evaluating the human perception of generated explanations, would increase the significance of this work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MentalMAC: Enhancing Large Language Models for Detecting Mental Manipulation via Multi-Task Anti-Curriculum Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15255v1)
- **Authors**: Yuansheng Gao, Han Bao, Tong Zhang, Bin Li, Zonghui Wang, Wenzhi Chen
- **Abstract**: Mental manipulation is a subtle yet pervasive form of psychological abuse that poses serious threats to mental health. Its covert nature and the complexity of manipulation strategies make it challenging to detect, even for state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs). This concealment also hinders the manual collection of large-scale, high-quality annotations essential for training effective models. Although recent efforts have sought to improve LLM's performance on this task, progress remains limited due to the scarcity of real-world annotated datasets. To address these challenges, we propose MentalMAC, a multi-task anti-curriculum distillation method that enhances LLMs' ability to detect mental manipulation in multi-turn dialogue. Our approach includes: (i) EvoSA, an unsupervised data expansion method based on evolutionary operations and speech act theory; (ii) teacher-model-generated multi-task supervision; and (iii) progressive knowledge distillation from complex to simpler tasks. We then constructed the ReaMent dataset with 5,000 real-world dialogue samples, using a MentalMAC-distilled model to assist human annotation. Vast experiments demonstrate that our method significantly narrows the gap between student and teacher models and outperforms competitive LLMs across key evaluation metrics. All code, datasets, and checkpoints will be released upon paper acceptance. Warning: This paper contains content that may be offensive to readers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MENTALMAC: Enhancing Large Language Models for Detecting Mental Manipulation via Multi-Task Anti-Curriculum Distillation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MENTALMAC, a novel approach to improve the ability of large language models (LLMs) to detect mental manipulation in multi-turn dialogues. The method tackles the challenges of limited data and the subtle nature of manipulation through three key components: (1) EvoSA, an unsupervised data augmentation technique based on evolutionary operations and speech act theory; (2) multi-task learning using teacher-model-generated supervision (rationales for both correct and incorrect answers with feedback); and (3) anti-curriculum distillation, where the model learns progressively from complex to simpler tasks. They also introduce REAMENT, a new dataset of 5,000 real-world dialogues designed for this task. The paper demonstrates that MENTALMAC significantly improves performance compared to SOTA LLMs and narrows the gap between student and teacher models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects.

    *   **MENTALMAC Approach:** The multi-task anti-curriculum distillation strategy is a significant contribution. While knowledge distillation and curriculum/anti-curriculum learning have been used before, the specific application to mental manipulation detection, along with the rationale-based feedback, is novel.
    *   **EvoSA:** The unsupervised data augmentation technique based on evolutionary operations and speech act theory seems like a promising way to make datasets.
    *   **REAMENT Dataset:** The creation of a new, real-world dialogue dataset addresses a key limitation in the field and is, in itself, a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The detection of mental manipulation is an important task with real-world implications for mental health, online safety, and interpersonal relationships. Improving the performance of LLMs on this task can have significant positive impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The paper addresses several challenges simultaneously (data scarcity, subtle cues, and complex reasoning) with a well-integrated approach.
    *   **Empirical results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of MENTALMAC, with significant improvements over SOTA LLMs. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Real-world data:** The REAMENT dataset is a significant contribution, providing a more realistic and challenging benchmark for evaluating models.
    *   The paper attempts to address the ethical considerations by offering psychological support to annotators, ensuring the data doesn't reveal any private information, and being careful when selecting data so that it isn't disturbing to the researchers.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The MENTALMAC approach is quite complex, involving several components and training stages. This makes it more difficult to implement and may limit its adoption. It can also be harder to pinpoint where any performance improvement is actually coming from.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** While the REAMENT dataset is a positive step, any real-world dataset is susceptible to biases, especially given the sensitive nature of the task. Further analysis of potential biases would strengthen the work.
    *   **Ethical Concerns:** Detection of mental manipulation is a task that might have privacy issues. The work might be improved by elaborating on these ethical issues.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** the study has limited generalization and is limited to English speakers.
    *   **Lack of detail on the LLMs:** While LLMs are used the details on each is limited.

*   **Impact:**  The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of harmful content detection and NLP. The MENTALMAC approach and the REAMENT dataset provide valuable resources for researchers working on detecting mental manipulation and related tasks. The paper will likely stimulate further research in this area.

**Justification:**

The paper addresses a relevant and challenging problem with a novel and well-executed approach. The experimental results are convincing, and the contributions (MENTALMAC, EvoSA, and REAMENT) are valuable for the research community. While the complexity of the approach and potential for bias are limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses.

### When Less Language is More: Language-Reasoning Disentanglement Makes LLMs Better Multilingual Reasoners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15257v1)
- **Authors**: Weixiang Zhao, Jiahe Guo, Yang Deng, Tongtong Wu, Wenxuan Zhang, Yulin Hu, Xingyu Sui, Yanyan Zhao, Wanxiang Che, Bing Qin, Tat-Seng Chua, Ting Liu
- **Abstract**: Multilingual reasoning remains a significant challenge for large language models (LLMs), with performance disproportionately favoring high-resource languages. Drawing inspiration from cognitive neuroscience, which suggests that human reasoning functions largely independently of language processing, we hypothesize that LLMs similarly encode reasoning and language as separable components that can be disentangled to enhance multilingual reasoning. To evaluate this, we perform a causal intervention by ablating language-specific representations at inference time. Experiments on 10 open-source LLMs spanning 11 typologically diverse languages show that this language-specific ablation consistently boosts multilingual reasoning performance. Layer-wise analyses further confirm that language and reasoning representations can be effectively decoupled throughout the model, yielding improved multilingual reasoning capabilities, while preserving top-layer language features remains essential for maintaining linguistic fidelity. Compared to post-training such as supervised fine-tuning or reinforcement learning, our training-free ablation achieves comparable or superior results with minimal computational overhead. These findings shed light on the internal mechanisms underlying multilingual reasoning in LLMs and suggest a lightweight and interpretable strategy for improving cross-lingual generalization.
- **Summary**: The paper "When Less Language is More: Language-Reasoning Disentanglement Makes LLMs Better Multilingual Reasoners" investigates the interplay between language and reasoning in large language models (LLMs), particularly focusing on multilingual reasoning capabilities. The authors hypothesize and empirically demonstrate that explicitly disentangling language-specific representations from underlying reasoning processes can significantly improve multilingual reasoning performance. They achieve this through a novel causal intervention: ablating language-specific components from the hidden states of LLMs during inference. Experiments across 10 open-source LLMs and 11 typologically diverse languages on benchmarks like MGSM, XWinograd, and M-MMLU consistently show performance gains after ablation. The paper also includes layer-wise analysis, comparison with post-training methods, and insights into the correlation between language-specific activation intensity and reasoning accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of disentangling language and reasoning in LLMs is not entirely new. However, the paper presents a novel causal intervention method—specifically ablating language-specific components identified via SVD within the hidden states—to achieve this disentanglement *during inference*. This is a lightweight, training-free approach that distinguishes it from existing methods that rely on extensive fine-tuning or dataset construction. The *method* is relatively novel.
*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a critical challenge in LLMs: the unequal performance across languages, with high-resource languages often dominating. Demonstrating a simple and effective method to improve multilingual reasoning, especially in low-resource languages, is a significant contribution. The consistent performance improvements across a wide range of models and tasks suggest the broad applicability of the proposed approach. More generally, the paper is valuable since it is probing into the internal mechanisms within LLMs that allow for multilingual reasoning. The claim that language-specific information may disrupt or hinder reasoning tasks is provocative and challenges current training paradigms that often assume more information and data always leads to improvements.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method is elegant and easily implementable. The projection-based ablation is computationally efficient and doesn't require retraining.
    *   The empirical evaluation is comprehensive. The experiments cover a diverse set of LLMs, languages, and reasoning benchmarks.
    *   The analysis is thorough. The paper provides layer-wise analysis, ablation strength analysis, and comparison with post-training techniques, offering valuable insights into the inner workings of LLMs.
    *   The results are convincing. The consistent performance gains across different settings provide strong support for the authors' hypothesis.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on Google Translate API for SFT and RL experiments introduces a potential source of noise and inconsistency. While necessary given the constraints, it weakens the comparison with the ablation method.
    *   The "anchor" language phenomenon (convergence toward English) warrants further investigation. While discussed briefly, a deeper exploration of the underlying causes and implications would strengthen the paper.
    *   While the ablation method is training-free, the identification of language-specific subspaces requires some initial computation, which is not fully explored in terms of its resource demands in different settings. A discussion of this overhead is needed.
    *   The paper does not fully address how the optimal "middle" layer for intervention should be determined. More specific advice would be useful.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field in several ways:
    *   It provides a practical technique for improving multilingual reasoning in LLMs without extensive retraining.
    *   It highlights the importance of disentangling language and reasoning in LLMs, suggesting a new direction for model development.
    *   It encourages further research into the internal mechanisms of LLMs and how they handle multilingual information.

Overall, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating a simple yet effective method for improving multilingual reasoning in LLMs. The comprehensive empirical evaluation and thorough analysis provide strong support for the authors' hypothesis. While some limitations exist, the paper's novelty, significance, and potential influence justify a solid score.

### ReGUIDE: Data Efficient GUI Grounding via Spatial Reasoning and Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15259v1)
- **Authors**: Hyunseok Lee, Jeonghoon Kim, Beomjun Kim, Jihoon Tack, Chansong Jo, Jaehong Lee, Cheonbok Park, Sookyo In, Jinwoo Shin, Kang Min Yoo
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have enabled autonomous agents to interact with computers via Graphical User Interfaces (GUIs), where accurately localizing the coordinates of interface elements (e.g., buttons) is often required for fine-grained actions. However, this remains significantly challenging, leading prior works to rely on large-scale web datasets to improve the grounding accuracy. In this work, we propose Reasoning Graphical User Interface Grounding for Data Efficiency (ReGUIDE), a novel and effective framework for web grounding that enables MLLMs to learn data efficiently through self-generated reasoning and spatial-aware criticism. More specifically, ReGUIDE learns to (i) self-generate a language reasoning process for the localization via online reinforcement learning, and (ii) criticize the prediction using spatial priors that enforce equivariance under input transformations. At inference time, ReGUIDE further boosts performance through a test-time scaling strategy, which combines spatial search with coordinate aggregation. Our experiments demonstrate that ReGUIDE significantly advances web grounding performance across multiple benchmarks, outperforming baselines with substantially fewer training data points (e.g., only 0.2% samples compared to the best open-sourced baselines).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ReGUIDE: Data Efficient GUI Grounding via Spatial Reasoning and Search" addresses the challenge of accurately localizing interface elements (like buttons) in Graphical User Interfaces (GUIs) for web agents powered by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The authors propose a novel framework called Reasoning Graphical User Interface Grounding for Data Efficiency (ReGUIDE). ReGUIDE enables MLLMs to learn data efficiently through two key mechanisms: (1) self-generation of language reasoning for localization using reinforcement learning, and (2) spatial-aware criticism of predictions based on spatial priors that enforce equivariance under input transformations. At inference time, ReGUIDE enhances performance using a test-time scaling strategy combining spatial search with coordinate aggregation. The experiments demonstrate ReGUIDE's superiority in web grounding across multiple benchmarks, achieving state-of-the-art performance with significantly fewer training data points.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to GUI grounding. The combination of self-generated reasoning via RL with spatial-aware criticism and test-time scaling represents a distinct methodology.  While individual components (RL for reasoning, spatial priors) have been used before, their specific integration within the ReGUIDE framework distinguishes this work.  The emphasis on data efficiency is also a valuable contribution.

**Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to reduce the data dependency of GUI grounding models.  Current SOTA models typically require massive datasets of synthesized or real-world GUI screenshots, posing scalability challenges.  ReGUIDE, by achieving comparable or better performance with substantially less data, offers a more practical and scalable solution.  The improvement in agentic tasks demonstrated further highlights the practical relevance of the improved grounding accuracy. However, its real-world agent applicability needs more validation as it's evaluated on offline datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   **Data Efficiency:** ReGUIDE's ability to achieve SOTA results with only 0.2% of the data used by some baselines is a major strength.
*   **Methodological Innovation:** The integration of RL, spatial priors, and test-time scaling is a well-designed and effective strategy.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides compelling experimental evidence demonstrating ReGUIDE's superiority across various benchmarks and settings.
*   **Improved Agent Performance:** Showing that the better GUI grounding performance translates to improved agentic task completion is crucial and adds real-world value to the research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Specific Model:** The paper predominantly uses Qwen-2.5-VL as the base model. While this choice provides a controlled comparison, it limits the generalizability of the findings. Showing the effectiveness of ReGUIDE with other MLLMs would strengthen the paper.
*   **Limited Ablation Study Details:** While the paper presents an ablation study, further insights into the specific impact of each component (e.g., varying the strength of spatial priors) could be explored. The ablation is presented in very high-level terms (Reason/Consistency/Search).
*   **Offline Evaluation:** The experiments are conducted primarily on offline datasets. Demonstrating ReGUIDE's performance in a real-world, interactive web agent setting would be a more compelling validation of its effectiveness.
*   **Computational Cost:** The test-time scaling strategy, while effective, increases computational cost. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the trade-off between performance and computational resources.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **7** is assigned. ReGUIDE presents a genuinely innovative approach with strong potential for impact in GUI grounding and web agents. Its data efficiency is a significant advantage. The experiments provide solid support for its effectiveness. However, the limitations in model generalizability, ablation details, offline evaluation, and computational cost prevent a higher score. Its influence hinges on how well its approach can transfer to other large multimodal models.

**Score: 7**

### Blind Spot Navigation: Evolutionary Discovery of Sensitive Semantic Concepts for LVLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15265v1)
- **Authors**: Zihao Pan, Yu Tong, Weibin Wu, Jingyi Wang, Lifeng Chen, Zhe Zhao, Jiajia Wei, Yitong Qiao, Zibin Zheng
- **Abstract**: Adversarial attacks aim to generate malicious inputs that mislead deep models, but beyond causing model failure, they cannot provide certain interpretable information such as ``\textit{What content in inputs make models more likely to fail?}'' However, this information is crucial for researchers to specifically improve model robustness. Recent research suggests that models may be particularly sensitive to certain semantics in visual inputs (such as ``wet,'' ``foggy''), making them prone to errors. Inspired by this, in this paper we conducted the first exploration on large vision-language models (LVLMs) and found that LVLMs indeed are susceptible to hallucinations and various errors when facing specific semantic concepts in images. To efficiently search for these sensitive concepts, we integrated large language models (LLMs) and text-to-image (T2I) models to propose a novel semantic evolution framework. Randomly initialized semantic concepts undergo LLM-based crossover and mutation operations to form image descriptions, which are then converted by T2I models into visual inputs for LVLMs. The task-specific performance of LVLMs on each input is quantified as fitness scores for the involved semantics and serves as reward signals to further guide LLMs in exploring concepts that induce LVLMs. Extensive experiments on seven mainstream LVLMs and two multimodal tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our method. Additionally, we provide interesting findings about the sensitive semantics of LVLMs, aiming to inspire further in-depth research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Blind Spot Navigation: Evolutionary Discovery of Sensitive Semantic Concepts for LVLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of vulnerability in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) to adversarial attacks. Unlike traditional adversarial attacks that focus on imperceptible perturbations, this work explores *semantic* vulnerabilities – specific concepts or content in images that make LVLMs more prone to errors or hallucinations.  The core contribution is a novel evolutionary algorithm framework that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) and Text-to-Image (T2I) models to automatically discover these sensitive semantic concepts. The LLM generates image descriptions that evolve over iterations based on feedback (fitness scores) from the LVLM's performance on related multimodal tasks (image captioning and VQA). The evolved descriptions are converted into images via T2I models, and these images are used to probe the LVLMs, ultimately uncovering "blind spots" or semantic concepts that induce failures. The paper presents experimental results across several mainstream LVLMs and demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed method in finding these sensitive semantic regions. It also shows the transferability of discovered sensitive concepts across different LVLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to discovering vulnerabilities in LVLMs. It moves beyond traditional adversarial attacks that rely on imperceptible perturbations to focus on semantic concepts. This shift is significant, as it provides more interpretable insights into model weaknesses. The use of an LLM-driven evolutionary algorithm combined with T2I models is a creative and original method for exploring the vast semantic space and discovering vulnerabilities.

*   **Significance:** The discovery of sensitive semantic concepts has the potential to improve LVLM robustness in a targeted way.  By understanding which concepts are problematic, developers can focus on improving the model's understanding and handling of those specific semantics. The work has implications for the security and reliability of LVLMs, especially as they are deployed in real-world applications where failures could have serious consequences. Furthermore, the transferability of sensitive semantics across different LVLMs is an interesting and potentially useful finding.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Methodological Innovation:** The evolutionary algorithm leveraging LLMs and T2I models is well-designed.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results on multiple LVLMs and across various tasks. The comparative results against existing methods (transferred from classification) clearly demonstrate the advantage of the new approach.
    *   **Interpretability:** By focusing on semantic concepts rather than pixel-level perturbations, the paper generates insights that are easier to understand and act upon.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the method, experiments, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The evolutionary algorithm relies on multiple calls to LLMs and T2I models, leading to significant computational cost and potentially limiting its scalability. The paper does acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** The results may be influenced by biases present in the LLMs and T2I models used within the framework. The discovered sensitive semantics might reflect limitations of these models rather than inherent weaknesses of the target LVLMs. While the use of multiple LVLMs to find generalizable failures mitigates this concern, it doesn't eliminate it.
    *   **Scope:**  While the paper explores image captioning and VQA, the vulnerability of LVLMs extends to many different applications. It would be insightful to understand if the findings generalize to other multimodal tasks.
    *   **Lack of Defense Mechanism:** The paper focuses on finding semantic failures in LVLMs and does not provide any defense mechanisms or mitigation strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in adversarial attacks and robustness for LVLMs. It highlights the importance of considering semantic vulnerabilities and provides a practical method for discovering them. The findings could also lead to the development of new training techniques and defense mechanisms for LVLMs.

**Justification:**

While the paper has some limitations regarding computational cost and the potential for bias inherited from the underlying LLMs and T2I models, it offers a significant contribution in terms of the novel problem definition and its well-validated method. Shifting the adversarial attack perspective from imperceptible perturbations to semantic concepts is both creative and practically valuable. The results highlight the importance of thoroughly investigating the internal "blind spots" of these complex models. The limitations are adequately acknowledged, and the results are robustly presented. The thorough experimental evaluation helps to increase our confidence in the method’s success. The clear communication and insightful results lead to the conclusion that this paper is likely to be a strong and impactful contribution to the field.

### LiveVLM: Efficient Online Video Understanding via Streaming-Oriented KV Cache and Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15269v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Ning, Guangda Liu, Qihao Jin, Wenchao Ding, Minyi Guo, Jieru Zhao
- **Abstract**: Recent developments in Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) have enabled models to process long video sequences and demonstrate remarkable performance. Nonetheless, studies predominantly focus on offline video question answering, neglecting memory usage and response speed that are essential in various real-world applications, such as Deepseek services, autonomous driving, and robotics. To mitigate these challenges, we propose $\textbf{LiveVLM}$, a training-free framework specifically designed for streaming, online video understanding and real-time interaction. Unlike existing works that process videos only after one question is posed, LiveVLM constructs an innovative streaming-oriented KV cache to process video streams in real-time, retain long-term video details and eliminate redundant KVs, ensuring prompt responses to user queries. For continuous video streams, LiveVLM generates and compresses video key-value tensors (video KVs) to reserve visual information while improving memory efficiency. Furthermore, when a new question is proposed, LiveVLM incorporates an online question-answering process that efficiently fetches both short-term and long-term visual information, while minimizing interference from redundant context. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LiveVLM enables the foundation LLaVA-OneVision model to process 44$\times$ number of frames on the same device, and achieves up to 5$\times$ speedup in response speed compared with SoTA online methods at an input of 256 frames, while maintaining the same or better model performance.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LiveVLM: Efficient Online Video Understanding via Streaming-Oriented KV Cache and Retrieval" based on the OCR'd text.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LiveVLM, a training-free framework designed for efficient online video understanding and real-time interaction using Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs). Unlike existing methods that process video offline or struggle with memory usage and response speed in online settings, LiveVLM constructs a streaming-oriented KV cache. This cache processes video streams in real-time, retains long-term details, and removes redundant KVs to ensure fast responses to user queries. The framework comprises: (1) continuous encoding and compression of video streams into KVs, (2) a video-specific KV compression technique focusing on KVs to minimize information loss, and (3) an online question-answering process that efficiently retrieves both short-term and long-term visual information. Experiments demonstrate LiveVLM's ability to process more frames on the same device and achieve significant speedups in response time compared to state-of-the-art online methods while maintaining comparable performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques to achieve efficient online video understanding in a training-free manner. While individual components like KV caching and attention mechanisms are not new, the architecture and strategy designed for continuous video streams, specifically the video-specific KV compression method and the online question-answering process, appear to be novel. The focus on compressing KVs rather than visual features is a good distinction.  The introduction of the streaming-oriented KV cache tailored for real-time interaction is well-motivated. The relative positioning embedding used to retain temporal information in long videos is also a plus.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper addresses a practical and important problem: deploying Video LLMs in real-world streaming applications. The focus on memory usage, response speed, and real-time interaction has a high degree of practical value. If the claims hold up to scrutiny (and are verified by other researchers replicating the claims), LiveVLM could be highly impactful for applications like robotics, autonomous driving, and Deepseek services. The provided experimental results, demonstrating improvements in frame processing and response speed, suggest that LiveVLM has the potential to advance the field of online video understanding and could potentially shift the focus of researchers in this domain.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies and motivates the challenges of online video understanding using Video LLMs.
    *   **Technical Solution:** The LiveVLM framework is technically sound, combining existing techniques (KV caching, attention) with novel components (video-specific KV compression, online question answering).
    *   **Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods, particularly in memory usage and response speed.
    *   **Training-free:** The fact that this is a training-free method makes it easier to adapt to other models and is computationally more efficient.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Foundation Model:** While the choice of LLaVA-OneVision-Qwen2-7B is acceptable as a starting point, it might be good to test the performance on other, more powerful models.
    *   **Incremental contributions:** The contributions can be described as largely incremental (making an existing method more efficient). The originality is primarily in the combination of techniques rather than a completely disruptive new idea.
    *   **Benchmark Details:** The description of the StreamingBench benchmark is limited. It would be useful to have a detailed description of the types of questions, data, and evaluation metrics involved. More details on the experimental setup would improve confidence in results.
    *   **Ablation studies:** While it mentions conducting ablation studies, the presentation could be made clearer. Additionally, it will be useful to add additional ablation studies that study different configurations.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    If LiveVLM proves to be robust and generalizable, it could have a significant influence on the field, inspiring further research into efficient online video understanding techniques and facilitating the deployment of Video LLMs in real-world applications.

**Justification for Score:**

While LiveVLM represents a solid engineering contribution with significant practical implications, it is not a revolutionary breakthrough in terms of core concepts. The novelty primarily lies in the clever combination and adaptation of existing techniques to a specific problem domain. The experimental results are encouraging, demonstrating tangible improvements in efficiency. However, a higher score would require more substantial theoretical advancements or a disruptive new approach.

### Scaling Diffusion Transformers Efficiently via $μ$P
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15270v1)
- **Authors**: Chenyu Zheng, Xinyu Zhang, Rongzhen Wang, Wei Huang, Zhi Tian, Weilin Huang, Jun Zhu, Chongxuan Li
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Transformers have emerged as the foundation for vision generative models, but their scalability is limited by the high cost of hyperparameter (HP) tuning at large scales. Recently, Maximal Update Parametrization ($\mu$P) was proposed for vanilla Transformers, which enables stable HP transfer from small to large language models, and dramatically reduces tuning costs. However, it remains unclear whether $\mu$P of vanilla Transformers extends to diffusion Transformers, which differ architecturally and objectively. In this work, we generalize standard $\mu$P to diffusion Transformers and validate its effectiveness through large-scale experiments. First, we rigorously prove that $\mu$P of mainstream diffusion Transformers, including DiT, U-ViT, PixArt-$\alpha$, and MMDiT, aligns with that of the vanilla Transformer, enabling the direct application of existing $\mu$P methodologies. Leveraging this result, we systematically demonstrate that DiT-$\mu$P enjoys robust HP transferability. Notably, DiT-XL-2-$\mu$P with transferred learning rate achieves 2.9 times faster convergence than the original DiT-XL-2. Finally, we validate the effectiveness of $\mu$P on text-to-image generation by scaling PixArt-$\alpha$ from 0.04B to 0.61B and MMDiT from 0.18B to 18B. In both cases, models under $\mu$P outperform their respective baselines while requiring small tuning cost, only 5.5% of one training run for PixArt-$\alpha$ and 3% of consumption by human experts for MMDiT-18B. These results establish $\mu$P as a principled and efficient framework for scaling diffusion Transformers.
- **Summary**: This paper addresses the challenge of scaling diffusion transformers by applying Maximal Update Parameterization (µP). The authors generalize standard µP to diffusion transformers (DiT, U-ViT, PixArt-a, MMDIT) and demonstrate its effectiveness in large-scale experiments. They prove that µP aligns with vanilla transformers, enabling direct application of existing µP methodologies. Empirically, they show that DiT-µP exhibits robust HP transferability and faster convergence than the original DiT. Furthermore, they validate the efficiency of µP on text-to-image generation tasks by scaling PixArt-a and MMDiT, showing improved performance with small tuning costs. The core claim is that µP provides a principled and efficient framework for scaling diffusion transformers.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in the application and adaptation of µP to diffusion transformers. While µP is established for vanilla transformers, extending it to the distinct architecture and objective of diffusion models is a non-trivial contribution.  The rigorous proof demonstrating the alignment of µP between these architectures strengthens the theoretical grounding. The experimental validation, especially the scaling of PixArt-a to 0.61B and MMDiT to 18B, provides practical evidence of µP's benefits. The fact that prior work may have implicitly assumed HP transferability, but lacked theoretical or empirical validation on the large scale tested here, makes this work impactful.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to reduce the cost of hyperparameter tuning for large-scale diffusion models. HP tuning is a major bottleneck, and the ability to transfer HPs from small proxy models to large models efficiently can accelerate research and development in this area. Demonstrating a 2.9x faster convergence for DiT-XL-2 and low tuning cost for scaling PixArt-a and MMDiT showcases the practical benefits. Furthermore, the reduction in the human effort necessary to scale up large generative models, as indicated by the reduced expert cost, emphasizes the significance. The rigorous validation of the proposed framework makes the findings more trustworthy and facilitates broader adoption.

*   **Strengths:** The paper presents a clear and well-structured argument. The theoretical proof is rigorous and strengthens the foundation for the empirical results. The experimental setup is thorough, and the results are compelling. The comparisons to baseline models are fair, and the ablation studies (analyzing HP transferability) provide valuable insights. The results from scaling both image generation (DiT, ImageNet) and text-to-image generation (PixArt-a, MMDiT) tasks are impactful.

*   **Weaknesses:** The paper mainly focuses on scaling model size and demonstrates results via transfer of HPs, with some experimentation done in transferring different dataset configurations.  The theoretical grounding and demonstrations primarily target *architecture* scale (parameter number). It would be beneficial to have a more systematic study or discussion of the limitations of µP in the context of *data scale*. Are there scenarios where µP transfer breaks down as the dataset size increases? It also assumes base configurations (including architecture) are already sufficiently good, and mainly focuses on HP selection.

*   **Potential influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the way large-scale diffusion transformers are trained and scaled. By providing a principled framework for HP tuning, it can reduce costs, accelerate research, and make large-scale diffusion models more accessible. This is highly likely, as shown by the related papers referencing this paper. The impact will be seen in new efficient methods of training large models with a reduction in computational costs as well as human costs for developing HP configurations.

**Justification of score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by adapting and rigorously validating µP for diffusion transformers.  The theoretical grounding and experimental validation are both strong. Although the study may not cover all scaling dimensions (such as data scaling), and its impact hinges on the initial configuration, it successfully demonstrates substantial practical benefits, with well-defined conclusions, which warrants a score close to excellent.

### Hallucinate at the Last in Long Response Generation: A Case Study on Long Document Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15291v1)
- **Authors**: Joonho Yang, Seunghyun Yoon, Hwan Chang, Byeongjeong Kim, Hwanhee Lee
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced text generation capabilities, including tasks like summarization, often producing coherent and fluent outputs. However, faithfulness to source material remains a significant challenge due to the generation of hallucinations. While extensive research focuses on detecting and reducing these inaccuracies, less attention has been paid to the positional distribution of hallucination within generated text, particularly in long outputs. In this work, we investigate where hallucinations occur in LLM-based long response generation, using long document summarization as a key case study. Focusing on the challenging setting of long context-aware long response generation, we find a consistent and concerning phenomenon: hallucinations tend to concentrate disproportionately in the latter parts of the generated long response. To understand this bias, we explore potential contributing factors related to the dynamics of attention and decoding over long sequences. Furthermore, we investigate methods to mitigate this positional hallucination, aiming to improve faithfulness specifically in the concluding segments of long outputs.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper "Hallucinate at the Last in Long Response Generation: A Case Study on Long Document Summarization" investigates the positional distribution of hallucinations in long-form text generation, specifically focusing on long document summarization. It identifies a phenomenon termed "Hallucinate at the Last," where factual errors are disproportionately concentrated in the concluding segments of generated summaries. The authors empirically characterize this bias across different LLMs, datasets, and output lengths, and explores potential contributing factors related to attention mechanisms. Finally, they explore mitigation strategies to improve faithfulness in the concluding portions of long outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focused investigation of the *positional distribution* of hallucinations. While the general problem of hallucination in LLMs is well-studied, the systematic analysis of how and where these errors manifest *within* long responses adds a valuable dimension to the existing literature. Exploring "Hallucinate at the Last" is a valuable contribution. The sensitivity metric proposed to analyze the phenomenon is simple but effective.

*   **Significance:** The identified "Hallucinate at the Last" phenomenon has significant implications for various applications involving long-form generation. It raises concerns about the reliability of conclusions and synthesized information presented in LLM-generated outputs. The study's findings could inform the development of more robust generation techniques and evaluation metrics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Rigor:** The study employs a diverse set of LLMs (Llama, Mistral, Qwen, GPT-40 mini) and datasets (Wikipedia, arXiv, PubMed, GovReport) to validate its findings, increasing the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the research question and defines the "Hallucinate at the Last" phenomenon.
    *   **Analysis of Potential Causes:** The authors explore plausible explanations related to attention mechanisms and the nature of summarization.
    *   **Exploration of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper takes a step towards addressing the problem by investigating potential mitigation strategies, although this section is relatively limited in scope.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Depth of Causal Analysis:** While the paper explores potential factors contributing to the "Hallucinate at the Last" phenomenon, the causal relationships are not fully established. Further investigation is needed to pinpoint the specific mechanisms driving this bias.
    *   **Mitigation Strategies are Incremental:** The mitigation strategies employed appear to be incremental improvements over existing techniques, rather than fundamentally novel approaches specifically designed to address the identified positional bias.
    *   **Lack of Human Evaluation:** While the authors evaluate faithfulness using NLI models, incorporating human evaluation could provide more nuanced insights into the nature and severity of the hallucinations.
    *   **The contribution of summarization characteristics can be improved.** Exploring the structure of hallucinated sentences and correlating them with the key information in the corresponding sections of the original text will add more insights.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in long-form generation, particularly in developing more robust and reliable summarization systems. It highlights the need for evaluation metrics that account for positional biases and for generation techniques that ensure faithfulness throughout the entire output. The work is practical and has the potential to guide future work in the field.

*   **Conclusion:** In summary, the paper makes a novel and valuable contribution by identifying and characterizing the "Hallucinate at the Last" phenomenon. Its empirical rigor and potential influence are notable strengths. However, the limited depth of causal analysis and the incremental nature of the mitigation strategies prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

### LLM-Explorer: A Plug-in Reinforcement Learning Policy Exploration Enhancement Driven by Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15293v1)
- **Authors**: Qianyue Hao, Yiwen Song, Qingmin Liao, Jian Yuan, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Policy exploration is critical in reinforcement learning (RL), where existing approaches include greedy, Gaussian process, etc. However, these approaches utilize preset stochastic processes and are indiscriminately applied in all kinds of RL tasks without considering task-specific features that influence policy exploration. Moreover, during RL training, the evolution of such stochastic processes is rigid, which typically only incorporates a decay in the variance, failing to adjust flexibly according to the agent's real-time learning status. Inspired by the analyzing and reasoning capability of large language models (LLMs), we design LLM-Explorer to adaptively generate task-specific exploration strategies with LLMs, enhancing the policy exploration in RL. In our design, we sample the learning trajectory of the agent during the RL training in a given task and prompt the LLM to analyze the agent's current policy learning status and then generate a probability distribution for future policy exploration. Updating the probability distribution periodically, we derive a stochastic process specialized for the particular task and dynamically adjusted to adapt to the learning process. Our design is a plug-in module compatible with various widely applied RL algorithms, including the DQN series, DDPG, TD3, and any possible variants developed based on them. Through extensive experiments on the Atari and MuJoCo benchmarks, we demonstrate LLM-Explorer's capability to enhance RL policy exploration, achieving an average performance improvement up to 37.27%. Our code is open-source at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/LLM-Explorer-19BE for reproducibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLM-Explorer, a novel plug-in module designed to enhance policy exploration in reinforcement learning (RL) using large language models (LLMs).  Instead of relying on pre-defined stochastic processes for exploration, LLM-Explorer leverages LLMs to analyze the agent's learning trajectory and generate task-specific exploration strategies.  This involves sampling the agent's experience, prompting an LLM to analyze the learning status, and then generating a probability distribution for future exploration based on that analysis.  The distribution is updated periodically, dynamically adjusting exploration.  The design is compatible with various RL algorithms (DQN, DDPG, TD3) and action spaces (discrete, continuous).  Experiments on Atari and MuJoCo demonstrate improved performance compared to baseline RL algorithms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of using LLMs to dynamically adjust exploration in RL is reasonably novel. While LLMs have been used for reward shaping, task decomposition, and state representation, the direct application to adaptive policy exploration using experience analysis represents a fresh angle. The plug-in architecture that can be directly added to different RL architectures is also valuable.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a genuine limitation of existing RL exploration methods: their lack of adaptability to task-specific features and the agent's real-time learning status.  The performance gains reported on standard benchmarks (up to 37.27% average improvement) suggest a potentially meaningful impact. The method has practical significance given it has been designed in a plug-and-play fashion, easy for researchers or engineers to try out and apply.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Adaptive Exploration:** The core strength is the ability to generate task-specific exploration strategies dynamically.
    *   **LLM-Driven Analysis:** The use of LLMs for experience analysis provides richer insights than simple variance decay.
    *   **Broad Compatibility:**  The plug-in design facilitates integration with diverse RL algorithms and action spaces.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** Demonstrating performance improvements on Atari and MuJoCo lends credibility to the approach.
    * The paper contains ablations and case studies that illustrate the value of individual components of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependence and Computational Cost:** The method introduces a dependence on LLMs, which may increase computational costs and introduce additional hyperparameters related to LLM usage (e.g. prompting strategy). While ablations explore the cost (in tokens) and performance tradeoff, this dependence needs to be carefully considered for real-world deployments, which might make the approach impractical.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The performance of the method is likely sensitive to the prompt design. The paper provides details about prompts, but the robustness to variations in prompt formulation remains a concern. A more detailed analysis would benefit the paper.
    *   **Limited LLM Exploration:** While the paper investigates using different LLMs, the analysis does not really offer insight into the type of LLM that are suited for the task, only that they improve performance.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence how RL exploration is approached, encouraging more adaptive and task-aware strategies. It could stimulate further research into leveraging LLMs for RL beyond reward shaping and state representation.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of reinforcement learning. The idea of dynamically adjusting exploration using LLMs is novel and addresses a key limitation of traditional methods. The plug-in architecture and the demonstrated performance improvements make the approach practically relevant. However, the dependence on LLMs and the sensitivity to prompt engineering are significant drawbacks that need to be carefully considered. The ablations are helpful, but the paper could benefit from more detailed analysis of the prompt and LLM choice. The improvement is also only shown on relatively simple RL tasks, it is not clear the result will carry over to more complex domains. Overall, while having limitations, the method makes a valuable contribution to the RL space.
