- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings, their ability to refuse ethically sensitive prompts-such as those involving hate speech or illegal activities-has become central to content moderation and responsible AI practices. While refusal responses can be viewed as evidence of ethical alignment and safety-conscious behavior, recent research suggests that users may perceive them negatively. At the same time, automated assessments of model outputs are playing a growing role in both evaluation and training. In particular, LLM-as-a-Judge frameworks-in which one model is used to evaluate the output of another-are now widely adopted to guide benchmarking and fine-tuning. This paper examines whether such model-based evaluators assess refusal responses differently than human users. Drawing on data from Chatbot Arena and judgments from two AI judges (GPT-4o and Llama 3 70B), we compare how different types of refusals are rated. We distinguish ethical refusals, which explicitly cite safety or normative concerns (e.g., "I can't help with that because it may be harmful"), and technical refusals, which reflect system limitations (e.g., "I can't answer because I lack real-time data"). We find that LLM-as-a-Judge systems evaluate ethical refusals significantly more favorably than human users, a divergence not observed for technical refusals. We refer to this divergence as a moderation bias-a systematic tendency for model-based evaluators to reward refusal behaviors more than human users do. This raises broader questions about transparency, value alignment, and the normative assumptions embedded in automated evaluation systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "AI vs. Human Judgment of Content Moderation: LLM-as-a-Judge and Ethics-Based Response Refusals" investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) used as evaluators (LLM-as-a-Judge or LaaJ) assess content moderation behaviors, specifically response refusals, compared to human users.  It differentiates between ethical refusals (based on safety/normative concerns) and technical refusals (based on system limitations). The study uses data from Chatbot Arena and finds that LaaJ systems (GPT-4o and Llama 3 70B) evaluate ethical refusals significantly more favorably than human users, while there's no such divergence for technical refusals. This divergence is termed a "moderation bias," suggesting a systematic tendency for model-based evaluators to reward refusal behaviors aligned with developer-defined safety objectives. The paper highlights potential implications for transparency, value alignment, and contestability in automated model assessment, especially as LaaJ systems are increasingly used for training and benchmarking LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper identifies and empirically demonstrates a novel form of bias in LLM-as-a-Judge frameworks. While previous studies have revealed biases related to length, tone, and confidence, the focus on *moderation bias*—the systematic overvaluation of ethical refusals compared to human preferences—is a significant contribution. This goes beyond simply identifying a bias; it links the bias to the alignment objectives embedded in LLM training.
* **Significance:** The findings have important implications for the responsible development and deployment of LLMs.  As LaaJ systems are increasingly used to train and evaluate other models, the discovered moderation bias could lead to a divergence between model behavior and user expectations, potentially resulting in systems that are overly cautious, restrictive, or disconnected from user intent. The paper rightly points out that this raises questions about transparency, accountability, and governance of AI evaluation infrastructures. The analysis also brings to light the potentially opaque and difficult-to-contest nature of developer-defined values embedded within these evaluation systems. The concerns around "normative lock-in" are crucial.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Research Question:** The research question is well-defined and directly addresses a relevant and important problem.
    *   **Sound Methodology:**  The use of a large-scale dataset (Chatbot Arena) and state-of-the-art LLMs (GPT-4o and Llama 3 70B) as evaluators lends credibility to the findings. The classification of refusals into ethical and technical categories is insightful and allows for a nuanced analysis. The inclusion of controls for stylistic factors strengthens the robustness of the results.  The regression analysis provides solid support for the descriptive findings.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers concrete recommendations for fostering more transparent and user-aligned LLM behavior, such as the use of evaluation cards, human-in-the-loop evaluation pipelines, and more participatory alignment approaches.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Data:** The analysis relies solely on the Chatbot Arena dataset, which may not be representative of all user populations or conversational contexts. The win/loss/tie evaluation scheme provides a relatively coarse assessment.
    *   **Descriptive Approach:** While the paper convincingly demonstrates a moderation bias, it does not directly causally link it to alignment training or specific model objectives.  This would require further investigation into model training processes and internal mechanisms. The argument for the effect of alignment training is plausible but remains largely speculative without more direct evidence.
    *   **Limited Model Diversity:** The evaluation is limited to GPT-4o and Llama 3 70B.  Exploring a wider range of judge models, including smaller or multilingual systems, would provide a more comprehensive understanding of the phenomenon.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by identifying and empirically demonstrating a novel and important bias in LLM-as-a-Judge frameworks. The implications for responsible AI development are significant. While there are some limitations in terms of the scope of the data and the depth of causal analysis, the paper provides a solid foundation for future research. The identification of the "moderation bias" and the linking of this bias to potential risks of "normative lock-in" are central to its contributions. It successfully combines solid empirical analysis with relevant theoretical underpinnings. For these reasons, while acknowledging the limitations, I will assign it a score of 8.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 8/10

### RePPL: Recalibrating Perplexity by Uncertainty in Semantic Propagation and Language Generation for Explainable QA Hallucination Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15386v1)
- **Authors**: Yiming Huang, Junyan Zhang, Zihao Wang, Biquan Bie, Xuming Hu, Yi R., Fung, Xinlei He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have become powerful, but hallucinations remain a vital obstacle to their trustworthy use. While previous works improved the capability of hallucination detection by measuring uncertainty, they all lack the ability to explain the provenance behind why hallucinations occur, i.e., which part of the inputs tends to trigger hallucinations. Recent works on the prompt attack indicate that uncertainty exists in semantic propagation, where attention mechanisms gradually fuse local token information into high-level semantics across layers. Meanwhile, uncertainty also emerges in language generation, due to its probability-based selection of high-level semantics for sampled generations. Based on that, we propose RePPL to recalibrate uncertainty measurement by these two aspects, which dispatches explainable uncertainty scores to each token and aggregates in Perplexity-style Log-Average form as total score. Experiments show that our method achieves the best comprehensive detection performance across various QA datasets on advanced models (average AUC of 0.833), and our method is capable of producing token-level uncertainty scores as explanations for the hallucination. Leveraging these scores, we preliminarily find the chaotic pattern of hallucination and showcase its promising usage.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RePPL: Recalibrating Perplexity by Uncertainty in Semantic Propagation and Language Generation for Explainable QA Hallucination Detection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RePPL, a novel method for detecting and explaining hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) applied to question answering. RePPL recalibrates uncertainty measurement by considering two key aspects: (1) semantic propagation within the model, where attention mechanisms fuse token information across layers, and (2) language generation, where probabilistic selection of high-level semantics occurs.  It uses an attribution-based explainability method to compute token-level uncertainty for semantic propagation and normalizes token confidence for language generation. These uncertainties are aggregated using a Perplexity-style approach, resulting in two scores: InnerPPL and OuterPPL.  The final hallucination score, RePPL, is derived by multiplying these scores. The method aims to improve hallucination detection performance and provide token-level explanations for why hallucinations occur.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its two-pronged approach to uncertainty measurement.  Prior work primarily focused on uncertainty in the generation stage (logits-based or sampling-based). RePPL extends this by explicitly modeling uncertainty during semantic propagation, which is potentially a more direct and granular measure of internal model inconsistencies. The token-level explanations based on uncertainty scores is another important novelty compared to prior work, which primarily focused on just detection performance.

*   **Significance:** Hallucination remains a critical issue limiting the trustworthiness of LLMs. Improving detection *and* explanation is valuable. Demonstrating strong performance on multiple QA datasets is promising, suggesting the method has practical relevance.  The paper's ability to generate token-level explanations is also a significant step towards debugging and improving LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on a variety of QA datasets and models.
    *   **Explainability:** RePPL offers a way to understand *why* a hallucination occurred by identifying uncertainty-inducing tokens.
    *   **Robustness:** Ablation studies and sensitivity analysis show the method to be relatively robust to different hyperparameters and sampling strategies.
    *   **Analysis of Hallucination Patterns:** The paper provides initial insights into the connection between attention disorder and hallucination, hinting towards a useful statistical signal to understand the underlying causes.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Explanations:** The paper acknowledges limitations in capturing high-confidence hallucinations and that their explanations are relatively coarse due to time-complexity.
    *   **Attribution Method Choice:** While the empirical results with the `AvgPool` attribution method seem good, it is not a well-established attribution method in general for explainability. It is not theoretically justified.
    *   **Theoretically Weak Justification for RePPL:** The authors mention that 'the multiplication form means recalibration of uncertainty from the two aspects', without providing sufficient mathematical justification.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the method proves generalizable across diverse tasks and models, RePPL could contribute to building more reliable LLM-based systems.  The explainability aspect has the potential to guide future research on improving the reasoning abilities of LLMs and mitigating hallucinations. Further work is needed to validate that the explanations correlate with actual issues in model reasoning and not simply noisy artifacts.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper tackles an important problem and offers a novel perspective with strong empirical results and some explainability. However, it also has limitations in scope of explanations, lacks a robust theoretical justification, and some weaknesses in attribution method. The combination of a good novelty score together with the identified strengths and weaknesses justifies a '7' rating.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### An Empirical Study of the Anchoring Effect in LLMs: Existence, Mechanism, and Potential Mitigations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15392v1)
- **Authors**: Yiming Huang, Biquan Bie, Zuqiu Na, Weilin Ruan, Songxin Lei, Yutao Yue, Xinlei He
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT has advanced natural language processing, yet concerns about cognitive biases are growing. In this paper, we investigate the anchoring effect, a cognitive bias where the mind relies heavily on the first information as anchors to make affected judgments. We explore whether LLMs are affected by anchoring, the underlying mechanisms, and potential mitigation strategies. To facilitate studies at scale on the anchoring effect, we introduce a new dataset, SynAnchors. Combining refined evaluation metrics, we benchmark current widely used LLMs. Our findings show that LLMs' anchoring bias exists commonly with shallow-layer acting and is not eliminated by conventional strategies, while reasoning can offer some mitigation. This recontextualization via cognitive psychology urges that LLM evaluations focus not on standard benchmarks or over-optimized robustness tests, but on cognitive-bias-aware trustworthy evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "An Empirical Study of the Anchoring Effect in LLMs: Existence, Mechanism, and Potential Mitigations" investigates the anchoring effect, a cognitive bias, in Large Language Models (LLMs).  It examines whether LLMs are susceptible to anchoring, explores the underlying mechanisms of this bias within LLMs, and tests several mitigation strategies. To facilitate research, the authors introduce *SynAnchors*, a new dataset specifically designed to evaluate the anchoring effect.  The paper benchmarks several current LLMs, uses causal tracing to understand internal mechanisms, and evaluates the effectiveness of different mitigation techniques, including a novel approach called Anti-DP (Anti-Dual-Process).  The key findings highlight that anchoring biases are common in LLMs, that the bias appears to operate at a relatively shallow level within the LLM's internal workings, and that conventional mitigation strategies are not fully effective, though reasoning-based interventions show promise.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers several aspects of novelty. The creation of *SynAnchors* dataset and the development of a structured methodology that leverages both semantic and numerical priming is a significant contribution. Applying causal tracing methods to understand cognitive biases inside LLMs is also innovative. The Anti-DP mitigation strategy, which is based on cognitive psychology, represents a new attempt to address biases. However, the *individual* components (using existing methods, causal tracing or specific mitigation strategies) are well-established. The novelty lies in *combining* these to tackle anchoring bias and the design of specialized dataset and methodology.

*   **Significance:** The study's significance stems from the growing concern about the trustworthiness of LLMs. Identifying and quantifying cognitive biases like the anchoring effect is essential for ensuring that LLMs are reliable and unbiased.  The findings that LLMs are susceptible to anchoring, even if at a shallow level, and the ineffectiveness of standard mitigation approaches, are important for guiding future research in this area.  The paper highlights the need to shift evaluation practices from standard benchmarks to cognitive-bias-aware assessments. The link to Dual Process theory is compelling.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Defined Research Questions:** The paper clearly outlines its research questions (RQ1, RQ2, RQ3), providing a clear structure for the investigation.
    *   **Comprehensive Methodology:** The use of both quantitative evaluation metrics (A-Index, R-Error) and qualitative analysis (causal tracing) provides a comprehensive understanding of the anchoring effect.
    *   **Carefully Designed Dataset:** *SynAnchors* is specifically designed to elicit anchoring effects, which improves the quality of the results.
    *   **Connection to Cognitive Psychology:** Framing the research within the context of cognitive psychology (Dual Process Theory, SAM) provides a strong theoretical foundation and valuable insights.
    *   **Empirical Results:** Extensive emperical evaluations of SOTA models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Mitigation Strategies:** Although Anti-DP shows some promise, the mitigation strategies tested were not able to eliminate the bias fully. Future research could explore more sophisticated debiasing techniques.
    *   **Shallow Anchoring:** The finding that anchoring is shallow, while interesting, requires further exploration.  It's important to investigate why reasoning models may not be eliminating anchoring at a deeper level.
    *   **Limited Human Comparison:** The paper highlights that, due to budgetary constraints, they cannot conduct same massive-scale test on human participants. Lack of a direct comparison with human participants regarding the intensity and mechanisms of the anchoring effect. While this is acknowledged, it somewhat limits the interpretation of the results in a broader context.
    *   **Specific Datasets and LLMs:** The findings are based on the specific SynAnchors dataset and a select group of LLMs. The generalizability of these findings to other datasets and LLMs needs to be further investigated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Encouraging researchers to develop more robust evaluation metrics that account for cognitive biases.
    *   Motivating further research into the internal mechanisms of cognitive biases within LLMs.
    *   Guiding the development of more effective debiasing techniques that are grounded in cognitive psychology.
    *   Promoting the creation of more trustworthy and reliable LLMs that are less susceptible to biases.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of LLM trustworthiness. It offers a novel dataset and a combination of methodologies to study the anchoring effect, providing compelling evidence for its existence in LLMs. While the mitigation strategies explored are not fully successful, the research highlights the challenges of debiasing LLMs and points to promising directions for future research. The connection to cognitive psychology enhances the theoretical understanding of the anchoring effect. However, the scope of the mitigation approaches is limited, and the absence of a strong comparison with human cognitive behavior is a drawback. I believe the novel dataset and overall methodology merit a score of 7, because this is a strong piece of work which opens a new path. However, the limitations discussed above prevent it from being in the 8-10 range.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reranking with Compressed Document Representation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15394v1)
- **Authors**: Hervé Déjean, Stéphane Clinchant
- **Abstract**: Reranking, the process of refining the output of a first-stage retriever, is often considered computationally expensive, especially with Large Language Models. Borrowing from recent advances in document compression for RAG, we reduce the input size by compressing documents into fixed-size embedding representations. We then teach a reranker to use compressed inputs by distillation. Although based on a billion-size model, our trained reranker using this compressed input can challenge smaller rerankers in terms of both effectiveness and efficiency, especially for long documents. Given that text compressors are still in their early development stages, we view this approach as promising.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary, a rigorous critical evaluation of the novelty and significance, and a score with justification.

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel approach to reranking in information retrieval by using compressed document representations. Instead of directly feeding the full text of documents to a reranker (which is often computationally expensive, especially with large language models), the authors first compress the documents into fixed-size embeddings using a method based on PISCO. This compressed representation is then used as input to a distilled reranker (based on Mistral-7B), trained to match the scores of a larger, state-of-the-art cross-encoder. The authors demonstrate that their compressed representation-based reranker (RRK) achieves comparable effectiveness to smaller rerankers while being significantly more efficient, particularly for long documents, and can even be faster than the teacher model.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a worthwhile and innovative idea that addresses a crucial bottleneck in modern information retrieval: the computational cost of reranking. The use of compressed document representations for reranking is a clever way to reduce the input size and accelerate the process.

Here's a breakdown of the aspects contributing to/ detracting from the score, including a critical assessment:

*   **Novelty:** The key innovation is the application of compression techniques, inspired by RAG context compression, *specifically to the reranking stage*. While document compression itself is not entirely new, its focused application for *reranking* based on a frozen model PISCO and the distilled training is the original component.  The method of freezing PISCO and using LoRA training is also a relevant detail to consider when evaluating the novelty, since this approach is a contribution for future RAG systems.
*   **Significance:**
    *   **Efficiency Improvement:**  The claim of a 10x speedup (Figure 1) compared to using raw text is a *significant* achievement, directly addressing a major challenge in LLM-based IR.
    *   **Scalability:** The reduced computational cost makes reranking more scalable, enabling the use of more sophisticated models on larger datasets.
    *   **Practical Impact:** The approach has the potential to be adopted in real-world search engines and information retrieval systems to improve efficiency without sacrificing effectiveness.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear Problem Statement: The paper clearly articulates the problem of reranking costs and motivates the proposed solution.
    *   Well-Defined Methodology: The compression and distillation processes are clearly explained, and the authors provide details on model architectures, training procedures, and evaluation metrics.
    *   Solid Experimental Results: The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of the proposed approach on several datasets.
    *   Comparison to Baselines: The authors compare their method to several strong baselines, including models using raw text and state-of-the-art architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on PISCO: The method relies on the PISCO compressor, and the performance of RRK is likely limited by the quality of the compressed representations. While this reliance also makes the methodology innovative, it is also a possible point of failure.
    *   Limited Evaluation of Very Long Documents: While the authors claim that the approach is particularly beneficial for long documents, the evaluation only includes documents up to 1024 tokens. Evaluating on datasets with significantly longer documents (e.g., full books or articles) would further strengthen the claims. The limitations in the PISCO compressor also affects this aspect of the work since PISCO models are trained using short documents.
    *   Generalization: The authors noted that it does not work well for very long queries, this is a point that should be investigated further in future studies.

*   **Impact:** The paper's ideas are likely to inspire further research into using compressed representations for various IR tasks, including document retrieval, question answering, and summarization. The concept has practical implications for building more efficient and scalable IR systems. The paper will also cause other researchers to investigate RAG models in this way for the next generation LLMs.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and worthwhile approach to a critical problem in information retrieval. It presents reasonable results, that shows the method performs efficiently. The major innovation lies in the use of compressed representation for re-ranking.  However, while significant speedups are demonstrated, the reliance on PISCO as a fixed element and the evaluation being constrained to relatively short documents (1024 tokens) lowers the impact of the work. An investigation of the impact on much larger context lengths and/or a compressed approach, based on the work, would increase the score. Despite these weaknesses, the idea is a strong and valid contribution and deserves a positive evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Efficient Data Driven Mixture-of-Expert Extraction from Trained Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15414v1)
- **Authors**: Uranik Berisha, Jens Mehnert, Alexandru Paul Condurache
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers have emerged as the state-of-the-art models in various Computer Vision tasks, but their high computational and resource demands pose significant challenges. While Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) can make these models more efficient, they often require costly retraining or even training from scratch. Recent developments aim to reduce these computational costs by leveraging pretrained networks. These have been shown to produce sparse activation patterns in the Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs) of the encoder blocks, allowing for conditional activation of only relevant subnetworks for each sample. Building on this idea, we propose a new method to construct MoE variants from pretrained models. Our approach extracts expert subnetworks from the model's MLP layers post-training in two phases. First, we cluster output activations to identify distinct activation patterns. In the second phase, we use these clusters to extract the corresponding subnetworks responsible for producing them. On ImageNet-1k recognition tasks, we demonstrate that these extracted experts can perform surprisingly well out of the box and require only minimal fine-tuning to regain 98% of the original performance, all while reducing MACs and model size, by up to 36% and 32% respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for efficiently creating Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) variants from pre-trained Vision Transformers (ViTs) *without* extensive retraining. The key idea is to extract expert subnetworks from the ViT's Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) layers *post-training*.  The approach has two main phases: 1) Cluster hidden layer activations using HDBSCAN to identify distinct activation patterns.  This allows the number and size of experts to be data-driven. 2) Extract the subnetworks responsible for these activation patterns.  During inference, a simple routing mechanism based on similarity to cluster means conditionally activates the appropriate expert. The method significantly reduces computational costs (MACs) and model size while preserving performance on ImageNet-1k recognition tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of extracting MoEs from *pre-trained* ViTs is *not entirely new* in itself; recent works like [32] have explored similar approaches in language transformers. However, the specific *data-driven* approach to determining expert configuration using HDBSCAN and variance-based neuron selection within the MLP layers of ViTs distinguishes this work. This is an important distinction given structural differences between vision and language transformers. The application of this approach to ViTs specifically and the investigation of emergent structure in ViT layers contributes to its novelty.  There's a degree of novelty in *how* they extract the experts (HDBSCAN + variance).
* **Significance:** The significant reduction in MACs (up to 36%) and model size (up to 32%) while retaining near original performance of larger ViTs addresses a critical bottleneck in deploying ViTs on resource-constrained hardware.  This makes ViTs more practical for real-world applications.  The work offers a practical strategy for efficient ViT deployment and a way of understanding internal organization of the network. The insights into how different ViT layers exhibit varying degrees of specialization are also valuable. *Moreover, the claim about eliminating need for auxiliary loss for MoE is substantial.*
* **Strengths:**
    * Clear and well-written presentation.
    * The method is relatively simple and efficient, allowing for quick conversion of pre-trained ViTs into MoE variants.
    * The use of HDBSCAN offers a flexible, data-driven way to identify expert structures.
    * The ablation studies rigorously validate the importance of each component of the method.
    * The experiments demonstrate significant reduction in computational cost and memory footprint.
    * The inclusion of insights into the specialization of different layers in ViTs strengthens the paper.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The paper relies on pre-trained models. While this is a strength in terms of efficiency, it limits the ability to control the training process and explore the full potential of MoEs (compared to training MoEs from scratch).
    * The routing mechanism, while efficient, is relatively simple. More sophisticated routing strategies could potentially lead to further performance improvements.
    *  The analysis of routed tokens while interesting, can be more in depth.
    * While the approach achieves very good accuracy, there's a relatively small dip in final accuracy (0.25-1.6% in Table 1). It's important to remember the goal here is efficiency, and the dip might be worth it, but it's a consideration.
    * Comparative Evaluation: While the results look quite good, the performance against the MoEfication approach isn't overwhelmingly better across all models. For the Deit-T,MoEE even has lower final accuracy than MoEfication. The differences mostly shine through on larger models, but the impact could've been highlighted better.

* **Potential Influence:** The method has the potential to influence how ViTs are deployed and utilized, particularly in scenarios where computational resources are limited.  It could spur further research into data-driven approaches for model compression and efficient inference.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**.

*Rationale:*

The paper introduces a useful and efficient technique for creating MoE variants from pre-trained ViTs, addressing a crucial practical challenge. The data-driven expert extraction is a solid contribution. It also provides interesting analytical insights. While the core idea of extracting MoEs from pre-trained models is not entirely novel, the *specific* implementation and its application to ViTs with strong empirical results, and valuable insights warrant a good score. Some elements of the work, especially the performance gap, compared to the non-MoE baseline could use further refinement. The method offers practical performance benefits with clearly demonstrated reduction in computational cost and memory.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Silent Leaks: Implicit Knowledge Extraction Attack on RAG Systems through Benign Queries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15420v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhao Wang, Wenjie Qu, Yanze Jiang, Zichen Liu, Yue Liu, Shengfang Zhai, Yinpeng Dong, Jiaheng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems enhance large language models (LLMs) by incorporating external knowledge bases, but they are vulnerable to privacy risks from data extraction attacks. Existing extraction methods typically rely on malicious inputs such as prompt injection or jailbreaking, making them easily detectable via input- or output-level detection. In this paper, we introduce Implicit Knowledge Extraction Attack (IKEA), which conducts knowledge extraction on RAG systems through benign queries. IKEA first leverages anchor concepts to generate queries with the natural appearance, and then designs two mechanisms to lead to anchor concept thoroughly 'explore' the RAG's privacy knowledge: (1) Experience Reflection Sampling, which samples anchor concepts based on past query-response patterns to ensure the queries' relevance to RAG documents; (2) Trust Region Directed Mutation, which iteratively mutates anchor concepts under similarity constraints to further exploit the embedding space. Extensive experiments demonstrate IKEA's effectiveness under various defenses, surpassing baselines by over 80% in extraction efficiency and 90% in attack success rate. Moreover, the substitute RAG system built from IKEA's extractions consistently outperforms those based on baseline methods across multiple evaluation tasks, underscoring the significant privacy risk in RAG systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new attack called Implicit Knowledge Extraction Attack (IKEA) on Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. Unlike existing attacks that rely on malicious inputs (prompt injection, jailbreaking), IKEA uses benign queries to extract knowledge. It achieves this through two main mechanisms: Experience Reflection Sampling (ERS), which selects anchor concepts based on past query-response history to increase relevance to RAG documents, and Trust Region Directed Mutation (TRDM), which iteratively mutates anchor concepts under similarity constraints to explore the embedding space thoroughly. The authors demonstrate IKEA's effectiveness in extracting knowledge from RAG systems, even in the presence of defenses. The extracted knowledge can then be used to build a substitute RAG system that performs comparably to the original, thereby compromising the privacy of the original data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key strength of the paper is its novelty. Existing attacks on RAG systems often focus on direct verbatim extraction using malicious inputs, which are becoming easier to defend against. IKEA's shift towards knowledge extraction using benign queries represents a significant advancement. The introduction of Experience Reflection Sampling and Trust Region Directed Mutation are clever approaches to guide the exploration of the knowledge base in a stealthy manner. This strategic shift, sidestepping traditional attack patterns, is a notable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a crucial vulnerability in RAG systems. The ability to extract knowledge through seemingly innocuous queries demonstrates a more subtle and insidious privacy risk than previously considered. This finding has significant implications for the security and privacy of RAG systems, particularly those dealing with sensitive information in domains like healthcare, finance, and law. The fact that a substitute RAG can be built from the extracted knowledge and still perform well emphasizes the real-world consequences of this vulnerability. The demonstration of success even against rudimentary defenses underscores the practical importance of addressing this attack vector.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing RAG extraction methods and motivates the need for a more stealthy approach.
    *   **Well-Designed Methodology:** IKEA is well-designed, with ERS and TRDM forming a coherent strategy for knowledge extraction. The explanations of these mechanisms are clear and easy to follow.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct thorough experiments across various datasets, RAG architectures, embedding models, and defense strategies. The use of multiple metrics (extraction efficiency, attack success rate, chunk recovery rate, semantic similarity) provides a holistic view of IKEA's performance.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies effectively demonstrate the importance of ERS and TRDM in IKEA's success.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** While the concepts behind ERS and TRDM are sound, implementing them requires careful tuning of several hyperparameters. The paper could benefit from more detailed guidance on setting these parameters in different scenarios.
    *   **Real-World Defenses:** The paper primarily focuses on simple input and output-level defenses. While these defenses are representative of early deployment strategies, future work should explore IKEA's effectiveness against more sophisticated defense mechanisms. The authors have some discussion around differential privacy but leave the thorough analysis to future work.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field. It will encourage researchers to develop more robust defense mechanisms against knowledge extraction attacks that go beyond simple input/output filtering. The study will also increase awareness of the subtle privacy risks associated with RAG systems and the need for more comprehensive security audits.
*   **Areas for Further Investigation:**
     *  Exploring more complex datasets.
     *  Evaluating the attack on more robust defense mechanisms.
     *  Exploring more advanced methods in query generation.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of RAG security. IKEA introduces a new class of attack that is stealthier and more effective than existing methods. The well-designed methodology, comprehensive evaluation, and convincing results make this a valuable contribution that will likely influence future research in this area. The weaknesses are relatively minor and do not detract significantly from the overall impact of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Responsible Diffusion Models via Constraining Text Embeddings within Safe Regions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15427v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiwen Li, Die Chen, Mingyuan Fan, Cen Chen, Yaliang Li, Yanhao Wang, Wenmeng Zhou
- **Abstract**: The remarkable ability of diffusion models to generate high-fidelity images has led to their widespread adoption. However, concerns have also arisen regarding their potential to produce Not Safe for Work (NSFW) content and exhibit social biases, hindering their practical use in real-world applications. In response to this challenge, prior work has focused on employing security filters to identify and exclude toxic text, or alternatively, fine-tuning pre-trained diffusion models to erase sensitive concepts. Unfortunately, existing methods struggle to achieve satisfactory performance in the sense that they can have a significant impact on the normal model output while still failing to prevent the generation of harmful content in some cases. In this paper, we propose a novel self-discovery approach to identifying a semantic direction vector in the embedding space to restrict text embedding within a safe region. Our method circumvents the need for correcting individual words within the input text and steers the entire text prompt towards a safe region in the embedding space, thereby enhancing model robustness against all possibly unsafe prompts. In addition, we employ Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) for semantic direction vector initialization to reduce the impact on the model performance for other semantics. Furthermore, our method can also be integrated with existing methods to improve their social responsibility. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that our method can effectively reduce NSFW content and mitigate social bias generated by diffusion models compared to several state-of-the-art baselines.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Responsible Diffusion Models via Constraining Text Embeddings within Safe Regions":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating Not Safe for Work (NSFW) content and social biases in text-to-image diffusion models. The authors propose a novel self-discovery approach to identify a semantic direction vector in the text embedding space, which allows them to constrain text embeddings within a "safe region." This method aims to steer the entire text prompt toward a safe region, improving robustness against unsafe prompts. They also use Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) for initializing the semantic direction vector, further reducing the impact on general model performance. The approach can be integrated with other existing methods and is evaluated on benchmark datasets to reduce NSFW content and mitigate social bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of identifying and leveraging a "safe region" in the CLIP embedding space by learning a semantic direction vector is somewhat novel. The self-discovery approach using the diffusion model as an implicit classifier and then refining with a LoRA-based vector is an interesting aspect. Constraining the *entire* text embedding as opposed to single words presents a subtle, yet important, difference from some previous work.

*   **Significance:** The problem of controlling content generation in diffusion models is significant, with practical implications for real-world deployment. Reducing NSFW content, mitigating social biases, and improving fairness are all crucial goals. The paper demonstrates promising results on benchmark datasets. However, the metrics used (classifiers for NSFW content and Winobias deviation ratios) are themselves limited and can be questioned on their reliability, as these classifiers are not perfect.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is theoretically sound and well-motivated. The idea of a "safe region" is intuitive.
    *   The self-discovery method avoids the need for manually labeled data to identify unsafe concepts, which is a practical advantage.
    *   The LoRA-based initialization helps to mitigate the impact of the safety constraints on overall model performance.
    *   The experiments show that the method effectively reduces NSFW content and mitigates social bias in diffusion models.
    *   The approach is complementary and integrates with other existing methods.
    *   The paper explores transferability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why the proposed approach works. Understanding the underlying landscape of the CLIP embedding space and how the learned direction vector interacts with it would provide valuable insights.
    *   The reliance on binary classification for evaluation is a limitation. The metrics could be improved with more fine-grained and contextual analysis.
    *   The computational cost may still be significant, even though it is a fraction of full fine-tuning. The absolute time costs and hardware requirements could be highlighted further.
    *   There is no analysis of failure cases, what types of prompts still produce undesirable results, or types of bias that are not mitigated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work provides a valuable contribution to the field by proposing a new approach for controlling diffusion models. The method's effectiveness in reducing NSFW content and mitigating social bias has the potential to influence future research in the area.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper demonstrates promising experimental results and the novelty is good, the work lacks rigorous theoretical analysis. The dependency on external classifiers for evaluation is also a constraint. While the LoRA optimization for reduced parameter count is great, that aspect isn't novel on its own.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

- **Authors**: Ao Liu, Botong Zhou, Can Xu, Chayse Zhou, ChenChen Zhang, Chengcheng Xu, Chenhao Wang, Decheng Wu, Dengpeng Wu, Dian Jiao, Dong Du, Dong Wang, Feng Zhang, Fengzong Lian, Guanghui Xu, Cheng Jiang, Cheng Zhang, Chengjun Liu, Chengxu Yang, Chiyu Wang, Chong Zha, Daisy Yi, Di Wang, Fanyang Lu, Fei Chen, Feifei Liu, Feng Zheng, Guanghua Yu, Guiyang Li, Guohua Wang, Haisheng Lin, Han Tongkai Li, Wei Deng, Wei Li, Weichao Wang, Weigang Zhang, Weixuan Sun, Wen Ouyang, Wenxiang Jiao, Wenzhi Sun, Wenzhuo Jia, Xiang Zhang, Xiangyu He, Xianshun Ren, XiaoYing Zhu, Xiaolong Guo, Xiaoxue Li, Xiaoyu Ma, Xican Lu, Xinhua Feng, Xinting Huang, Xinyu Guan, Xirui Li, Xu Zhang, Xudong Gao, Xun Luo, Xuxiang Qi, Yangkun Chen, Yangyu Tao, Yanling Xiao, Yantao Mai, Yanze Chen, Yao Ding, Yeting Yang, YiFan Song, Yifan Yang, Yijiao Zhu, Yinhe Wu, Yixian Liu, Yong Yang, Yuanjun Cai, Yuanlin Tu, Yue Zhang, Yufei Huang, Yuhang Zhou, Yuhao Jiang, Yuhong Liu, Yuhui Hu, Yujin Lin, Yun Yang, Yunhao Wang, Yusong Zhang, Zekun Wu, Zelong Zhang, Zhan Yu, Zhaoliang Yang, Zhe Zhao, Zheng Li, Zhenyu Huang, Zhiguang Liu, Zhijiang Xu, Zhiqing Kui, Zhiyin Zeng, Zhiyuan Xiong, Zhuo Han, Zifan Wu, Zigang Geng, Zilong Zhao, Ziyan Tang, Ziyuan Zhu, Zonglei Zhu, Zhijiang Xu
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) rapidly advance, we introduce Hunyuan-TurboS, a novel large hybrid Transformer-Mamba Mixture of Experts (MoE) model. It synergistically combines Mamba's long-sequence processing efficiency with Transformer's superior contextual understanding. Hunyuan-TurboS features an adaptive long-short chain-of-thought (CoT) mechanism, dynamically switching between rapid responses for simple queries and deep "thinking" modes for complex problems, optimizing computational resources. Architecturally, this 56B activated (560B total) parameter model employs 128 layers (Mamba2, Attention, FFN) with an innovative AMF/MF block pattern. Faster Mamba2 ensures linear complexity, Grouped-Query Attention minimizes KV cache, and FFNs use an MoE structure. Pre-trained on 16T high-quality tokens, it supports a 256K context length and is the first industry-deployed large-scale Mamba model. Our comprehensive post-training strategy enhances capabilities via Supervised Fine-Tuning (3M instructions), a novel Adaptive Long-short CoT Fusion method, Multi-round Deliberation Learning for iterative improvement, and a two-stage Large-scale Reinforcement Learning process targeting STEM and general instruction-following. Evaluations show strong performance: overall top 7 rank on LMSYS Chatbot Arena with a score of 1356, outperforming leading models like Gemini-2.0-Flash-001 (1352) and o4-mini-2025-04-16 (1345). TurboS also achieves an average of 77.9% across 23 automated benchmarks. Hunyuan-TurboS balances high performance and efficiency, offering substantial capabilities at lower inference costs than many reasoning models, establishing a new paradigm for efficient large-scale pre-trained models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Hunyuan-TurboS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Hunyuan-TurboS, a hybrid Transformer-Mamba Mixture of Experts (MoE) model designed for large language modeling. It aims to combine the long-sequence processing efficiency of Mamba with the contextual understanding capabilities of Transformers. Key innovations include: an adaptive long-short chain-of-thought (CoT) mechanism for dynamically switching between rapid responses and deep reasoning; a novel AMF/MF block pattern in the architecture; the use of Mamba2 for linear complexity; Grouped-Query Attention to minimize KV cache; and MoE within FFN layers.  The model is pre-trained on 16T tokens and supports a 256K context length. The post-training strategy involves Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), an adaptive long-short CoT fusion method, multi-round deliberation learning, and a two-stage reinforcement learning process.  Experimental results demonstrate strong performance, placing it among the top models in the LMSYS Chatbot Arena and achieving high scores on automated benchmarks. The paper highlights its balance between performance and efficiency, claiming substantial capabilities at lower inference costs compared to many reasoning models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Hybrid Architecture:** The combination of Transformer and Mamba architectures is a promising approach to leverage the strengths of both. The use of Mamba addresses a major bottleneck in Transformer models (quadratic complexity with sequence length).
    *   **Adaptive CoT:** The adaptive CoT mechanism appears to be a valuable feature, allowing the model to tailor its reasoning depth to the complexity of the task, saving computational resources for simpler queries.
    *   **Comprehensive Training Strategy:** The multi-stage training pipeline, incorporating SFT, deliberation learning, and reinforcement learning, indicates a rigorous approach to model optimization.  The emphasis on data quality and diversity during both pre-training and fine-tuning is commendable.
    *   **Efficiency Claims:** The paper makes strong claims about inference efficiency, which, if validated, would be a significant contribution. The token efficiency analysis is particularly compelling.
    *   **Strong Results:** The reported results on LMSYS Chatbot Arena and automated benchmarks suggest that Hunyuan-TurboS achieves competitive performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Details on Mamba Integration:** While the paper mentions the Mamba2 architecture, it provides relatively few technical details regarding how the Transformer and Mamba components are specifically integrated and interact within the AMF/MF blocks. More detail on the design choices behind the particular hybrid structure and the rationale for the specific block patterns would increase the paper's contribution.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** The paper lacks extensive ablation studies to demonstrate the individual contributions of the various components. For instance, it would be valuable to see how performance changes with and without the adaptive CoT mechanism or with different architectural configurations.
    *   **Generalization of Efficiency:** The efficiency claims need stronger support. While the token usage is lower in the benchmarks reported, the true inference cost depends on factors beyond just tokens – particularly the hardware implementations of Mamba operations. Comparative wall-clock time benchmarks against comparable models would strengthen the efficiency claims. The 1.8x speedup claim by optimizing inference with AngelHCF sounds promising, more details on this framework should be included.
    *   **Lack of Transparency:** Although the model parameters are stated, more transparency about architecture configurations such as Mamba d_state size or FFN MoE parameters would be preferred for proper replicability. The same applies to training, as specifics on compute resources are not specified.
    *   **Overstating Novelty:** While the combination of Transformer and Mamba is interesting, it's an incremental development. Adaptive CoT has precedents, as do MoE layers within LLMs. The paper tends to frame these components as groundbreaking innovations, which exaggerates their novelty.

*   **Significance:**

    *   If the efficiency claims are substantiated with more rigorous benchmarks, Hunyuan-TurboS could have a significant impact by enabling high-performance language modeling at lower computational costs.  This would be particularly important for real-world deployment scenarios.
    *   The integration of Mamba into a large-scale LLM could spur further research into hybrid architectures.
    *   The comprehensive training methodology provides valuable insights into optimizing LLMs.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

    The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of large language models, showcasing a promising hybrid architecture and a rigorous training methodology. However, the lack of architectural detail, ablation studies, and robust efficiency benchmarks limits its impact. The paper overstates its novelty, which further reduces its overall score.

### Set-LLM: A Permutation-Invariant LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15433v1)
- **Authors**: Beni Egressy, Jan Stühmer
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) demonstrate impressive capabilities across numerous applications, their robustness remains a critical concern. This paper is motivated by a specific vulnerability: the order sensitivity of LLMs. This vulnerability manifests itself as the order bias observed when LLMs decide between possible options (for example, a preference for the first option) and the tendency of LLMs to provide different answers when options are reordered. The use cases for this scenario extend beyond the classical case of multiple-choice question answering to the use of LLMs as automated evaluators in AI pipelines, comparing output generated by different models. We introduce Set-LLM, a novel architectural adaptation for pretrained LLMs that enables the processing of mixed set-text inputs with permutation invariance guarantees. The adaptations involve a new attention mask and new positional encodings specifically designed for sets. We provide a theoretical proof of invariance and demonstrate through experiments that Set-LLM can be trained effectively, achieving comparable or improved performance and maintaining the runtime of the original model, while eliminating order sensitivity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Set-LLM, a novel architectural adaptation for pre-trained large language models (LLMs) designed to achieve permutation invariance when processing mixed set-text inputs. This addresses the vulnerability of LLMs to order sensitivity, where the order of choices (e.g., in multiple-choice questions) affects the model's response. Set-LLM achieves this invariance through a new attention mask (SetMask) and positional encodings (SetPE) specifically designed for sets. The authors provide a theoretical proof of invariance and demonstrate experimentally that Set-LLM can be trained effectively, maintaining or improving performance while eliminating order sensitivity without increasing model complexity (runtime). The approach is tested on multiple-choice datasets using several different base LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of permutation-invariant neural networks is not entirely new. Specifically, the usage of DeepSets [43] or GNNs [17] is well-known. However, directly integrating such a design into the LLM architecture is a significant contribution. Set-LLM is the first decoder-only LLM that guarantees robustness to permutations. The specific design choices regarding attention masks and positional encodings tailored for sets within text sequences are innovative and well-justified. The theoretical proof of invariance adds considerable weight to the approach.
* **Significance:** The vulnerability of LLMs to input order is a serious issue, especially given their increasing use as automated evaluators and in safety-critical applications. Addressing this directly within the model architecture is crucial for reliability. The fact that Set-LLM achieves this without sacrificing performance or increasing model complexity is a significant practical advantage. It makes LLMs more trustworthy in scenarios involving sets of options, comparisons, or evaluations. The paper offers a simple solution that can be added to the architecture to eliminate order sensitivity that might impact LLM reasoning, evaluation, or other tasks.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of order sensitivity in LLMs and its implications.
    * **Principled Approach:**  The approach is grounded in theoretical analysis and well-motivated architectural choices. The SetMask and SetPE are designed to enforce invariance without losing contextual information.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results across multiple datasets and base LLMs, demonstrating the effectiveness of Set-LLM. The experiments confirm both the robustness to adversarial orderings and the preservation of (or improvement in) accuracy.  Results show similar out-of-distribution performance with respect to standard fine-tuned models.
    * **Practicality:** The model maintains its runtime performance with respect to standard LLMs. The runtime costs have a negligible impact on large-scale LLMs.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The experimental evaluation is primarily focused on multiple-choice question answering. While this is a relevant application, it would be beneficial to explore other scenarios where LLMs process sets of information, like graph reasoning or document retrieval. Some results point out that some of the results can be dataset-dependent (See section E.6 and the different figures in the results appendix).
    * **Runtime Overhead:** A higher model precision is required to guarantee invariance which adds a constant overhead to the runtime costs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLMs by directly addressing the order sensitivity issue through architectural modifications. The theoretical foundation, extensive experimental validation, and practical advantages (no performance degradation) make it a valuable contribution. While the scope of the evaluation is somewhat limited, the potential impact on the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs is considerable.

**Score: 8.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Stronger ViTs With Octic Equivariance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15441v1)
- **Authors**: David Nordström, Johan Edstedt, Fredrik Kahl, Georg Bökman
- **Abstract**: Recent efforts at scaling computer vision models have established Vision Transformers (ViTs) as the leading architecture. ViTs incorporate weight sharing over image patches as an important inductive bias. In this work, we show that ViTs benefit from incorporating equivariance under the octic group, i.e., reflections and 90-degree rotations, as a further inductive bias. We develop new architectures, octic ViTs, that use octic-equivariant layers and put them to the test on both supervised and self-supervised learning. Through extensive experiments on DeiT-III and DINOv2 training on ImageNet-1K, we show that octic ViTs yield more computationally efficient networks while also improving performance. In particular, we achieve approximately 40% reduction in FLOPs for ViT-H while simultaneously improving both classification and segmentation results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces octic-equivariant Vision Transformers (ViTs), which incorporate equivariance under the octic group (rotations and reflections) as an inductive bias. The authors develop new architectures and efficient implementations, including a custom Triton kernel, to enable fast and scalable octic-equivariant layers. Through experiments on DeiT-III and DINOv2 training on ImageNet-1K, they demonstrate that octic ViTs improve computational efficiency (approximately 40% reduction in FLOPs for ViT-H) while maintaining or improving classification and segmentation performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in the specific application of octic equivariance to ViTs and the development of an efficient implementation that makes this feasible at scale.  Prior work has explored equivariance in CNNs and, to a lesser extent, in ViTs (e.g., horizontal flip equivariance), but extending this to the full octic group with an optimized implementation contributes a meaningful advance.  The use of a custom Triton kernel for the activation function is also a significant practical contribution towards efficient execution. The systematic evaluation using both supervised and self-supervised tasks boosts confidence in general applicability.

*   **Significance:** The significance is substantial.  The paper demonstrates a practical way to improve the computational efficiency of ViTs without sacrificing (and sometimes improving) performance. Given the trend towards larger and more complex vision models, methods that improve efficiency are highly valuable. The results are convincing, showing consistent improvements across different training regimes and tasks. The findings challenge the current state-of-the-art, where octic equivariance isn't widely adopted, suggesting a potential paradigm shift. It creates avenues for further research focused on scaling equivariance, potentially exploring larger groups or different equivariant architectures. The fact that performance is sustained on datasets without canonical orientations is extremely important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear and well-defined problem:** The paper tackles a relevant and well-defined problem in computer vision.
    *   **Efficient implementation:** The optimized implementation of octic-equivariant layers, including the Triton kernel, is a significant strength.
    *   **Comprehensive experimental validation:** The extensive experiments on both supervised and self-supervised learning provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Performance gains at scale:** The paper demonstrates that the benefits of octic equivariance become more pronounced as models scale.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited hyperparameter tuning:** The authors acknowledge that they did not perform extensive hyperparameter tuning, which could potentially further improve performance. This implies that the reported results may not be the absolute best achievable.
    *   **Throughput improvements not fully realized:**  The FLOPs savings do not translate linearly to throughput improvements in the current implementation, suggesting that there is room for further optimization of the execution pipeline. The authors note that their custom Triton kernel has eight points to eight point, but details on that is sparse.
    *   **Lack of deeper theoretical analysis:** While the empirical results are strong, a deeper theoretical analysis of why octic equivariance works particularly well in ViTs, and under what conditions it is most beneficial, would strengthen the paper. A comparison with data augmentation would be beneficial, particularly at test time.
    *   **Limited exploration of different invariantizations:** While an ablation study of invariantization methods is presented, more exploration is warranted.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could influence the design of future ViT architectures, leading to more efficient and robust models.
    *   It could inspire further research into the application of equivariance to other deep learning models and tasks.
    *   The efficient implementation of octic-equivariant layers could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners, facilitating the development of equivariant models.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper presents a solid contribution to the field. The demonstrated benefits of octic equivariance in ViTs, combined with an efficient implementation and thorough experimental validation, make this a valuable addition to the literature. However, the limitations regarding hyperparameter tuning, throughput optimization, and theoretical analysis prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. It's a strong incremental improvement, but doesn't completely revolutionize the landscape.

### On the Generalization vs Fidelity Paradox in Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15442v1)
- **Authors**: Suhas Kamasetty Ramesh, Ayan Sengupta, Tanmoy Chakraborty
- **Abstract**: Knowledge distillation (KD) is a key technique for compressing large language models into smaller ones while preserving performance. Despite the recent traction of KD research, its effectiveness for smaller language models (LMs) and the mechanisms driving knowledge transfer remain underexplored. In this work, we present the first large-scale empirical and statistical analysis of KD across models ranging from 0.5B to 7B parameters on 14 complex reasoning tasks in a zero-shot setting. Our findings reveal that KD can improve the average performance of smaller models by up to $10\%$, with a peak task specific gain of $22\%$, while providing only marginal benefits ($\sim 1.3\%$) for larger models. Surprisingly, teacher performance has a minimal impact on student outcomes, while teacher task expertise impacts KD effectiveness. A correlation study indicates that smaller LMs benefit more from KD, whereas larger LMs show diminished gains. Additionally, we uncover a misalignment between improvements in student performance and reasoning fidelity, suggesting that while KD enhances accuracy, it does not always maintain the structured decision-making processes of the teacher. Our ablation study further highlights the importance of teacher signals and logit smoothing in influencing students' performance after distillation. Overall, our study offers a comprehensive empirical and statistical assessment of KD, highlighting both its benefits and trade-offs when distilling knowledge from larger to smaller LMs.
- **Summary**: This paper presents a large-scale empirical study of Knowledge Distillation (KD) for compressing Large Language Models (LLMs) into smaller ones. The authors analyze KD's effectiveness across models ranging from 0.5B to 7B parameters on 14 reasoning tasks in a zero-shot setting. Their findings indicate that KD significantly improves the performance of smaller models but offers diminishing returns for larger ones. They also explore the impact of teacher performance, KD methods, and reasoning fidelity, revealing insights into the mechanisms driving knowledge transfer. Notably, they find a misalignment between improved student performance and reasoning fidelity, suggesting that KD may not always preserve the teacher's structured decision-making processes.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Large-scale Empirical Analysis:** The paper's primary strength is its comprehensive empirical evaluation. By analyzing KD across a range of model sizes, tasks, and KD methods, the authors provide valuable insights into its behavior and limitations. This breadth sets it apart from many existing studies that focus on task-specific setups or limited model sizes.
*   **Identification of the Generalization vs. Fidelity Paradox:** The paper's discovery of a misalignment between performance improvements and reasoning fidelity is a significant contribution. It challenges the assumption that improved student performance necessarily implies faithful replication of the teacher's reasoning process. This finding raises important questions about the interpretability and reliability of distilled models.
*   **Analysis of Teacher Performance and Task Expertise:** The paper's investigation into the impact of teacher performance and task expertise on KD effectiveness is insightful. The finding that teacher task expertise is more crucial than overall teacher performance highlights the importance of selecting teachers with relevant knowledge.
*   **Statistical Rigor:** The paper employs statistical tests to support its claims. This enhances the credibility of the findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in KD Methods:** The KD methods used are established techniques. While the study is broad in its scope and findings, the analysis does not introduce any new distillation algorithms or strategies, potentially limiting its technical innovation.
*   **Reasoning Fidelity Metric:** The BLEU score used for measuring reasoning fidelity has limitations. It focuses on ngram overlap and may not fully capture the nuances of semantic similarity or logical consistency in reasoning steps. The reliance on a single metric provides an incomplete picture of the transfer of reasoning fidelity, particularly given recent progress in robust alignment metrics.
*   **Zero-shot Setting:** While zero-shot performance is valuable, the paper might have benefited from including few-shot or fine-tuning experiments to better understand how KD affects adaptation to specific datasets, as some of the reasoning datasets are known to significantly benefit from fine-tuning.
*   **Limited Explanation of Mismatch:** The paper highlights the mismatch between performance gains and reasoning fidelity but provides only limited discussion on the underlying causes. While they suggest limitations with student architecture, additional insight in the optimization process and potential sources of bias in distilled models would have added greater depth.

**Significance and Potential Influence:**

The paper's findings have important implications for researchers and practitioners working on model compression and knowledge transfer. The identification of the generalization vs. fidelity paradox underscores the need for developing KD techniques that balance performance improvements with the preservation of reasoning integrity. The insights into teacher performance and task expertise can guide the selection of appropriate teachers for distillation.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its empirical nature, the paper makes a valuable contribution by thoroughly exploring the benefits and trade-offs of KD across different model sizes, tasks, and KD methods. The core contribution concerning the generalization vs. fidelity trade-off carries weight because it points to limitations of existing methods and the necessity for better alignment. While the paper does not introduce novel KD methods or a more robust metric for measuring reasoning, the scale and analysis makes a significant contribution, while pointing directions for follow-up work. Thus, a high score below the level of "exceptional" is warranted.
