### ViaRL: Adaptive Temporal Grounding via Visual Iterated Amplification Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15447v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqiang Xu, Qi Dai, Tian Xie, Yifan Yang, Kai Qiu, DongDong Chen, Zuxuan Wu, Chong Luo
- **Abstract**: Video understanding is inherently intention-driven-humans naturally focus on relevant frames based on their goals. Recent advancements in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled flexible query-driven reasoning; however, video-based frameworks like Video Chain-of-Thought lack direct training signals to effectively identify relevant frames. Current approaches often rely on heuristic methods or pseudo-label supervised annotations, which are both costly and limited in scalability across diverse scenarios. To overcome these challenges, we introduce ViaRL, the first framework to leverage rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) for optimizing frame selection in intention-driven video understanding. An iterated amplification strategy is adopted to perform alternating cyclic training in the video CoT system, where each component undergoes iterative cycles of refinement to improve its capabilities. ViaRL utilizes the answer accuracy of a downstream model as a reward signal to train a frame selector through trial-and-error, eliminating the need for expensive annotations while closely aligning with human-like learning processes. Comprehensive experiments across multiple benchmarks, including VideoMME, LVBench, and MLVU, demonstrate that ViaRL consistently delivers superior temporal grounding performance and robust generalization across diverse video understanding tasks, highlighting its effectiveness and scalability. Notably, ViaRL achieves a nearly 15\% improvement on Needle QA, a subset of MLVU, which is required to search a specific needle within a long video and regarded as one of the most suitable benchmarks for evaluating temporal grounding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ViaRL: Adaptive Temporal Grounding via Visual Iterated Amplification Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViaRL, a novel framework for intention-driven video understanding that leverages rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) to optimize frame selection. Addressing the lack of direct training signals for identifying relevant frames in video Chain-of-Thought (CoT) systems, ViaRL uses an iterated amplification strategy. This involves cyclic training, where a frame selector is trained using the answer accuracy of a downstream multimodal large language model (MLLM) as a reward signal. The selector identifies relevant frames for the MLLM. The core idea is to use reinforcement learning to selectively focus on specific video segments based on the task at hand and downstream QA accuracy, avoiding the need for costly frame selection annotations and mimicking human-like attentional processes. They show the system is effective and scalable on multiple benchmarks including VideoMME, LVBench, and MLVU, showing improvements in temporal grounding performance and generalization ability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of rule-based reinforcement learning specifically to the *temporal grounding* problem within video CoT systems. Previous frame selection approaches often rely on heuristics, pseudo-labels, or direct optimization strategies that aren't as dynamically aligned with task-specific requirements. The use of iterated amplification system, where the frame selector and MLLM refine each other iteratively through a feedback loop, adds another layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multifaceted:
    *   **Improved Temporal Grounding:** The experimental results, especially the near 15% improvement on Needle QA (a subset of MLVU particularly sensitive to temporal grounding), demonstrate ViaRL's ability to locate relevant information within long videos effectively. This is a key challenge in video understanding.
    *   **Human-like Learning:** ViaRL's trial-and-error RL approach aligns with how humans learn to focus their attention on relevant information, enhancing interpretability and potentially improving the generalizability of video understanding systems.
    *   **Reduced Annotation Cost:** By eliminating the need for extensive frame-selection annotations, ViaRL makes video understanding systems more scalable and adaptable to diverse scenarios.
    *   **Generalization across Tasks:** Consistent performance gains across multiple benchmarks indicates that ViaRL isn't just tailored to a specific dataset but offers a robust solution for various video understanding tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing frame selection methods and motivates the need for a more dynamic and task-aware approach.
    *   **Elegant Solution:** The ViaRL framework is well-designed and integrates seamlessly with existing video CoT pipelines. The RL formulation using answer accuracy as a reward is intuitive and effective.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks provide strong evidence for ViaRL's superior performance and robustness. The ablation studies offer valuable insights into the effectiveness of the different components of the framework.
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to understand the concepts and follow the experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity of RL:** Reinforcement learning systems can be difficult to train and tune, requiring careful reward engineering and policy optimization. While the paper demonstrates that ViaRL is effective, the training process and sensitivity to hyperparameter settings aren't fully explored. It is possible that its performance degrades under certain hyperparameter choices.
    *   **Reliance on MLLM Quality:** The effectiveness of ViaRL relies on the downstream MLLM's ability to accurately assess answers and generate informative reasoning processes. While the paper highlights consistent performance, improvements in MLLM architecture could further enhance system performance.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Failure Cases:** A more detailed analysis of failure cases and limitations would provide further insights into the framework's strengths and weaknesses.

*   **Potential Impact:** The framework could influence future research on video understanding, particularly in the areas of temporal grounding, reinforcement learning for video processing, and intention-driven video analysis. It could be applied to improve the accuracy, efficiency, and scalability of a wide range of video-based applications, such as video search, video summarization, and video question answering.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of video understanding by introducing a novel and effective approach for temporal grounding based on reinforcement learning. The empirical evidence demonstrates the superiority of ViaRL over existing methods. While there is room for further exploration of the training process and potential failure cases, the paper offers substantial novelty and has the potential to significantly impact future research and applications in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Comprehensive Evaluation and Analysis for NSFW Concept Erasure in Text-to-Image Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15450v1)
- **Authors**: Die Chen, Zhiwen Li, Cen Chen, Yuexiang Xie, Xiaodan Li, Jinyan Ye, Yingda Chen, Yaliang Li
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image diffusion models have gained widespread application across various domains, demonstrating remarkable creative potential. However, the strong generalization capabilities of diffusion models can inadvertently lead to the generation of not-safe-for-work (NSFW) content, posing significant risks to their safe deployment. While several concept erasure methods have been proposed to mitigate the issue associated with NSFW content, a comprehensive evaluation of their effectiveness across various scenarios remains absent. To bridge this gap, we introduce a full-pipeline toolkit specifically designed for concept erasure and conduct the first systematic study of NSFW concept erasure methods. By examining the interplay between the underlying mechanisms and empirical observations, we provide in-depth insights and practical guidance for the effective application of concept erasure methods in various real-world scenarios, with the aim of advancing the understanding of content safety in diffusion models and establishing a solid foundation for future research and development in this critical area.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical issue of NSFW content generation in text-to-image diffusion models. It identifies the lack of comprehensive evaluation for existing concept erasure methods. To address this gap, the authors present a full-pipeline toolkit specifically designed for evaluating NSFW concept erasure. This toolkit includes fine-grained thematic annotation of NSFW datasets, a taxonomy of concept erasure methods, automated detection tools, and a comprehensive set of evaluation metrics.  The authors conduct extensive experiments using the toolkit to provide in-depth insights and practical guidance for the effective application of concept erasure methods in various scenarios.  The paper offers a publicly released code repository to facilitate further research.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by providing the **first comprehensive benchmark for evaluating NSFW concept erasure in text-to-image diffusion models**.  While existing benchmarks exist for general content safety or object/style erasure, none specifically target the challenges associated with NSFW content.  The paper's novelty lies in:

*   **NSFW-Specific Focus:** Addresses a critical ethical and practical problem directly relevant to the deployment of diffusion models.
*   **Full-Pipeline Toolkit:**  Provides a complete framework encompassing data annotation, method classification, automated detection, and evaluation metrics, enabling systematic analysis.
*   **In-Depth Analysis:**  Goes beyond numerical comparisons to analyze the characteristics and underlying mechanisms of different concept erasure methods. The identification of the weak correlation between prompt toxicity and unsafe image generation is a noteworthy finding.
*   **Practical Insights:** Derives practical guidance for various application scenarios, considering factors like resource constraints and security requirements.
*   **Public Code Release:** Encourages further research and development in this critical area by providing an open platform.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the importance of addressing NSFW content generation in diffusion models.
*   **Comprehensive Toolkit:** The provided toolkit is a valuable resource for the research community.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed and cover a wide range of methods and scenarios.
*   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers useful guidance for practitioners deploying diffusion models in real-world settings.
*   **Publicly Available Code:** The code release will facilitate further research and development.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of NSFW Themes:** The paper acknowledges that the benchmark currently covers a limited set of NSFW themes, deliberately excluding politically or legally sensitive topics due to challenges in reliable detection, annotation, and ethical considerations. This limited scope may not fully capture the diverse range of NSFW content that can be generated.
*   **Dependence on Stable Diffusion v1.4:**  The evaluation is primarily based on Stable Diffusion v1.4. While this is a widely used model, the findings may not fully generalize to newer diffusion models.
*   **Limited Analysis of Dataset Cleaning Method:** The experiments mainly exclude the Dataset Cleaning, as it "needs large, concept-specific datasets for training from scratch." However, the exploration of this method should also be considered, which could improve the comprehensiveness of the benchmark.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

*   **Standardizing Evaluation:** Providing a standardized benchmark for evaluating NSFW concept erasure methods.
*   **Guiding Method Development:**  Offering insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different methods, guiding future research and development.
*   **Promoting Responsible Deployment:** Helping practitioners deploy diffusion models in a safe and responsible manner.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a substantial contribution in an important area. The creation of a comprehensive benchmark, along with the insightful analysis and practical guidance, elevates it beyond a simple empirical study. The limitations, while present, are clearly acknowledged and do not detract significantly from the overall value of the work. Although the exclusion of certain NSFW categories as well as a narrow focus to the older version of the models do detract from its overall impact, it can still be useful as a starting point. Therefore, a score reflecting a significant contribution, but with room for improvement with regards to its scope and generality, is appropriate.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Teaching Language Models to Evolve with Users: Dynamic Profile Modeling for Personalized Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15456v1)
- **Authors**: Weixiang Zhao, Xingyu Sui, Yulin Hu, Jiahe Guo, Haixiao Liu, Biye Li, Yanyan Zhao, Bing Qin, Ting Liu
- **Abstract**: Personalized alignment is essential for enabling large language models (LLMs) to engage effectively in user-centric dialogue. While recent prompt-based and offline optimization methods offer preliminary solutions, they fall short in cold-start scenarios and long-term personalization due to their inherently static and shallow designs. In this work, we introduce the Reinforcement Learning for Personalized Alignment (RLPA) framework, in which an LLM interacts with a simulated user model to iteratively infer and refine user profiles through dialogue. The training process is guided by a dual-level reward structure: the Profile Reward encourages accurate construction of user representations, while the Response Reward incentivizes generation of responses consistent with the inferred profile. We instantiate RLPA by fine-tuning Qwen-2.5-3B-Instruct, resulting in Qwen-RLPA, which achieves state-of-the-art performance in personalized dialogue. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that Qwen-RLPA consistently outperforms prompting and offline fine-tuning baselines, and even surpasses advanced commercial models such as Claude-3.5 and GPT-4o. Further analysis highlights Qwen-RLPA's robustness in reconciling conflicting user preferences, sustaining long-term personalization and delivering more efficient inference compared to recent reasoning-focused LLMs. These results emphasize the potential of dynamic profile inference as a more effective paradigm for building personalized dialogue systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Teaching Language Models to Evolve with Users: Dynamic Profile Modeling for Personalized Alignment":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of personalized alignment in large language models (LLMs) for user-centric dialogue.  It argues that existing approaches (prompt-based and offline optimization) are insufficient for cold-start scenarios and long-term personalization due to their static nature.  The authors introduce a Reinforcement Learning for Personalized Alignment (RLPA) framework where an LLM interacts with a simulated user model to iteratively infer and refine user profiles through dialogue.  The training process uses a dual-level reward structure: Profile Reward for accurate user representation and Response Reward for responses consistent with the inferred profile.  The RLPA framework is instantiated by fine-tuning Qwen-2.5-3B-Instruct, resulting in Qwen-RLPA.  The paper demonstrates through empirical evaluations that Qwen-RLPA outperforms baselines and even surpasses advanced commercial models in personalized dialogue.  Further analysis highlights the framework's robustness, long-term personalization capabilities, and efficient inference.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The RLPA framework itself constitutes a substantial novelty. Framing personalized alignment as a multi-turn Markov Decision Process (MDP) and using reinforcement learning to dynamically infer user profiles addresses a key limitation in the existing literature. Most prior work relies on static user profiles or offline optimization. The dual-level reward system (Profile Reward and Response Reward) is also a novel aspect, explicitly guiding the LLM towards both accurate profile modeling and consistent response generation. The comparison against strong commercial baselines is also a plus.

*   **Significance:** Personalized alignment is a critical challenge for the widespread adoption of LLMs in interactive applications. A framework that enables LLMs to learn and adapt to individual user preferences dynamically is highly significant. Qwen-RLPA's performance in surpassing established techniques and even closed-source models indicates the potential to significantly improve user experience in domains like long-term dialogue, personalized education, and user-centered decision support. The potential impact is high since the personalized alignment can address the limitation of one-size-fits-all approaches.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The RLPA framework provides a principled approach to dynamic personalized alignment.
    *   The dual-reward system offers effective supervision for learning user profiles.
    *   Empirical evaluations are thorough, comparing against both open-source and closed-source baselines, and providing ablation studies.
    *   The analysis offers insights into Qwen-RLPA's robustness, long-term coherence, and efficiency.
    *   The model is relatively small and easy to implement (Qwen 2.5 3B)

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on a simulated user: The framework is trained using a simulated user model. The realism and complexity of the simulated user will directly impact the performance and generalization capabilities of the trained LLM. The paper does evaluate the user model, but the gap between simulated and real user interaction could be significant.
    *   Limited generalizability outside dataset: The method is evaluated on existing datasets that may contain bias. A rigorous testing using real users is missing.
    *   The paper does not explore potential failure modes of the user modeling. What happens when the model infers an incorrect or inappropriate user profile? How does the model handle conflicting user preferences? It does briefly mention that the RLPA can reconcile conflicting user preferences, but doesn't provide any discussion or ablation study on this point.
    *   The paper doesn't provide a strong discussion on the ethical implications of the research.
    *   Hyper-parameters and architecture are not analyzed and they were found by grid search.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by shifting the focus towards dynamic profile inference for personalized alignment.  It provides a valuable framework and empirical evidence that demonstrate the effectiveness of RL for learning personalized dialogue strategies. Future work could build on RLPA by exploring more sophisticated user models, incorporating external knowledge, or addressing the ethical considerations surrounding personalized alignment.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a novel framework, backed by solid empirical results, for addressing a crucial problem in LLMs. The dynamic profile modeling is a clear advance over existing static approaches, and the comprehensive evaluation provides convincing evidence of the framework's effectiveness and efficiency. The reliance on a simulated user model is a key limitation, and the paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of ethical considerations and the limitations of the RL framework. Overall, the contribution is valuable and could inspire significant future research, but isn't transformative.

### Joint Flashback Adaptation for Forgetting-Resistant Instruction Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15467v1)
- **Authors**: Yukun Zhao, Lingyong Yan, Zhenyang Li, Shuaiqiang Wang, Zhumin Chen, Zhaochun Ren, Dawei Yin
- **Abstract**: Large language models have achieved remarkable success in various tasks. However, it is challenging for them to learn new tasks incrementally due to catastrophic forgetting. Existing approaches rely on experience replay, optimization constraints, or task differentiation, which encounter strict limitations in real-world scenarios. To address these issues, we propose Joint Flashback Adaptation. We first introduce flashbacks -- a limited number of prompts from old tasks -- when adapting to new tasks and constrain the deviations of the model outputs compared to the original one. We then interpolate latent tasks between flashbacks and new tasks to enable jointly learning relevant latent tasks, new tasks, and flashbacks, alleviating data sparsity in flashbacks and facilitating knowledge sharing for smooth adaptation. Our method requires only a limited number of flashbacks without access to the replay data and is task-agnostic. We conduct extensive experiments on state-of-the-art large language models across 1000+ instruction-following tasks, arithmetic reasoning tasks, and general reasoning tasks. The results demonstrate the superior performance of our method in improving generalization on new tasks and reducing forgetting in old tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Joint Flashback Adaptation for Forgetting-Resistant Instruction Tuning" introduces a novel continual learning method called Joint Flashback Adaptation (JFA) to mitigate catastrophic forgetting in large language models (LLMs). JFA addresses the limitations of existing approaches (replay, regularization, task differentiation) by using a small number of "flashbacks" (prompts from old tasks) during the adaptation to new tasks. It constrains model output deviations from the original model and interpolates between flashbacks and new tasks via latent task learning for better knowledge sharing and smoother adaptation. The method is task-agnostic and doesn't require access to the original training data of old tasks. Experiments on Vicuna-13B and Llama3.1-8B across a variety of tasks demonstrate JFA's effectiveness in improving generalization on new tasks while reducing forgetting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of using flashbacks is interesting as an alternative to full replay or complex regularization techniques. The joint task learning via latent task interpolation on top of flashback is also novel. By combining the flashback prompts with joint latent tasks, the proposed method is task-agnostic and does not require any extra information about previous training data for mitigating forgetting.

*   **Significance:** Catastrophic forgetting is a major challenge in the practical deployment of LLMs. A method that can alleviate forgetting using a limited number of flashbacks, without requiring data replay, addresses a significant practical need. The experiments show promising results across various tasks and datasets. This could lead to more efficient and effective continual learning of LLMs in real-world scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The method is task-agnostic.
    *   Requires a significantly smaller amount of data compared to replay methods (just a handful of flashbacks).
    *   Joint task learning is innovative.
    *   The results across various benchmarks (SNI, GSM8K, SVAMP, ARC, BBH, MMLU-Pro) provide evidence of effectiveness.
    *   Detailed ablation studies are conducted (effect of number of flashbacks, choice of alpha, hyperparameter tuning for joint task learning).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The selection of flashback prompts might be crucial to the performance of JFA, which needs to be further tested with a wide range of settings. Currently, the paper only mentions randomly sampling the prompts from validation sets without further justification.
    *   While the paper claims the method addresses the stability-plasticity dilemma, a deeper exploration of the trade-off is needed. The performance degradation on SNI with higher numbers of flashbacks suggests there are still trade-offs and not fully addressed by JFA.
    *   The computational overhead compared to basic fine-tuning may limit its scalability to even larger models. A more thorough examination of runtime and memory usage is needed.
    *   Lack of comparison to other PEFT-based continual learning methods beyond PACE.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper can inspire new research directions in continual learning for LLMs by highlighting the potential of lightweight "flashback" approaches.  It could also stimulate further investigation into more effective techniques for latent task interpolation and knowledge sharing.

*   **Rigour and Justification of Score:** The paper has strong novelty and significance, with well-designed experiments and ablation studies to support the claims. It tackles an important problem in a practical way. However, the weaknesses concerning scalability, robustness to different flashback strategies, and further investigation of performance trade-offs prevent it from receiving a higher score.

### A Qualitative Investigation into LLM-Generated Multilingual Code Comments and Automatic Evaluation Metrics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15469v1)
- **Authors**: Jonathan Katzy, Yongcheng Huang, Gopal-Raj Panchu, Maksym Ziemlewski, Paris Loizides, Sander Vermeulen, Arie van Deursen, Maliheh Izadi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models are essential coding assistants, yet their training is predominantly English-centric. In this study, we evaluate the performance of code language models in non-English contexts, identifying challenges in their adoption and integration into multilingual workflows. We conduct an open-coding study to analyze errors in code comments generated by five state-of-the-art code models, CodeGemma, CodeLlama, CodeQwen1.5, GraniteCode, and StarCoder2 across five natural languages: Chinese, Dutch, English, Greek, and Polish. Our study yields a dataset of 12,500 labeled generations, which we publicly release. We then assess the reliability of standard metrics in capturing comment \textit{correctness} across languages and evaluate their trustworthiness as judgment criteria. Through our open-coding investigation, we identified a taxonomy of 26 distinct error categories in model-generated code comments. They highlight variations in language cohesion, informativeness, and syntax adherence across different natural languages. Our analysis shows that, while these models frequently produce partially correct comments, modern neural metrics fail to reliably differentiate meaningful completions from random noise. Notably, the significant score overlap between expert-rated correct and incorrect comments calls into question the effectiveness of these metrics in assessing generated comments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a qualitative investigation into the performance of large language models (LLMs) in generating code comments for non-English code. It examines five state-of-the-art code models (CodeGemma, CodeLlama, CodeQwen1.5, GraniteCode, and StarCoder2) across five natural languages (Chinese, Dutch, English, Greek, and Polish). The authors conduct an open-coding study to identify common errors in the generated comments and assess the reliability of standard evaluation metrics in capturing comment correctness across different languages. Their analysis identifies a taxonomy of 26 distinct error categories, revealing variations in language cohesion, informativeness, and syntax adherence. The paper demonstrates that while models often produce partially correct comments, neural metrics struggle to differentiate meaningful completions from random noise, raising concerns about the effectiveness of these metrics in evaluating generated comments, especially in non-English contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by directly addressing the under-explored area of multilingual code comment generation using LLMs. While some works touch upon multilingual NLP or code generation separately, this work focuses specifically on code comments and performs a qualitative error analysis, which is relatively novel. The taxonomy of errors specific to multilingual code comment generation is a valuable contribution. However, the finding that neural metrics struggle isn't entirely new; previous work has highlighted similar limitations in NLP tasks.

*   **Significance:** The research is important for several reasons:

    *   It highlights the limitations of current code LLMs and evaluation metrics when applied to non-English contexts, which is crucial for broadening the accessibility and usability of these tools globally.
    *   The taxonomy of errors provides valuable insights for future model development, enabling researchers to target specific weaknesses in multilingual code understanding and generation.
    *   The findings have practical implications for software development teams working with multilingual codebases, suggesting a need for caution when relying on automatically generated comments and automated evaluation metrics. The paper pushes for more manual verification in such cases.
    *   The public release of the labeled dataset of 12,500 comments in 5 languages is a valuable resource to the community for testing the results from the paper, and performing future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The open-coding approach, with iterative refinement of the error taxonomy and involvement of native speakers, enhances the validity and reliability of the qualitative analysis. The methodology for data collection seems well-defined, even if restricted by model context length limitations.
    *   **Comprehensive Error Taxonomy:** The detailed error taxonomy is a key strength, providing a structured framework for understanding the specific challenges of multilingual code comment generation.
    *   **Multi-faceted Evaluation:** The paper combines qualitative error analysis with quantitative metric evaluation, offering a well-rounded assessment of model performance.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper identifies practical challenges in using LLMs for code comment generation in non-English contexts and provides concrete suggestions for improving the development and evaluation of multilingual code models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Size:** Restricting the analysis to 7B-8B parameter models limits the generalizability of the findings to larger, potentially more capable models.
    *   **Context Length Limitations:** The short context window constraint may have impacted the quality of generated comments and might not fully reflect the models' capabilities when provided with more contextual information.
    *   **Metric Analysis Scope:** The analysis, while highlighting the issues with certain metrics, could benefit from a more detailed investigation into alternative or adapted metrics specifically designed for multilingual code generation. The comparison with human performance is also somewhat limited.
    *   **Generality:** While the paper focuses on five languages, the specific conclusions might not necessarily apply to all languages equally, particularly those significantly different from the ones studied.

*   **Impact:** This paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in multilingual code generation, emphasizing the need for more sophisticated evaluation methods and prompting investigations into techniques that improve performance in low-resource languages. It also encourages greater awareness of the limitations of current LLMs in diverse coding environments. The insights into the types of errors produced by LLMs will aid in the development of targeted improvements in these models.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **7** is justified. While the individual findings about metric performance aren't groundbreaking, the comprehensive qualitative analysis of multilingual code comment generation, the detailed error taxonomy, and the emphasis on a less-researched area provide a valuable contribution to the field. The practical implications for developers and the call for more rigorous evaluation strategies further enhance the paper's significance. However, the limitations related to model size, context length, and metric analysis prevent a higher score.

### CoLA: Collaborative Low-Rank Adaptation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15471v1)
- **Authors**: Yiyun Zhou, Chang Yao, Jingyuan Chen
- **Abstract**: The scaling law of Large Language Models (LLMs) reveals a power-law relationship, showing diminishing return on performance as model scale increases. While training LLMs from scratch is resource-intensive, fine-tuning a pre-trained model for specific tasks has become a practical alternative. Full fine-tuning (FFT) achieves strong performance; however, it is computationally expensive and inefficient. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods, like LoRA, have been proposed to address these challenges by freezing the pre-trained model and adding lightweight task-specific modules. LoRA, in particular, has proven effective, but its application to multi-task scenarios is limited by interference between tasks. Recent approaches, such as Mixture-of-Experts (MOE) and asymmetric LoRA, have aimed to mitigate these issues but still struggle with sample scarcity and noise interference due to their fixed structure. In response, we propose CoLA, a more flexible LoRA architecture with an efficient initialization scheme, and introduces three collaborative strategies to enhance performance by better utilizing the quantitative relationships between matrices $A$ and $B$. Our experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of CoLA, outperforming existing PEFT methods, especially in low-sample scenarios. Our data and code are fully publicly available at https://github.com/zyy-2001/CoLA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "COLA: Collaborative Low-Rank Adaptation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CoLA (Collaborative Low-Rank Adaptation), a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) approach for large language models (LLMs). CoLA aims to address the limitations of existing PEFT methods like LoRA, particularly in low-data scenarios where task interference and noise can hinder performance. The core idea of CoLA is to create a more flexible LoRA architecture by relaxing the fixed numerical relationships between the low-rank matrices A and B.  It also leverages an efficient initialization scheme based on extending PiSSA to CoLA and introducing three collaborative strategies designed to enhance performance by better utilizing the quantitative relationships between the A and B matrices: (i) Fully collaborative, (ii) Random collaborative, and (iii) Heuristic collaborative CoLA. The paper presents extensive experimental results on Llama models, demonstrating that CoLA outperforms existing PEFT methods, especially in low-sample scenarios and across diverse single-domain and multi-domain tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a somewhat novel architecture that builds upon the existing LoRA and MoE-LoRA methods, mainly the flexibility of #A and #B that the fixed relationships that other LoRA variations present. The proposed collaborative strategies are incremental improvements, and the extended PiSSA initialization scheme is also an adaptation of existing work. Overall, the novelty is moderate.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its improved performance in low-data regimes, a critical area for practical applications of LLMs where data labeling can be expensive and data is scarce. The collaborative strategies also present novel perspectives for collaboration between two matrices A and B. The experiments provide strong evidence for the effectiveness and robustness of CoLA in different scenarios, suggesting that it could be a valuable alternative to existing PEFT methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Improved Performance in Low-Data Scenarios:**  The primary strength of CoLA is its superior performance compared to other PEFT methods when data is limited. This is a practically relevant problem that the paper directly addresses.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents a detailed experimental evaluation on different Llama models and datasets, providing strong evidence for CoLA's effectiveness and robustness. The ablation studies further analyze the impact of the different components of CoLA.
    *   **Addresses Limitations of LoRA:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of LoRA, particularly task interference in low-data settings, and proposes a solution that demonstrates improved performance.
    *   **Thorough Ablation Studies:** The exploration of parameters #A and #B is unique, which provided novel perspectives in PEFT.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the performance gains are evident, the underlying architecture and collaborative strategies are incremental improvements over existing LoRA-based methods.
    *   **Empirical Justification of Collaboration Strategies:** The collaborative strategies seem primarily driven by intuition with a heavy emphasis on the experimental results. More theoretical insights into why these collaborations are effective would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited Code-Domain Evaluation:** The lack of evaluation in the code domain is a limitation, especially considering the current focus on code generation capabilities of LLMs.
    *   **Writing Clarity:** In some sections, the writing could be more concise and clear.

*   **Potential Influence:** CoLA has the potential to influence the field by providing a more robust and efficient PEFT method for low-data scenarios.  The design principles behind CoLA's flexibility in matrix relationships could also inspire further research into more adaptive and task-aware PEFT architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. Here's the rationale:

*   The paper makes a valuable contribution by improving the performance of PEFT in low-data settings, a practically relevant problem.
*   The experimental evaluation is thorough and provides strong evidence for CoLA's effectiveness.
*   However, the proposed architecture and collaborative strategies are incremental improvements upon existing methods, which limits the novelty of the work.
*   The lack of evaluation in the code domain and more theoretical insights also lowers the score.

### PhysicsArena: The First Multimodal Physics Reasoning Benchmark Exploring Variable, Process, and Solution Dimensions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15472v1)
- **Authors**: Song Dai, Yibo Yan, Jiamin Su, Dongfang Zihao, Yubo Gao, Yonghua Hei, Jungang Li, Junyan Zhang, Sicheng Tao, Zhuoran Gao, Xuming Hu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in diverse reasoning tasks, yet their application to complex physics reasoning remains underexplored. Physics reasoning presents unique challenges, requiring grounding in physical conditions and the interpretation of multimodal information. Current physics benchmarks are limited, often focusing on text-only inputs or solely on problem-solving, thereby overlooking the critical intermediate steps of variable identification and process formulation. To address these limitations, we introduce PhysicsArena, the first multimodal physics reasoning benchmark designed to holistically evaluate MLLMs across three critical dimensions: variable identification, physical process formulation, and solution derivation. PhysicsArena aims to provide a comprehensive platform for assessing and advancing the multimodal physics reasoning abilities of MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PHYSICSARENA: The First Multimodal Physics Reasoning Benchmark Exploring Variable, Process, and Solution Dimensions":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PHYSICSARENA, a new multimodal physics reasoning benchmark designed to evaluate the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Unlike existing physics benchmarks, PHYSICSARENA holistically assesses MLLMs across three key dimensions: variable identification, physical process formulation, and solution derivation. The dataset consists of over 5,000 high-quality instances derived from high school physics problems, incorporating both visual and textual information. The authors also present an evaluation framework using GPT-4o as an automatic judge, analyze the performance of several state-of-the-art MLLMs on the benchmark, and identify key limitations and areas for improvement in current models. The paper contributes a comprehensive resource and evaluation platform for advancing MLLMs in physics reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty by addressing a critical gap in physics reasoning benchmarks. Current benchmarks often focus solely on text-based inputs or end-to-end problem-solving, neglecting the intermediate reasoning steps crucial for physics understanding. PHYSICSARENA's multi-dimensional approach – variable identification, process formulation, and solution derivation – is a valuable contribution. This approach directly tackles the challenge of assessing not just whether an MLLM can arrive at the correct answer but also whether it understands the underlying physics.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to drive research and development of MLLMs with improved physics reasoning capabilities. By providing a structured and challenging benchmark, PHYSICSARENA enables researchers to quantitatively assess and compare different models' performance across key reasoning dimensions. The thorough analysis of MLLM performance reveals critical limitations in current models, such as insufficient visual understanding and difficulties in capturing fine-grained process details, which are key insights for future research directions. The rigorous data collection, expert annotation, and the multi-stage preparation process contribute to the overall significance of the paper.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive and Multi-Dimensional Assessment:** PHYSICSARENA's focus on variable identification, process formulation, and solution derivation offers a more complete evaluation of physics reasoning compared to existing benchmarks.
*   **High-Quality Dataset:** The paper details a rigorous data preparation process, including initial data collection, preprocessing, AI-assisted expert annotation, and sampling review, ensuring the dataset's quality and reliability.
*   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of MLLM performance on the benchmark, identifying key limitations and potential areas for improvement.
*   **Use of GPT-4o as a judge**: While also mentioned as a limitation below, the authors use GPT-4o in an interesting way to gauge an evaluation metric for the models.
*  **Clear Task definition**: The overall task is very easy to understand with clear evaluation metrics, which increases its value.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Automated Judge (GPT-4o):** While scalable, using GPT-4o as the primary judge introduces potential biases and limitations compared to human expert evaluations, particularly in nuanced reasoning chains. This might undermine the evaluation fidelity, especially considering the complexity of the task.
*   **Limited Scope of Visual Inputs:**  The visual inputs are largely static diagrams and images. More realistic physics reasoning often requires understanding dynamic scenarios from videos or interactive simulations. This limits the ecological validity of the benchmark.
*   **High School Physics Focus:**  The benchmark primarily covers high school physics topics, which may not adequately challenge MLLMs in more advanced or specialized physics domains.
*   **Lack of comparison across difficulty levels**: Although the benchmark has various difficult levels, they are not effectively compared in a multi-faceted format.
*   **The evaluation could use more human metrics**: The evaluation has a single automated metric but fails to assess human-centered evaluations.

**Potential Influence:**

PHYSICSARENA has the potential to significantly influence the development of MLLMs for physics reasoning. It provides a valuable resource for researchers to benchmark their models, identify areas for improvement, and ultimately advance the field. The benchmark's multi-dimensional assessment approach could also be adopted in other scientific reasoning domains.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel and significant contribution to the field by introducing a comprehensive and multi-dimensional physics reasoning benchmark. PHYSICSARENA addresses a crucial gap in existing benchmarks and provides valuable insights into the limitations of current MLLMs. The meticulous data preparation and thorough analysis further enhance the paper's value.

However, the reliance on an automated judge (GPT-4o) and the limited scope of visual inputs introduce limitations that could be addressed in future work. The focus on high school physics is also a constraint, although it allows for a large and well-defined dataset. The lack of effective comparison metrics can be seen as a major drawback. Taking the strengths and limitations into account, a score of 7.5 is appropriate, reflecting a significant contribution with room for further improvement and refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LFTF: Locating First and Then Fine-Tuning for Mitigating Gender Bias in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15475v1)
- **Authors**: Zhanyue Qin, Yue Ding, Deyuan Liu, Qingbin Liu, Junxian Cai, Xi Chen, Zhiying Tu, Dianhui Chu, Cuiyun Gao, Dianbo Sui
- **Abstract**: Nowadays, Large Language Models (LLMs) have attracted widespread attention due to their powerful performance. However, due to the unavoidable exposure to socially biased data during training, LLMs tend to exhibit social biases, particularly gender bias. To better explore and quantifying the degree of gender bias in LLMs, we propose a pair of datasets named GenBiasEval and GenHintEval, respectively. The GenBiasEval is responsible for evaluating the degree of gender bias in LLMs, accompanied by an evaluation metric named AFGB-Score (Absolutely Fair Gender Bias Score). Meanwhile, the GenHintEval is used to assess whether LLMs can provide responses consistent with prompts that contain gender hints, along with the accompanying evaluation metric UB-Score (UnBias Score). Besides, in order to mitigate gender bias in LLMs more effectively, we present the LFTF (Locating First and Then Fine-Tuning) algorithm.The algorithm first ranks specific LLM blocks by their relevance to gender bias in descending order using a metric called BMI (Block Mitigating Importance Score). Based on this ranking, the block most strongly associated with gender bias is then fine-tuned using a carefully designed loss function. Numerous experiments have shown that our proposed LFTF algorithm can significantly mitigate gender bias in LLMs while maintaining their general capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new approach, called LFTF (Locating First and Then Fine-Tuning), to mitigate gender bias in Large Language Models (LLMs).  It also proposes two new datasets, GenBiasEval and GenHintEval, for evaluating gender bias.  GenBiasEval is designed to quantify the degree of gender bias using a novel metric called AFGB-Score, while GenHintEval assesses LLMs' ability to produce responses consistent with gender hints in prompts, using the UB-Score metric.  The LFTF algorithm identifies the LLM blocks most associated with gender bias (using a Block Mitigating Importance Score - BMI) and then fine-tunes only those blocks using a specially designed loss function.  Experimental results show that LFTF reduces gender bias while maintaining general capabilities of the LLM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Datasets & Metrics:** The creation of GenBiasEval and GenHintEval with corresponding metrics (AFGB-Score and UB-Score) is a valuable contribution. These datasets provide a means to evaluate gender bias specifically tailored for LLMs, going beyond existing datasets by incorporating "gender hints." This aspect represents some novelty. AFGB-Score addresses a specific nuance related to how "male" and "female" should be equal when debiasing gender related terms. UB-Score focuses on how consistently LLMs follow gender hints.
    *   **LFTF Algorithm:**  The idea of selectively fine-tuning only specific blocks within an LLM based on their contribution to gender bias (identified by BMI) is somewhat novel. The approach builds on the modularity concept but the specific application to gender bias mitigation with the BMI ranking is a new approach.  The modified loss function to encourage gender neutrality at the identified bias-related blocks is also a contribution, though conceptually straightforward.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Addressing a Critical Problem:**  Gender bias in LLMs is a widely recognized and important problem. The paper's focus on mitigation is timely and relevant.
    *   **Effectiveness:** The experimental results suggest that LFTF can effectively reduce gender bias. However, a careful comparison of the results with existing state-of-the-art debiasing techniques (not just a few ablations) would strengthen the paper.  The paper notes that performance on Math Reasoning tasks decreases with the LFTF algorithm, so there is a tradeoff that needs to be further evaluated.
    *   **Generalizability:** The experiments on multiple LLMs (Qwen2.5-7B, Meta-Llama3-8B, Vicuna-7B-v1.5) suggest some generalizability of the LFTF algorithm, adding to its significance.
    *   **Limitations and Future Work:** The paper acknowledges that the datasets are based on a binary gender concept and the methodology is only tested in English.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Algorithm Complexity:** While the concept is fairly novel, the LFTF algorithm has limitations in that it is based on a rather ad-hoc definition (BMI). More theoretically-motivated approach to identifying problematic blocks might be preferred.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** The limited comparison to basic baselines (FPFT, prompt-based, and DAMA) weakens the claims of effectiveness. A comparison with more established and recent debiasing methods would significantly strengthen the paper. The "disastrous" results of simply applying model editing is a little too dismissive, and needs further evaluation. The evaluation of "anti-bias" is also superficial.
    *   **Domain Specificity:** The LFTF algorithm is specifically designed to mitigate gender bias and is shown to decrease math performance in one tested LLM. It is unclear whether the proposed method might degrade performance on other tasks or biases if applied directly.
    *   **Presentation:** The paper would benefit from clearer writing and more detailed explanations of certain aspects (e.g., the rationale behind the specific loss function used, the interpretation of the BMI).

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The datasets could become valuable benchmarks for future research on gender bias mitigation in LLMs.
    *   The selective fine-tuning approach may inspire other researchers to explore similar techniques for addressing bias in LLMs or for efficiently adapting LLMs for specific tasks.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper presents some valuable contributions, primarily the new datasets and the idea of selectively fine-tuning LLM blocks to mitigate gender bias. It tackles a critical problem and demonstrates some effectiveness. However, the somewhat ad-hoc approach to identifying and finetuning blocks limits the innovation. The limited comparison to existing debiasing methods is a major weakness that prevents a higher score. Furthermore, the generalizability of the algorithm needs further exploration, especially since it shows declining performance on math reasoning problems. While this paper represents a worthwhile contribution, it requires more rigorous evaluation and theoretical underpinnings to truly stand out. The limitations and unaddressed concerns about performance trade-offs also influence the score.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### KaFT: Knowledge-aware Fine-tuning for Boosting LLMs' Domain-specific Question-Answering Performance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15480v1)
- **Authors**: Qihuang Zhong, Liang Ding, Xiantao Cai, Juhua Liu, Bo Du, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a common approach to improve the domain-specific question-answering (QA) performance of large language models (LLMs). However, recent literature reveals that due to the conflicts between LLMs' internal knowledge and the context knowledge of training data, vanilla SFT using the full QA training set is usually suboptimal. In this paper, we first design a query diversification strategy for robust conflict detection and then conduct a series of experiments to analyze the impact of knowledge conflict. We find that 1) training samples with varied conflicts contribute differently, where SFT on the data with large conflicts leads to catastrophic performance drops; 2) compared to directly filtering out the conflict data, appropriately applying the conflict data would be more beneficial. Motivated by this, we propose a simple-yet-effective Knowledge-aware Fine-tuning (namely KaFT) approach to effectively boost LLMs' performance. The core of KaFT is to adapt the training weight by assigning different rewards for different training samples according to conflict level. Extensive experiments show that KaFT brings consistent and significant improvements across four LLMs. More analyses prove that KaFT effectively improves the model generalization and alleviates the hallucination.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "KaFT: Knowledge-aware Fine-tuning for Boosting LLMs' Domain-specific Question-Answering Performance" addresses the problem of knowledge conflict that arises during supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of Large Language Models (LLMs) for domain-specific question-answering (QA). It argues that vanilla SFT can be suboptimal because the LLMs' internal knowledge might conflict with the knowledge present in the fine-tuning data. The paper proposes a Knowledge-aware Fine-Tuning (KaFT) approach. KaFT first uses a query diversification strategy to detect knowledge conflicts. Based on detected conflicts, the method assigns different rewards (weights) to training samples during SFT. Samples with high conflicts receive lower rewards, effectively reducing their impact on the fine-tuning process. The authors perform experiments on medical QA and out-of-domain datasets using several LLMs, demonstrating that KaFT improves performance, generalization, and alleviates hallucination compared to vanilla SFT and other baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in the combination of conflict detection with sample-adaptive rewards for SFT. While the individual components aren't entirely new (conflict detection has been explored, and adaptive learning is a general technique), the way they're integrated to address the knowledge conflict problem in domain-specific LLM fine-tuning is reasonably novel. The query diversification for improving conflict detection is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is significant. Knowledge conflicts are a real issue that can hinder the effectiveness of SFT and introduce unwanted behaviors (e.g., hallucination) in LLMs. A method like KaFT that can mitigate these issues can improve the reliability and accuracy of LLMs in specialized domains. The reported improvements on medical QA and in reducing hallucination are promising. The fact that the method improves out-of-domain performance also suggests a positive impact on generalization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed experiments using multiple LLMs and datasets.
    *   Comprehensive analysis of results, including ablation studies and parameter sensitivity analysis.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in performance, generalization, and hallucination reduction.
    *   The method is simple and relatively easy to implement.
    *   Query diversification shows effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvements, while consistent, are not necessarily dramatic in every case. More significant gains would strengthen the case for the method's importance.
    *   The experiments are primarily focused on medical QA. While this is a relevant domain, expanding the evaluation to other domains (e.g., legal, technical) would increase the method's generalizability.
    *   The hyperparameter tuning for the rewards *a* and *β* is relatively simple. Exploring more sophisticated reward structures or using automated hyperparameter optimization techniques could potentially lead to further improvements.
    *   The conflict detection, while improved with query diversification, is still based on ICL which can be sensitive to the choice of few-shot examples. More robust conflict detection methods would be beneficial.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research and practice in LLM fine-tuning. It provides a practical and effective approach to address the knowledge conflict problem. If the results can be replicated and extended to other domains, KaFT could become a standard technique for domain-specific LLM adaptation. Further, the analysis of how training data with varying conflicts affect LLM performance offers valuable insights.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by proposing and evaluating KaFT to address the knowledge conflict problem. It provides a reasonable level of novelty with the combined approach of query diversification and sample-adaptive rewards in SFT. The results are promising and have the potential to influence the field. However, the improvements aren't always dramatic, and the evaluation could be broadened to solidify its generality. The method is relatively simple, so the barrier to adoption is low. Although other areas can be looked into, the improvements provided and analysis shown justify the proposed framework. The significance of the improvements, the scope of the evaluation, and the sophistication of the method are taken into account.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Protoknowledge Shapes Behaviour of LLMs in Downstream Tasks: Memorization and Generalization with Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15501v1)
- **Authors**: Federico Ranaldi, Andrea Zugarini, Leonardo Ranaldi, Fabio Massimo Zanzotto
- **Abstract**: We introduce the concept of protoknowledge to formalize and measure how sequences of tokens encoding Knowledge Graphs are internalized during pretraining and utilized at inference time by Large Language Models (LLMs). Indeed, LLMs have demonstrated the ability to memorize vast amounts of token sequences during pretraining, and a central open question is how they leverage this memorization as reusable knowledge through generalization. We then categorize protoknowledge into lexical, hierarchical, and topological forms, varying on the type of knowledge that needs to be activated. We measure protoknowledge through Knowledge Activation Tasks (KATs), analyzing its general properties such as semantic bias. We then investigate the impact of protoknowledge on Text-to-SPARQL performance by varying prompting strategies depending on input conditions. To this end, we adopt a novel analysis framework that assesses whether model predictions align with the successful activation of the relevant protoknowledge for each query. This methodology provides a practical tool to explore Semantic-Level Data Contamination and serves as an effective strategy for Closed-Pretraining models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Protoknowledge Shapes Behaviour of LLMs in Downstream Tasks: Memorization and Generalization with Knowledge Graphs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "protoknowledge" to understand how Large Language Models (LLMs) internalize and use Knowledge Graph (KG) information during pretraining for downstream tasks.  The authors categorize protoknowledge into lexical, hierarchical, and topological forms and measure it using Knowledge Activation Tasks (KATs). They analyze the semantic bias of protoknowledge and its impact on Text-to-SPARQL performance by varying prompting strategies. The paper proposes a framework to assess the alignment between model predictions and the activation of relevant protoknowledge, offering a method to explore semantic-level data contamination, especially within Closed-Pretraining models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a useful framework for analyzing how LLMs leverage KG data encountered during pretraining, and the concept of "protoknowledge" provides a new lens for understanding knowledge transfer and generalization in LLMs. The categorisation into lexical, hierarchical, and topological further decomposes the analysis.
*   **Significance:** By focusing on how LLMs *use* memorized KG information for tasks like Text-to-SPARQL, the paper tackles a crucial question that moves beyond simple memorization benchmarks. Identifying and measuring semantic bias as a limiting factor in generalization is also significant. The proposed framework for analyzing Text-to-SPARQL performance by linking it to protoknowledge activation has practical implications for understanding and potentially mitigating data contamination. However, the reliance on closed-pretraining models and the limited scope of knowledge graphs tested limits the broader applicability.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear definition of protoknowledge and its different forms.
    *   Introduction of Knowledge Activation Tasks (KATs) to measure the acquisition and use of KG information.
    *   Framework for analyzing Text-to-SPARQL performance based on protoknowledge activation.
    *   Investigation of semantic bias and its impact on generalization.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited selection of LLMs used for the experiments (GPT-4, GPT-3.5, and two variants of Llama-3) which limits the generalizability of results.
    *   The claim of closed pretraining models is problematic as the knowledge acquired is compared against well-known datasets.
    *   The reliance on DBpedia and Wikidata alone limits the scope of the analysis, as the models could have been pre-trained on these datasets.
    *   The evaluation of "topological protoknowledge" through the proposed SPS score could be more rigorous, as it is somewhat speculative and relies on triples, which are only validated through a triple validity check (not a full query).
    *   Overconfidence is discussed, but not rigorously quantiied.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on:

    *   Developing more effective methods for pretraining LLMs on structured data.
    *   Designing evaluation benchmarks that are less susceptible to data contamination.
    *   Understanding the limitations of LLMs in generalizing to out-of-distribution domains.
    *   Analyzing and mitigating semantic bias in LLMs.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel concept and a framework with promising practical applications for understanding KG-enhanced LLMs. It tackles a significant challenge in the field: moving beyond memorization to understand how LLMs leverage structured knowledge for downstream tasks. However, the limitations regarding the scope of evaluation (limited LLMs, limited KGs), and the speculative nature of certain aspects of the experimental analysis (especially SPS and "overconfidence") reduce its overall impact.  The results are interesting and suggest a fruitful direction for future research, but the experimental evidence doesn't fully support all the claims made. Also, the closed pretraining argument has fundamental flaws.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Directional Non-Commutative Monoidal Structures for Compositional Embeddings in Machine Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15507v1)
- **Authors**: Mahesh Godavarti
- **Abstract**: We introduce a new algebraic structure for multi-dimensional compositional embeddings, built on directional non-commutative monoidal operators. The core contribution of this work is this novel framework, which exhibits appealing theoretical properties (associativity along each dimension and an interchange law ensuring global consistency) while remaining compatible with modern machine learning architectures. Our construction defines a distinct composition operator circ_i for each axis i, ensuring associative combination along each axis without imposing global commutativity. Importantly, all axis-specific operators commute with one another, enforcing a global interchange law that enables consistent crossaxis compositions. This is, to our knowledge, the first approach that provides a common foundation that generalizes classical sequence-modeling paradigms (e.g., structured state-space models (SSMs) and transformer self-attention) to a unified multi-dimensional framework. For example, specific one-dimensional instances of our framework can recover the familiar affine transformation algebra, vanilla self-attention, and the SSM-style recurrence. The higher-dimensional generalizations naturally support recursive, structure-aware operations in embedding spaces. We outline several potential applications unlocked by this structure-including structured positional encodings in Transformers, directional image embeddings, and symbolic modeling of sequences or grids-indicating that it could inform future deep learning model designs. We formally establish the algebraic properties of our framework and discuss efficient implementations. Finally, as our focus is theoretical, we include no experiments here and defer empirical validation to future work, which we plan to undertake.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel algebraic structure designed for multi-dimensional compositional embeddings in machine learning. It proposes directional non-commutative monoidal operators and outlines theoretical properties like associativity along each dimension and a global interchange law that maintains consistency across axes. The construction defines a distinct composition operator for each axis, avoiding global commutativity while maintaining compatibility with machine learning architectures. The authors claim this approach unifies classic sequence modeling paradigms like SSMs and Transformer self-attention under a multi-dimensional framework. The paper presents potential applications in structured positional encodings, directional image embeddings, and symbolic modeling and details formal algebraic properties, efficient implementations, and future research directions, while deferring empirical validation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific algebraic structure defined by the directional non-commutative monoidal operators with the interchange law. While multi-monoidal structures and non-commutative algebras have been explored, the combination of directional operators *with* a specific global interchange law to enforce consistency in multi-dimensional composition *specifically in the context of machine learning embeddings* appears to be a new contribution. Prior work often focuses on specific types of data or algorithmic improvements without this specific formalization. However, the individual components (monoidal structures, non-commutativity, tensor products) are individually established, suggesting a *synthesis* of existing concepts rather than invention of a totally new mathematical object. The extent to which the proposed algebraic structure truly *generalizes* SSMs and transformers is largely theoretical at this stage.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in providing a principled foundation for building multi-dimensional models, which is crucial for complex data formats. The framework offers a way to reason about structure-aware operations within embedding spaces. The proposed applications – structured positional encodings, image embeddings, symbolic modeling – touch upon important areas within machine learning. *However*, the paper lacks empirical validation, making it difficult to assess its practical impact. It remains to be seen whether this structure can lead to tangible improvements over existing techniques. The absence of experimental results significantly diminishes the immediate significance. It's essentially a framework waiting for applications to prove its worth. The emphasis on theory also means that someone will need to translate this well-defined framework into useful and practical code.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a clearly defined algebraic structure with rigorous theoretical properties.
    *   It connects to established concepts from abstract algebra and category theory.
    *   It outlines potential applications across several machine learning domains.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains complex concepts.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The *complete* lack of experimental validation is a major weakness.
    *   The claim of generalizing SSMs and transformers requires empirical support.
    *   The potential computational overhead of the approach needs further investigation. GL(n) may not be practical for high-dimensional data.
    *   It's not clear how this framework *substantially* improves upon existing architectural choices, except to provide a theoretical foundation.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the framework proves to be practically useful, it could influence the design of future multi-dimensional models, providing a unifying lens for seemingly disparate approaches and enabling the creation of new types of architectures. If the proposed algebraic framework translates well, it could unlock new ways to approach multi-dimensional composition and reasoning in machine learning models.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novel *combination* of algebraic elements to define the structure, the potential (though as yet unrealized) significance, but also the major weakness of lacking any empirical validation, a score of **6** is warranted. The paper contributes a formal, well-defined framework with promising applications, but its true impact is contingent on demonstrating its practical benefits in future work. It is a rigorous theoretical contribution that needs experimental validation before its impact can be assessed. If the framework proves to be useful and improves performance, then a higher score could be reassessed at a later date.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Visual Thoughts: A Unified Perspective of Understanding Multimodal Chain-of-Thought
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15510v1)
- **Authors**: Zihui Cheng, Qiguang Chen, Xiao Xu, Jiaqi Wang, Weiyun Wang, Hao Fei, Yidong Wang, Alex Jinpeng Wang, Zhi Chen, Wanxiang Che, Libo Qin
- **Abstract**: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have achieved significant success in multimodal tasks, with multimodal chain-of-thought (MCoT) further enhancing performance and interpretability. Recent MCoT methods fall into two categories: (i) Textual-MCoT (T-MCoT), which takes multimodal input and produces textual output; and (ii) Interleaved-MCoT (I-MCoT), which generates interleaved image-text outputs. Despite advances in both approaches, the mechanisms driving these improvements are not fully understood. To fill this gap, we first reveal that MCoT boosts LVLMs by incorporating visual thoughts, which convey image information to the reasoning process regardless of the MCoT format, depending only on clarity and conciseness of expression. Furthermore, to explore visual thoughts systematically, we define four distinct forms of visual thought expressions and analyze them comprehensively. Our findings demonstrate that these forms differ in clarity and conciseness, yielding varying levels of MCoT improvement. Additionally, we explore the internal nature of visual thoughts, finding that visual thoughts serve as intermediaries between the input image and reasoning to deeper transformer layers, enabling more advanced visual information transmission. We hope that the visual thoughts can inspire further breakthroughs for future MCoT research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Visual Thoughts: A Unified Perspective of Understanding Multimodal Chain-of-Thought":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the mechanisms behind the success of Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (MCoT) reasoning in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). It proposes a unified perspective: MCoT works by incorporating "visual thoughts," which are intermediate, logic-driven cross-modal representations that distill image information for the reasoning process.  The paper categorizes visual thoughts into four expressions: Natural Language (N-LANG), Structured Language (S-LANG), Edited Image (E-IMG), and Generative Image (G-IMG).  The study finds that (1) visual thoughts are crucial for MCoT's effectiveness; (2) different expressions of visual thoughts suit different scenarios based on clarity and efficiency; and (3) visual thoughts act as intermediaries, conveying visual information to deeper transformer layers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its unified perspective on MCoT through the concept of "visual thoughts." While prior work has focused on specific MCoT methods (textual vs. interleaved), this paper attempts to provide a higher-level understanding applicable across different paradigms. Categorizing and analyzing different expressions of visual thoughts (N-LANG, S-LANG, E-IMG, G-IMG) is a further point of novelty. The connection to deeper transformer layers is also a new insight.
    *   **Strengths:** The paper introduces a useful abstraction ("visual thoughts") for understanding the inner workings of MCoT, instead of treating various method types as mutually exclusive.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The categorization of visual thoughts, while helpful, might not be entirely exhaustive, and some expressions can arguably overlap. The analysis relies heavily on empirical evaluation using existing datasets and models. A more theoretical framework to support the observations would strengthen the paper. The experiments primarily focus on specific tasks (CoMT, IsoBench). While these are representative, it remains to be seen if the insights generalize across all possible multi-modal tasks.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to guide future MCoT research. By identifying "visual thoughts" as a core component, it suggests that future work should focus on optimizing how these thoughts are generated, expressed, and integrated into the reasoning process. The finding about visual thoughts enabling deeper visual information transmission is also significant, suggesting that current LVLMs might be limited by how they process visual information early in the network.
    *   **Strengths:** The paper provides clear, actionable insights. Researchers can use these findings to inform the design of new MCoT methods or to improve existing ones. It highlights that the clarity and conciseness of visual thoughts are crucial factors to consider.
    *   **Weaknesses:**  The paper's analysis is largely descriptive. It identifies correlations and patterns but doesn't fully explain *why* visual thoughts act as effective intermediaries. The theoretical underpinnings could be more robust. It would be useful to include some analyses with a more thorough mathematical framework that further formalizes visual thoughts.

*   **Rigor:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments to validate its claims.  The ablation studies (removing visual thoughts) and comparisons between different expressions provide evidence for the importance of visual thoughts. The internal attention analysis offers insights into how visual information flows within the model.
    *   **Strengths:** The experiments are well-designed and address the core research questions. The use of multiple LVLMs strengthens the generality of the findings.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The human evaluation for conciseness and clarity might introduce some subjectivity. More details regarding the annotation process (number of annotators, agreement scores) would enhance the credibility of those results. All the data is from previous benchmark data sets, but does not offer any experiments on new benchmarks that specifically assess the role of visual thoughts.

*   **Potential Influence:** If validated by further research, the visual thought perspective could influence the direction of MCoT research. Specifically:  (1) It could shift the focus from simply creating new architectures to understanding how to best represent and integrate visual information within these architectures. (2) It could encourage the development of new evaluation metrics that specifically assess the clarity, conciseness, and relevance of visual thoughts.

**Overall:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by proposing a unified perspective on MCoT. The concept of "visual thoughts" is a useful abstraction for understanding how multi-modal information is processed during reasoning. While the analysis is primarily empirical and lacks a strong theoretical foundation, the insights are actionable and could guide future research in this area. It is clearly written and well-organized, and offers a good contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**
