- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Keep Security! Benchmarking Security Policy Preservation in Large Language Model Contexts Against Indirect Attacks in Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15805v1)
- **Authors**: Hwan Chang, Yumin Kim, Yonghyun Jun, Hwanhee Lee
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in sensitive domains such as enterprise and government, ensuring that they adhere to user-defined security policies within context is critical-especially with respect to information non-disclosure. While prior LLM studies have focused on general safety and socially sensitive data, large-scale benchmarks for contextual security preservation against attacks remain lacking. To address this, we introduce a novel large-scale benchmark dataset, CoPriva, evaluating LLM adherence to contextual non-disclosure policies in question answering. Derived from realistic contexts, our dataset includes explicit policies and queries designed as direct and challenging indirect attacks seeking prohibited information. We evaluate 10 LLMs on our benchmark and reveal a significant vulnerability: many models violate user-defined policies and leak sensitive information. This failure is particularly severe against indirect attacks, highlighting a critical gap in current LLM safety alignment for sensitive applications. Our analysis reveals that while models can often identify the correct answer to a query, they struggle to incorporate policy constraints during generation. In contrast, they exhibit a partial ability to revise outputs when explicitly prompted. Our findings underscore the urgent need for more robust methods to guarantee contextual security.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces CoPriva, a new large-scale benchmark dataset for evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to adhere to user-defined security policies within a contextual setting, particularly focusing on non-disclosure. The dataset consists of 4,184 instances derived from realistic meeting transcripts, explicit security policies, and direct/indirect attack queries designed to elicit prohibited information. The authors evaluate 10 LLMs on CoPriva, revealing a significant vulnerability: many models leak sensitive information by violating user-defined policies, especially in the face of indirect attacks. The paper also explores factors influencing leakage, such as context length and policy presence, and investigates whether model revision can improve policy compliance.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper tackles an important and increasingly relevant problem: ensuring that LLMs deployed in sensitive domains respect dynamically defined security policies within their context. This is a step beyond prior work primarily focusing on general safety or toxicity concerns and pre-defined privacy settings.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Dataset:** The CoPriva dataset is a significant contribution. The scale (4k instances) and the incorporation of realistic contexts, explicit policies, and both direct and *indirect* attack queries fill a crucial gap in existing benchmarks. The inclusion of indirect attacks, which attempt to subtly bypass restrictions, is particularly noteworthy.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper systematically evaluates 10 LLMs, including a mix of open-source and proprietary models, reasoning and non-reasoning models, providing a broad perspective on current LLM vulnerabilities.
*   **Analysis of Influencing Factors:** The analysis of factors influencing leakage (context length, policy presence, domain) offers valuable insights into the limitations of current LLM alignment techniques. The finding that *simply including* a policy doesn't guarantee compliance and can even *increase* leakage is surprising and important.
*   **Revision Exploration:** Investigating whether models can revise outputs to comply with policies is a useful exploration of potential mitigation strategies, even if the results show only limited improvement.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Dataset Creation:** The heavy reliance on GPT-4 for generating policies and performing evaluation raises concerns about potential biases or limitations inherited from the model itself. Human validation, while present, could be more extensive. This is especially pertinent as the models are being evaluated *against* the benchmark created by a powerful LLM. How much the results are just echoing the limitations of GPT-4 needs further scrutiny.
*   **Limited Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper identifies the problem and analyses contributing factors well, it only briefly explores one mitigation strategy (revision). More research is needed to develop effective methods to guarantee contextual security.
*   **Lack of Comparison with Existing Datasets:** While the paper states prior work has limitations, deeper dive comparing the dataset against CONFAIDE (Mireshghallah et al., 2024) would be beneficial. Specifically, elaborating in-depth how it improve and complement such dataset.
*   **Specificity of Context:** The dataset is focused on meeting transcripts. While realistic, this specific context limits the generalizability to other sensitive domains.
*   **Limited exploration of reasons for failing revision strategies:** While it is mentioned that only some of the models succeeded after prompting, further exploration of why the LLMs fail the prompting strategy would increase the value of the study.

**Significance:**

The paper's findings highlight a significant vulnerability in current LLMs, especially concerning indirect attacks, indicating that they are not adequately aligned for sensitive applications requiring strict contextual security. This finding is likely to spur further research into more robust alignment techniques. The CoPriva dataset will likely become a valuable resource for researchers working in this area.

**Overall:** The paper provides a valuable contribution by identifying and quantifying a critical gap in current LLM safety alignment and offers a useful benchmark for future research.

Score: 7

**Rationale:** The paper addresses a highly relevant problem and introduces a valuable dataset, but the heavy reliance on GPT-4 in dataset construction, limited scope of mitigation strategies, and limited domain generalizability prevent it from achieving a higher score. Future research using the CoPriva dataset and exploring more robust defense mechanisms is likely to build on this foundation and significantly improve the security of LLMs in sensitive domains. The current score reflects the important and novel contribution with an acknowledgement of the areas for improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Atlas of In-Context Learning: How Attention Heads Shape In-Context Retrieval Augmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15807v1)
- **Authors**: Patrick Kahardipraja, Reduan Achtibat, Thomas Wiegand, Wojciech Samek, Sebastian Lapuschkin
- **Abstract**: Large language models are able to exploit in-context learning to access external knowledge beyond their training data through retrieval-augmentation. While promising, its inner workings remain unclear. In this work, we shed light on the mechanism of in-context retrieval augmentation for question answering by viewing a prompt as a composition of informational components. We propose an attribution-based method to identify specialized attention heads, revealing in-context heads that comprehend instructions and retrieve relevant contextual information, and parametric heads that store entities' relational knowledge. To better understand their roles, we extract function vectors and modify their attention weights to show how they can influence the answer generation process. Finally, we leverage the gained insights to trace the sources of knowledge used during inference, paving the way towards more safe and transparent language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper delves into the inner workings of in-context learning (ICL) with retrieval augmentation in large language models (LLMs). It treats a prompt as a composition of informational components (instructions, context, relational knowledge) and proposes an attribution-based method (AttnLRP) to identify specialized attention heads. The study reveals that certain attention heads specialize in comprehending instructions and retrieving relevant contextual information (in-context heads), while others store entities' relational knowledge (parametric heads). By manipulating these heads through function vectors and attention weights, the paper demonstrates how they influence the answer generation process. Finally, the authors explore tracing the sources of knowledge used during inference, aiming towards safer and more transparent LLMs with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach by combining attribution methods with a component-wise view of prompts to understand ICL in RAG. The idea of dissecting attention heads into specialized "in-context" and "parametric" roles is insightful. The experimental setup, particularly the controlled biography dataset, adds to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a critical gap in understanding how LLMs leverage external knowledge during ICL.  Identifying specific attention heads responsible for instruction comprehension and knowledge retrieval is highly relevant for improving RAG systems. The ability to trace knowledge sources has broad implications for safety, transparency, and controlling factual accuracy. However, the presented approach has some limitations, and its significance depends on the extent to which the insights can be generalized and practically applied.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The methodology is clearly explained and grounded in existing attribution techniques.
    *   The introduction of a biography dataset tailored for this analysis is a valuable contribution.
    *   Demonstrations of manipulating attention heads via function vectors and attention weights to influence generation are compelling.
    *   The preliminary results on source tracing offer a promising avenue for future research.
    *   The work explores models beyond the standard LLaMA.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are primarily focused on question answering within a controlled setting. The generalizability of the findings to more complex and diverse tasks and datasets is uncertain.
    *   While AttnLRP is used, the unfaithfulness of attribution methods is a limitation. More explicit handling of "attention sinks" and other known issues with attention mechanisms as explanations could strengthen the results.
    *   The connection between the identified heads and specific architectural features is not thoroughly explored. Understanding *why* certain heads specialize in certain ways would add significant value.
    *   The preliminary nature of the source tracing makes it difficult to assess its real-world applicability. The presented results could be considered proof-of-concept.
    *   The definition of "relevance" (Eq 1.) could be strengthened and further examined; using only positive relevance scores is a strong restriction.
    *   The evaluation criteria could be improved; reliance on recall can be too verbose depending on the LLM.
    *   The paper sometimes oversells findings (e.g., Figure 1 suggesting "well-structured" attention heads when these conclusions should be tempered due to limitations above.)

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in ICL, RAG, and LLM interpretability. The identification of specialized attention heads can guide the development of more efficient and controllable RAG systems. The source tracing methodology, if refined, could become a valuable tool for ensuring factual accuracy and mitigating hallucinations.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a strong methodology and clear insights into ICL for retrieval-augmented generation. The approach combines attribution techniques with a component-wise view of prompts, leading to novel findings regarding the roles of different attention heads. Although the study is primarily focused on question answering within a controlled setting, it opens up exciting possibilities for improving RAG systems, enhancing transparency, and ensuring factual accuracy. The work provides a valuable contribution to the field, but it has limitations in terms of generalizability and depth of architectural understanding. The promising preliminary results on source tracing may require refinement before it achieves real-world applicability.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### MMaDA: Multimodal Large Diffusion Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15809v1)
- **Authors**: Ling Yang, Ye Tian, Bowen Li, Xinchen Zhang, Ke Shen, Yunhai Tong, Mengdi Wang
- **Abstract**: We introduce MMaDA, a novel class of multimodal diffusion foundation models designed to achieve superior performance across diverse domains such as textual reasoning, multimodal understanding, and text-to-image generation. The approach is distinguished by three key innovations: (i) MMaDA adopts a unified diffusion architecture with a shared probabilistic formulation and a modality-agnostic design, eliminating the need for modality-specific components. This architecture ensures seamless integration and processing across different data types. (ii) We implement a mixed long chain-of-thought (CoT) fine-tuning strategy that curates a unified CoT format across modalities. By aligning reasoning processes between textual and visual domains, this strategy facilitates cold-start training for the final reinforcement learning (RL) stage, thereby enhancing the model's ability to handle complex tasks from the outset. (iii) We propose UniGRPO, a unified policy-gradient-based RL algorithm specifically tailored for diffusion foundation models. Utilizing diversified reward modeling, UniGRPO unifies post-training across both reasoning and generation tasks, ensuring consistent performance improvements. Experimental results demonstrate that MMaDA-8B exhibits strong generalization capabilities as a unified multimodal foundation model. It surpasses powerful models like LLaMA-3-7B and Qwen2-7B in textual reasoning, outperforms Show-o and SEED-X in multimodal understanding, and excels over SDXL and Janus in text-to-image generation. These achievements highlight MMaDA's effectiveness in bridging the gap between pretraining and post-training within unified diffusion architectures, providing a comprehensive framework for future research and development. We open-source our code and trained models at: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/MMaDA
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MMADA, a novel framework for multimodal diffusion language models. MMADA aims to improve performance in tasks like textual reasoning, multimodal understanding, and text-to-image generation. Key innovations include: (1) a unified diffusion architecture with a modality-agnostic design, (2) a mixed long chain-of-thought (CoT) fine-tuning strategy to align reasoning processes across modalities, and (3) UniGRPO, a unified policy-gradient-based reinforcement learning algorithm tailored for diffusion foundation models. The paper demonstrates that MMADA-8B achieves strong generalization capabilities, outperforming models like LLaMA-3-7B, Qwen2-7B, Show-o, SDXL, and Janus across various tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components that contribute to the overall architecture. The unified diffusion architecture, although building on existing work, is significant as it eliminates modality-specific components.  The mixed long-CoT post-training and UniGRPO, are specifically designed for diffusion based architectures and addresses an existing gap in training methodologies for this setting. However the long COT is largely inspired by the existing CoT methods, and the UniGRPO can be seen as an modification of the GRPO methods in autoregressive setting.
*   **Significance:** MMADA's ability to achieve strong performance across diverse tasks using a unified framework is a significant contribution. This demonstrates the potential of diffusion models as general-purpose language models and highlights the importance of bridging the pretraining/post-training gap. The performance gains relative to existing large language models and vision-language models are noteworthy.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Architecture:** Simplifies model design and facilitates knowledge transfer between modalities.
    *   **Mixed Long-CoT Fine-Tuning:** Enables cold-start training for reinforcement learning and improves the model's ability to handle complex tasks.
    *   **UniGRPO:** Tailors RL specifically for diffusion models, ensuring consistent performance improvements.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Demonstrates significant performance gains compared to powerful baseline models across various tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** Training diffusion models is often more computationally expensive than autoregressive models, and this is not explicitly addressed in the paper, although performance improvements in section 5.4 does suggest improvements.
    *   **Model Size Limitation:** The paper evaluates MMADA-8B (8 billion parameters), which while significant is not the most competitive setting, there remains questions of the applicability of their proposed model to larger scale models, and a theoretical upper bound analysis is missing.
    *   **Incremental Innovation:**  While the components are novel, they build upon existing techniques (diffusion models, CoT, GRPO). The extent to which each individual contribution drives the performance is not thoroughly analysed in a component-wise manner.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal language models by demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion models as a unified framework and proposing novel post-training strategies. The strong empirical results provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of the MMADA architecture. However, the incremental nature of the innovations, the computational cost considerations, and the limited details on certain design choices and analyses mean that it isn't a game-changing breakthrough.

- **Score**: 7/10

### GUI-G1: Understanding R1-Zero-Like Training for Visual Grounding in GUI Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15810v1)
- **Authors**: Yuqi Zhou, Sunhao Dai, Shuai Wang, Kaiwen Zhou, Qinqlin Jia, Junxu
- **Abstract**: Recent Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents replicate the R1-Zero paradigm, coupling online Reinforcement Learning (RL) with explicit chain-of-thought reasoning prior to object grounding and thereby achieving substantial performance gains. In this paper, we first conduct extensive analysis experiments of three key components of that training pipeline: input design, output evaluation, and policy update-each revealing distinct challenges arising from blindly applying general-purpose RL without adapting to GUI grounding tasks. Input design: Current templates encourage the model to generate chain-of-thought reasoning, but longer chains unexpectedly lead to worse grounding performance. Output evaluation: Reward functions based on hit signals or box area allow models to exploit box size, leading to reward hacking and poor localization quality. Policy update: Online RL tends to overfit easy examples due to biases in length and sample difficulty, leading to under-optimization on harder cases. To address these issues, we propose three targeted solutions. First, we adopt a Fast Thinking Template that encourages direct answer generation, reducing excessive reasoning during training. Second, we incorporate a box size constraint into the reward function to mitigate reward hacking. Third, we revise the RL objective by adjusting length normalization and adding a difficulty-aware scaling factor, enabling better optimization on hard samples. Our GUI-G1-3B, trained on 17K public samples with Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, achieves 90.3% accuracy on ScreenSpot and 37.1% on ScreenSpot-Pro. This surpasses all prior models of similar size and even outperforms the larger UI-TARS-7B, establishing a new state-of-the-art in GUI agent grounding. The project repository is available at https://github.com/Yuqi-Zhou/GUI-G1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "GUI-G1: Understanding R1-Zero-Like Training for Visual Grounding in GUI Agents" investigates the challenges of applying R1-Zero-like reinforcement learning to visual grounding in GUI agents.  It identifies and analyzes three core components of the training pipeline: input design (template), output evaluation (reward function), and policy update (RL objective). The authors find that longer reasoning chains, commonly used in such setups, can actually hurt grounding performance, that standard reward functions lead to size-related reward hacking, and that the GRPO objective suffers from length and difficulty biases. To address these, the paper proposes a "Fast Thinking Template" (direct answer generation), a box-size constrained reward function, and a modified GRPO objective with difficulty weighting. The resulting model, GUI-G1-3B, achieves state-of-the-art performance on ScreenSpot and ScreenSpot-Pro benchmarks using a relatively small training dataset and fewer output tokens.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper offers a thorough and well-structured analysis of the R1-Zero-like training pipeline, pinpointing specific issues related to grounding in GUI agents. The decomposition into input design, output evaluation, and policy update is insightful.
    *   **Targeted Solutions:**  The proposed solutions directly address the identified problems. The "Fast Thinking Template," box-size constraint, and modified GRPO are clearly motivated and well-implemented.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The GUI-G1-3B model demonstrates significant performance improvements on standard benchmarks, surpassing prior state-of-the-art methods, even those using larger models and datasets. The ablation studies in the paper effectively justify their individual contribution to the overall performance gains.
    *   **Efficiency:** The model achieves these improvements using less data, fewer output tokens and no intermediate reasoning steps.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper presents a solid engineering effort with notable performance gains, the individual components (Fast Thinking Template, box-size constraint, weighted GRPO) are largely adaptations or refinements of existing techniques. The novelty primarily lies in the application and combination of these elements within the specific context of GUI grounding.
    *   **Limited Scope of Benchmarks:** The focus on ScreenSpot and ScreenSpot-Pro, while valuable, represents a relatively narrow scope of GUI environments. Generalization to other, more diverse GUI datasets (e.g., AndroidControl dataset) remains to be demonstrated.
    *   **Reliance on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct:** The method is tied to a specific base model (Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct) and it would be more impactful if the improvements generalized to other vision language models.
    *   **Limited discussion on data generation diversity:** The training data has a relatively limited scope, the paper does not provide much discussion or insight into the performance when trained using more diverse data or on data distributions far from the original three.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Improved Understanding of R1-Zero for Grounding:** The paper contributes valuable insights into the nuances of applying R1-Zero-like training to visual grounding, highlighting the need for task-specific adaptations.
    *   **Practical Performance Improvements:** The GUI-G1-3B model represents a significant step forward in GUI grounding performance and efficiency, enabling better-performing agents with reduced training requirements.
    *   **Direction for Future Research:** The identified limitations (scope of benchmarks, limited training data) point to promising avenues for future investigation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of GUI agents, specifically in visual grounding. The comprehensive analysis and targeted solutions demonstrate a strong understanding of the problem and result in significant performance improvements. However, the limited novelty of the individual components and the reliance on a specific base model temper the overall impact. The paper focuses on improvements within an established paradigm (R1-Zero) rather than introducing fundamentally new concepts. Despite this, the thoroughness of the analysis and the practical significance of the results justify a solid score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging the Powerful Attention of a Pre-trained Diffusion Model for Exemplar-based Image Colorization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15812v1)
- **Authors**: Satoshi Kosugi
- **Abstract**: Exemplar-based image colorization aims to colorize a grayscale image using a reference color image, ensuring that reference colors are applied to corresponding input regions based on their semantic similarity. To achieve accurate semantic matching between regions, we leverage the self-attention module of a pre-trained diffusion model, which is trained on a large dataset and exhibits powerful attention capabilities. To harness this power, we propose a novel, fine-tuning-free approach based on a pre-trained diffusion model, making two key contributions. First, we introduce dual attention-guided color transfer. We utilize the self-attention module to compute an attention map between the input and reference images, effectively capturing semantic correspondences. The color features from the reference image is then transferred to the semantically matching regions of the input image, guided by this attention map, and finally, the grayscale features are replaced with the corresponding color features. Notably, we utilize dual attention to calculate attention maps separately for the grayscale and color images, achieving more precise semantic alignment. Second, we propose classifier-free colorization guidance, which enhances the transferred colors by combining color-transferred and non-color-transferred outputs. This process improves the quality of colorization. Our experimental results demonstrate that our method outperforms existing techniques in terms of image quality and fidelity to the reference. Specifically, we use 335 input-reference pairs from previous research, achieving an FID of 95.27 (image quality) and an SI-FID of 5.51 (fidelity to the reference). Our source code is available at https://github.com/satoshi-kosugi/powerful-attention.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to exemplar-based image colorization that leverages the self-attention mechanism of a pre-trained diffusion model (specifically, Stable Diffusion). The method is "fine-tuning-free," meaning it doesn't require retraining the diffusion model.  The key contributions are: 1) a dual attention-guided color transfer module that uses both gray-to-gray and colorized-to-color attention to achieve more precise semantic alignment between the input grayscale image and the reference color image and 2) classifier-free colorization guidance, which enhances the transferred colors by combining color-transferred and non-color-transferred outputs. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach through experiments on existing datasets and a newly created dataset of historical paintings and contemporary photos, showing improved image quality and fidelity to the reference image compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its effective adaptation of pre-trained diffusion model's self-attention for exemplar-based colorization *without* fine-tuning.  While previous work explored attention mechanisms in colorization, they typically involved training attention modules from scratch, limiting their potential due to smaller datasets.  The use of "dual attention" (gray-to-gray and colorized-to-color) is another innovative aspect that addresses the challenge of directly comparing grayscale and color images. The classifier-free guidance, adapted from image generation, provides a unique way to enhance the color transfer quality in this specific application. However, other recent works have explored attention within the diffusion model framework for editing tasks, which somewhat diminishes the distinctiveness. The incremental, albeit clever, extension of existing diffusion principles to a specific colorization problem has a notable lack of generalization.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work is twofold. First, it demonstrates the potential of leveraging the rich semantic understanding learned by large pre-trained models (like Stable Diffusion) for downstream tasks without the need for expensive fine-tuning. This "parameter-efficient transfer learning" is a crucial research direction. Second, the improved performance in exemplar-based colorization, particularly in challenging scenarios like historical paintings, opens up new possibilities for image restoration and enhancement applications. A significant limitation is the reliance on a high-powered computation which constrains accessibility.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents comprehensive quantitative and qualitative results on multiple datasets, showing improvements over existing methods.  The ablation studies clearly demonstrate the contribution of each component. The inclusion of a new cross-domain dataset is also a positive aspect.
    * **Clear and well-written:** The paper is easy to understand, and the method is clearly explained.
    * **Fine-tuning-free approach:** avoids a significant computational cost.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited generalization:** The method is specifically tailored to exemplar-based colorization and doesn't immediately generalize to other image editing tasks. While the attention-based transfer approach is sound, the classifier-free aspect seems somewhat *ad hoc*.
    * **Runtime limitations:** Although the work is fine-tuning-free, it suffers from a relatively long runtime, restricting its practical applications.  While acknowledged by the authors, the need for distilling the model implies the framework needs to be improved.
    * **Dependency on Semantic Correspondence:** Similar to other examplar-based colorization, it requires semantic correspondence, meaning the input and reference images need similar content.
    * **Limited theoretical novelty:** The paper builds upon existing diffusion model frameworks and adapts known techniques like attention and classifier-free guidance. There's no fundamentally new theoretical insight.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can inspire further research in leveraging pre-trained models for image colorization and other image editing tasks. The dual attention mechanism could be useful in other scenarios where aligning features from different modalities is important. However, the practical impact will be limited until the runtime is significantly improved and computation made more accessible.
Score: 6

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid and technically sound contribution to exemplar-based image colorization. The effective adaptation of pre-trained diffusion model attention and the introduction of the dual-attention is somewhat novel, although building on existing techniques from attention mechanisms and diffusion models. The experimental results clearly demonstrate improved performance, which makes the work scientifically sound. It's an incremental improvement, lacking the "leap" required for very high scores, and suffering from a runtime bottleneck. The limitations in terms of generalization somewhat restrict the overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Learning to Reason via Mixture-of-Thought for Logical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15817v1)
- **Authors**: Tong Zheng, Lichang Chen, Simeng Han, R. Thomas McCoy, Heng Huang
- **Abstract**: Human beings naturally utilize multiple reasoning modalities to learn and solve logical problems, i.e., different representational formats such as natural language, code, and symbolic logic. In contrast, most existing LLM-based approaches operate with a single reasoning modality during training, typically natural language. Although some methods explored modality selection or augmentation at inference time, the training process remains modality-blind, limiting synergy among modalities. To fill in this gap, we propose Mixture-of-Thought (MoT), a framework that enables LLMs to reason across three complementary modalities: natural language, code, and a newly introduced symbolic modality, truth-table, which systematically enumerates logical cases and partially mitigates key failure modes in natural language reasoning. MoT adopts a two-phase design: (1) self-evolving MoT training, which jointly learns from filtered, self-generated rationales across modalities; and (2) MoT inference, which fully leverages the synergy of three modalities to produce better predictions. Experiments on logical reasoning benchmarks including FOLIO and ProofWriter demonstrate that our MoT framework consistently and significantly outperforms strong LLM baselines with single-modality chain-of-thought approaches, achieving up to +11.7pp average accuracy gain. Further analyses show that our MoT framework benefits both training and inference stages; that it is particularly effective on harder logical reasoning problems; and that different modalities contribute complementary strengths, with truth-table reasoning helping to overcome key bottlenecks in natural language inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Mixture-of-Thought (MoT), a novel framework that enables LLMs to reason across three complementary modalities: natural language, code, and truth-table reasoning. MoT uses a two-phase approach: (1) self-evolving MoT training, where the model jointly learns from filtered, self-generated rationales across modalities, and (2) MoT inference, which leverages the synergy of the three modalities for better predictions. Experiments on logical reasoning benchmarks (FOLIO and ProofWriter) demonstrate that MoT consistently outperforms strong LLM baselines with single-modality chain-of-thought approaches. The paper also analyzes the benefits of training and inference stages, the effectiveness on harder problems, and the complementary strengths of the modalities, especially truth-table reasoning for overcoming natural language inference bottlenecks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining different reasoning modalities during *training* in a self-supervised manner is a significant step beyond previous work that focused on modality selection or augmentation *only during inference*. Introducing truth tables as a structured symbolic reasoning modality is also a good contribution as it provides a structured way to enumerate cases and mitigate failure modes in NL reasoning.

*   **Significance:** The reported improvements in accuracy on logical reasoning benchmarks, particularly the gains on FOLIO and ProofWriter are noteworthy. Matching the performance of GPT-4 + Logic-LM on FOLIO with a much smaller model (9B parameters) suggests a practical advantage in terms of efficiency. The detailed error analysis, especially the insights into the limitations of natural language reasoning and the benefits of truth-table reasoning, provides valuable directions for future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing LLM approaches in logical reasoning, particularly their reliance on a single modality and the lack of synergistic training across modalities.

    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** MoT is a well-defined framework with a clear training and inference procedure.

    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on standard benchmarks with careful ablation studies. The results show that the proposed method outperforms the single modality and other LLM baselines, demonstrates the effectiveness of MoT training.

    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The error analysis, study of modality combinations, and investigation into the impact of problem difficulty provide a thorough understanding of MoT's strengths and weaknesses.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Task Scope:** The evaluation is primarily focused on logical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are important, it would be valuable to see how MoT generalizes to other types of reasoning, such as mathematical or commonsense reasoning.

    *   **Truth Table Limitations**: While the authors address potential scaling issues with the truth table method, it remains an issue and it has not been proven to scale up to other tasks. In general, the approach remains as a pipeline LLM -> Truth Table (reasoning) -> LLM.

    *   **Dependency on Few-Shot Examples:** The experiments relies on few-shot exemplars to enhance the structural following ability. It can hinder the applications on various tasks.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper's focus on cross-modal reasoning and self-supervised training has the potential to influence future research on LLMs, especially in areas where structured reasoning is important.  It could lead to more efficient and robust LLM systems that can leverage multiple reasoning modalities. The method is relatively straightforward and easy to implement in practice.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper presents a solid contribution with promising results and detailed analysis, the limited task scope and the reliance on a few shot exemplars slightly temper its overall impact.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10
